VARIATION IN REDUCTION STRATEGIES
       OF DUTCH WORD-INITIAL
         CONSONANT CLUSTERS




                           by




                    Wenckje Jongstra




  A thesis submitted in conformity with the requirements
          for the degree of Doctor of Philosophy
           Graduate Department of Linguistics
                  University of Toronto


         © Copyright by Wenckje Jongstra 2003
                                                                                                         ii


ABSTRACT
The main goal of this thesis is to investigate between-individual and within-individual variation
in consonant cluster reduction strategies (C1C2 is realised as C1 or C2) among young children.
The empirical base of the study is a Dutch database with over 9,000 instances of C1 and C2
realisations of 23 word-initial consonant clusters from 45 children aged between two and three
years old. A major contribution of the thesis concerns the methodology of the study, in which
both between- and within-child variation as well as between- and within-cluster variation are
tested in a systematic way.
        One of the findings is that within-child variation is very limited, whereas between-child
variation occurs. Thus, although an individual child is consistent in his/her reduction strategy,
there is a difference between children in the way clusters are typically reduced. Some clusters are
always reduced in one particular way (e.g. /sp/, /sk/, /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/, /fr/,
/xr/, /tw/, /sw/). In other clusters, either target segment can be realised (e.g. /st/, /sx/, /sm/, /sn/,
/sl/, /kn/, /fl/, /xl/). It was furthermore found that there are implicational relationships between
some clusters, i.e., realising C2 in cluster x implies realising C2 in cluster y, but not vice versa.
        Neither the difference between stable and variable clusters, nor the implicational
relationships between some clusters, can be explained by the feature [sonorant] alone. Instead,
the variation in reduction strategies is the result of a difference in prosodification, which is
caused by the fact that some children tend to avoid onsets with similar structure in terms of
sonority distance and place of articulation.
        The perception of clusters was tested in a pilot study. The results suggest that it is not
always correct to assume that a child has clusters underlyingly. In future research, a stronger
focus must be put on the status of the child’s prosodic and segmental representation. This can be
done only if studies target both production and perception.

Chapter 1 Introduction


1.1    Scope of the thesis
Children rarely produce target consonant clusters at early stages (for a detailed and up to date
overview on normal acquisition of consonant clusters, see McLeod, Van Doorn and Reed
2001a). In general, the child will initially reduce the cluster, realising only one consonant in
various ways (C1C2 is realised as C1, C2 or Cother). The process of cluster reduction is
frequently described in the literature on child language (e.g., Barlow 1997, Beers 1995, Fikkert
1994, Grunwell 1982, Ingram 1989, Locke 1983, Ohala 1999, Olmsted 1971, Templin 1957). In
this thesis, I focus on variation within reduction strategies of word-initial clusters by two-year-
olds. I will discuss the amount and the patterns of variation: i.e., how variable reduction
strategies of children are and which patterns in variation occur.
       The issue of variation in child phonology has been interpreted differently over the past
decades (for a recent overview see Rice and Avery 1995). The earlier literature on child
phonology focuses on universal patterns (Jakobson 1968/41; Stampe 1969), correlating patterns
found in child language with common patterns in adult languages. The more recent literature
emphasises that there is individual variation (e.g., Macken and Ferguson 1983, McLeod, Van
Doorn and Reed 2001b, Stoel-Gammon and Cooper 1984, Vihman and Greenlee 1987). Some
linguists however have argued that despite variation, there are consistent patterns in child
language cross-linguistically (Dinnsen 1995, Goad and Ingram 1987). The issue of variation is
relevant with respect to the more important question of whether or not the child’s realisations are
system based.


Regarding variation in consonant cluster reduction (CC is realised as C), language acquisition
research reveals that there is a huge amount of variation in children's production of consonant
clusters. Variation in the realisation of one cluster by the same child, even on the same day, is
also observed (De Houwer and Gillis 1998, p.12):


"There may well be quite a bit of inter-individual and intra-individual variation. Children may
have particular preferences in their reduction patterns and one child may offer instances of
                                                                                                 2


various patterns of reduction (...) The issue of variability needs further investigation, though. So
far, the necessary quantitative data are not available."


The claims in the literature can be divided into two approaches. First, there is variation in terms
of speakers; different speakers are observed to have different reduction strategies. In addition, a
single speaker may have different reduction strategies at a given point in time. Second, variation
is subject to phonological criteria: while some clusters are invariant in terms of which consonant
is omitted, others show greater variation.
         Although variation is a well-observed phenomenon in child language, variation in
consonant cluster reduction has never been subjected to close investigation until now. Previous
work on reduction strategies of Dutch word-initial consonant clusters (e.g. Beers 1995, Broecke,
Ruijters and Van der Meulen 1984, Fikkert 1994) focuses either on outputs of a few children or
on a few outputs for each child, or discusses reduction strategies in terms of sonority classes
rather than individual clusters. This thesis focuses on both between-individual as well as within-
individual variation in reduction strategies of 23 word-initial consonant clusters in Dutch.


1.2       Empirical and theoretical questions
The empirical base of the study consists of in total 27,712 realisations of word-initial CC clusters
by 45 children, 34 from the main study and 11 from a pilot study that was done earlier (see
Jongstra 2000, 2001a). Focusing on reduction data alone, 9,430 observations show reduction in
which a child realises either the first or the second target consonant. The thesis addresses the
following empirical and theoretical questions:


Empirical questions:
•     What are the patterns in between-individual variation and within-individual variation in
      reduction strategies in clusters, in which C1C2 is realised as C1 or C2?
•     Assuming variation for some clusters, are clusters within a sonority group related:
      - in that they are all variable (or non-variable)?
      - in that they undergo the same type of reduction strategy by the same child?
•     What are the patterns in variation when other definitions of cluster reduction are used?
                                                                                                    3


Theoretical questions:
•     To what extent does sonority by itself explain reduction strategies?
•     If sonority is inadequate, how can the variation be accounted for?


In addition to the study of variation patterns in the production of word-initial consonant clusters,
a pilot study was carried out on variation in the perception of word-initial consonant clusters.
The important question of whether child language is system-based or not relates directly to the
nature of underlying phonological representations in a child’s language. Most linguists assume
that the child’s input is akin to the adult output, at least at the segmental level (e.g. Fikkert 1994,
Gnanadesikan 1995, Goad and Rose to appear, Ohala 1996, Smith 1973, Stampe 1969). Correct
perceptual judgements are often the main piece of evidence that a child has adult representations.
However, contrary to the production of consonant clusters, there has not been much work done
on the perception of consonant clusters. An important question is whether it is correct to assume
perfect perception from the moment children start to speak, or whether perception develops
gradually, akin to production.
         In chapter 6 of the thesis, I will compare the results in production with the results in
perception for 19 children that participated in the main study. The main goal of this study is to
investigate whether perception is perfect or not in the language of a two-year-old child who has
just begun to speak. The main questions in the pilot study on perception are defined as follows.


•     How well do individual children perform in a cluster discrimination task
      i) when they have non-adult outputs?
      ii) when they have adult outputs?
•     How well are target clusters discriminated from target singletons?


The methodology and results of the pilot study on perception are given in chapter 6.


1.3      The Dutch consonant system
In section 1.3.1, I will discuss the consonantal and featural system of consonants in Dutch. In
section 1.3.2 I discuss frequent and infrequent word-initial consonant clusters in Dutch. I will
base the notion of frequency on written Dutch and child directed Dutch.
                                                                                                               4




1.3.1      Consonants and features of consonants in Dutch
In Table 1.1, the Dutch consonant system is given (adopted from Booij 1995).


Table 1.1            Dutch consonant inventory (taken from Booij 1995)
                     Bilabial           Lab-dental         Alveolar          Palatal Velar      Glottal
Plosives             S E                                  W G                        N


Fricatives                              I Y               V ]                        [        K


Nasals               P                                     Q                           ×

Liquids                                                    O U

Glides                                  Ù                                    M




The phoneme /Ù/ is realised as bilabial glide [Z] in coda position and as [Ù] in other
environments. Following common practice, I use the symbol “w” as a cover symbol for this
consonant. The phoneme /U/ can be realised in many different ways, as De Houwer and Gillis

(1998) note: as an alveolar trill [U], an alveolar tap [Å], a uvular trill [5], a velar fricative [x], or a

velar approximant [É] depending on the dialect and on the position within the syllable.1

           Dutch does not have a voice distinction in velar stops. The voiced velar stop [Ý] only

occurs in borrowings ([ÝRO] "goal") or as an allophone of /N/ before a voiced stop ([]nÝGXN]

/]nN + GXN/ "handkerchief"). The palato-alveolar fricatives [] and [] only occur in borrowings

([RNRODG?] "chocolate" and >Q?] "embarrassment").
           The voicing of initial fricatives is a matter of debate. Linguists have noticed that many
speakers neutralise the voice contrast in this position (Trommelen 1984, van der Hulst 1984,
Booij 1995). The velar fricative [[] in particular is neutralised. Therefore, some linguists assume

no underlying voice distinction for these segments (van der Hulst 1984, Grijzenhout 1998). Not
only initial velar fricatives are debated. For example, Trommelen (1984) argues that [z] is
underlyingly /s/ before the labio-velar glide (/sw/aard sword). Because of the questionable status
of initial fricative clusters, I do not distinguish between voiced and voiceless outputs of these
clusters. Thus, in the study, there are only five target clusters with fricatives (/fl/, /fr/, /xl/, /xr/,

1
    In the north, where the subjects of the main study come from, /r/ is usually realized as alveolar trill.
                                                                                                                      5


/sw/). Both possible realisations [v] or [f] for /fl/ will be counted as C1 realisation (see chapter
3).

1.3.2         Inventory and frequency of word-initial clusters in Dutch
In order to get an idea of which clusters occur frequently and which clusters occur less frequently
in adult Dutch, a token-frequency search involving word-initial clusters was done in the Celex
database (Baayen et al. 1995). The total number of words with word-initial clusters in this
database is 2,786,248. Out of this number, the frequency results for each word-initial cluster are
given in Appendix 1.1, ordered in two columns from high to low frequency. In the Celex
database no distinction is made between voiced and voiceless velar fricatives (/l/ and /r/),
presumably because the authors assume that the voice contrast for these types of fricatives is lost.
Several linguists working on Dutch notice that the voicing in initial fricatives is neutralised (see

The question is whether the difference between frequent and infrequent clusters, as given in
Table 1.3, is in fact the same in child directed speech. Recall that Table 1.3 is based on data in
Celex, which is a database of written Dutch. The Celex results are compared with the cluster
token frequencies of child directed speech to eight children, taken from The Groningen Corpus
(compiled by Bol, Krikhaar and Wijnen) in Childes, and from The Maarten Corpus in Childes
(compiled by Gillis; see Schaerlakens and Gillis 1987). In the Groningen database, longitudinal
data from seven Dutch learning children aged between 1;05 and 3;07 are collected together with
adult utterances. In the Maarten database, longitudinal data from one child (Maarten) is collected
between the ages of 0;11 and 1;11, together with adult utterances. The total number of words
with word-initial CC clusters in child directed speech in the eight data files is 6,587. The cluster
frequency in child directed speech in the Childes files is given in Appendix 1.2, per individual
child and across children. In Table 1.4, percentages per cluster in Celex (written Dutch) and
Childes (spoken child directed Dutch) are compared.

There is a strong correlation between the frequency of target clusters in Celex and the frequency
of target clusters in the Childes database (R(23) = .815, p<.0001). In other words, from these
data we see that frequent clusters in written Dutch are also frequent clusters in spoken Dutch. I
will thus consider the frequent clusters in Table 1.3 as target clusters in this thesis. In chapter 3, I
will discuss in more detail the target words that were used in the study.


1.4     Outline of the thesis
The outline of the thesis is as follows. In chapter 2 I discuss the literature on variation in
reduction strategies in child language. I give an overview of different predictions made for /s/-
sonorant clusters, since such clusters are reported to be variable. I discuss the difference between
theories that account for this variation by referring to features and theories that refer to prosodic
structure. Moreover, I compare the predictions in realisation patterns that these two theories
make. In chapter 3, the methodology of the main study of the thesis is discussed. In chapter 4, I
present the results on between- and within-variation in reduction strategies, focusing on a strict
definition of cluster reduction, namely in which one of the target consonants is realised (C1 or
C2). I will present the data in a step-by-step fashion, considering various parts of the database. I
compare patterns in reduction strategies between clusters in one child, and look for implicational
relationships in the reduction pattern between clusters. In chapter 5, I discuss the results of
                                                                                                8


chapter 4 in the light of theoretical implications. I account for the difference between between-
child stable and between-child variable clusters and for the implicational relationships found in
chapter 4. I also discuss the results defining the term cluster reduction in a less strict way,
namely where one consonant is realised regardless of whether this consonant is part of one of the
target consonants. In chapter 6, I present the results of a pilot study that focuses on the
perception of clusters. The pilot study was carried out with a subset of the children from the main
study. I will compare production with perception results. Chapter 7 is devoted to the conclusion.
                                                                                                                   9


Chapter 2 Overview of the literature on cluster reduction


2.1      Introduction
In this chapter, I present an overview of the literature on variation in cluster reduction strategies
(C1C2 is realised as C1, C2, or Cother). Since my study focuses on word-initial consonant clusters, I
will limit myself to the literature on variation in reduction strategies of word-initial clusters,
discussing the reported patterns and theoretical accounts found in the literature. Two major
points are striking in this literature: i) the lack of quantitative data, which are needed to gain
insight into between-individual and within-individual variation, and ii) the role attributed to
sonority in accounting for the reported variability.
         In section 2.2, I will discuss patterns in realisation strategies in cluster reduction as
described in the literature. In section 2.3, I will discuss in more detail Fikkert’s data (1994) on
reduction patterns in Dutch from the perspective of variation. In section 2.4, I will discuss
theoretical accounts of variation in cluster reduction. Conclusions are given in section 2.5.


2.2      Patterns in cluster reduction
Consonant cluster reduction is a very common process in child language around the world (e.g.,
Beers 1995, Berman 1977, Bernhardt and Stemberger 1998, Chin and Dinnsen 1992, Grunwell
1982, Ingram 1976, Lleó and Prinz 1996, Locke 1983, McLeod, Van Doorn and Reed 2001a,
2001b, Schaerlakens and Gillis 1987, Smit 1993, Templin 1957). Given a word-initial C1C2
cluster target, often only one consonant surfaces in the child’s realisation of the cluster. The
consonant that is realised can be either part of the target cluster (C1C2 is realised as C1 or C2) or
shares features that are part of one or both consonants of the target cluster (C1C2 is realised as
CF(1,2), where F(1,2) means that the consonant has some features of C1 and some of C2). Deletion
of the whole cluster is possible, but reported to be rare (Ingram 1976, Chin and Dinnsen 1992,
Smit 1993).5
         Many linguists have explained the patterns in consonant cluster reduction in which only
one consonant is realised in terms of the manner feature [sonorant]. It is often pointed out that

5
 Smit (1993) reports on the realization of word-initial clusters by 1049 children in different age groups (from 2;0 to
9;0). Chin and Dinnsen (1992) report on the reduction data of 47 children with phonological disorders (ages between
3;4 to 6;8). All null onsets found in their database come from only one child, child 24. Bernhardt and Stemberger
                                                                                                               10


the child has a preference for realising the less sonorous segment of the onset, regardless of
whether this segment is the first or second member in the onset cluster (Barlow 1997, Fikkert
1994, Gierut 1999, Ingram 1989, Jakobson 1968/1941, Gnanadesikan 1995, Ohala 1996, 1999,
among others). In general, in an onset, a plosive is preferred over a fricative, and a fricative is
preferred over a nasal, liquid or glide, the more sonorous consonant class. For instance, if the
target cluster is stop-nasal, the stop is retained. If the target cluster is a fricative-stop, the stop is
retained. If the target cluster is a fricative-nasal the fricative is retained, etc. A typical
presentation of the sonority hierarchy is given in (2.1) (for discussion see Clements 1990, Selkirk
1984, Zec 1988).


(2.1)    plosives >> fricatives >> nasals >> liquids >> glides
         least sonorous                               most sonorous



The sonority-based prediction for retention of a consonant works quite well for the vast majority
of reduced consonant clusters. It is therefore understandable that the focus in explaining
reduction has often been put solely on the feature sonorant. However, sonority by itself does not
always lead to correct predictions, as many linguists have pointed out in recent years; see for
example, Barlow (1997), Goad and Rose (to appear), Jongstra (2000, 2001a), Pater and Barlow
(2002). In particular, the reduction pattern in /s/-sonorant clusters is reported to be deviant from
other clusters. These clusters are reported to be reduced, often but not always, in such a way that
the more sonorous consonant surfaces rather than the less sonorous consonant (Beers 1995, De
Houwer and Gillis 1998, Grunwell 1982, Smith 1973, Smit 1993). Examples of different
realisations for /s/-sonorant clusters and information about subjects, if available, are given for
English (2.2a) and for Dutch (2.2b).




(1998) note that deleting the whole cluster is reported to occur in very young children and in children with
phonological disorders.
                                                                                                    11


(2.2a)   Realisation of s-sonorant clusters: English
         Gnanadesikan (1995)       a fricative is realised
         Smith (1973)              a sonorant is realised
         Barlow (1997)             all sonorant clusters: /s/ is realised (subject 25)
                                   /s/-approximant clusters: approximant is realised (subject 24)
         Grunwell (1982)           /s/-nasal: nasal is realised
                                   /s/-approximant: /s/ or approximant is realised
         Ohala (1999)              /sn/: fricative (25%) or nasal (19%) is realised
         Pater and Barlow (2002) /s/-nasal: nasal is realised (Trevor and Julia)
                                   /s/-liquid: /s/ is realised (Trevor and Julia)
         Smit (1993)               /s/-nasal: nasal is usually realised;
                                   /s/-glide: glide is usually realised
                                   /s/-liquid: /s/ is usually realised
(2.2b)   Realisation of s-sonorant clusters: Dutch
         Fikkert (1994)            fricative-sonorant: fricative or sonorant is realised
         Beers (1995)              sonorant is usually realised; sometimes /s/



Three points in (2.2) are striking. First, the reported patterns are sometimes different between
and within data, reported by linguists. Looking for example at the reduction strategy of /s/-nasal
clusters, it is seen from (2.2) that some linguists report consistent or usual realisation of the nasal
(Beers 1995, Grunwell 1982, Pater and Barlow 2002, Smit 1993, Smith 1973); one other reports
consistent realisation of /s/ (Gnanadesikan 1995), while others report both /s/ and nasal
realisations (Barlow 1997, Fikkert 1994, Ohala 1999).
         The second striking aspect of (2.2) is that different patterns seem to be related to different
children for those linguists who report on individual reduction strategies (Barlow 1997, Pater and
Barlow 2002, Gnanadesikan 1995, Smith 1973). Thus, the individual child seems to be
consistent. For example, Smith’s child systematically realises sonorants. Smith’s rule 7 (1973)
predicts deletion of /s/ in all initial sC clusters. Gnanadesikan’s child on the other hand
systematically realises fricatives in /s/-sonorant clusters. Apparently, both of these children are
consistent in their choice of reduction strategy (both Smith and Gnanadesikan are studies of
single children, while the other studies included a number of children).6 Barlow notes that /sw/ is
realised as [s] by one child (subject 25) and as [w] by another child (subject 24). Fikkert reports
                                                                                                                    12


that besides the usual pattern in which the fricative is realised in a fricative-sonorant cluster,
some children might realise the sonorant at a certain stage of their development, suggesting that
variation observed in these children is over time rather than at the same point in time (see section
2.3). Whether the variation reported by other linguists listed in (2.2) is between or within
children is not always clear, since these linguists present their reduction results across children
(Beers 1995, Grunwell 1982, Ohala 1999, Smit 1993). For example, Ohala bases her conclusions
on 16 children, looking at group results. Out of the group of fricative-sonorant clusters, Ohala
targeted words with /sn/ clusters only, thus no other fricative sonorant clusters (/sm/, /sl/, /sw/)
were tested by her. The main goal of Ohala’s thesis is to investigate whether reduction patterns
are based on sonority or on articulatory ease. The targets were non-existing but possible words of
English. The task was an imitation task. Although the percentages in the realisation patterns of
fricative-nasal clusters indicate that there is variation in that sometimes the nasal is realised and
sometimes the fricative is realised, Ohala nevertheless argues that the choice of consonant in
C1C2 is sonority-based (Ohala 1996, 1999). Why Ohala makes this claim is unclear to me.
         The third striking aspect of these studies is the variable patterning reported within the
group of /s/-sonorant clusters. Grunwell (1982) reports that either [s] or [l] can be realised in /sl/
clusters, whereas it is only the nasal that is realised in /sm/ and /sn/ clusters. Smit (1993) claims
that relative markedness plays a role in reduction strategies: out of the two target consonants it is
the unmarked consonant that is realised, resulting in C2 in /s/-plosive, /s/-nasal, /s/-glide clusters,
since stops, nasals, and glides are less marked than /s/ according to Smit, but C1 in /s/-liquid
clusters, since /s/ is less marked than /l/. The notion of markedness refers to order of acquisition:
plosives, nasals and glides are acquired earlier than liquids. However, occasional realisations of
the more marked consonant are reported by Smit. In their discussion of reduction strategies,
Pater and Barlow (2002) include data from two children (Trevor and Julia) for whom /s/-nasal
clusters are realised as nasal, but /s/-liquid clusters (and other fricative-liquid clusters) are
realised as /s/.
         Summarising the reports, it seems that there is variation in the realisation of /s/-sonorant
clusters: sometimes /s/ is realised and sometimes the sonorant. Comparing reduction strategies
within /s/-sonorant clusters, it seems that /s/-nasal clusters are usually realised as the nasal, while

6
 A difference between the two children is that Smith’s child realizes the target sonorant systematically, whereas
Gnanadesikan’s child does not always realize the target fricative, but a consonant that shares features of both target
consonants (/sm/ is realized as [f]; /sn/ is realized as [s]; [sw] is realized as [f]).
                                                                                                13


/s/-liquid clusters are usually realised as /s/. Given that there is variation in the realisation of
sonorant clusters, and that there is a difference in reduction strategy between different /s/-
sonorant clusters, one must question whether sonority is the appropriate feature for
characterising how reduction occurs.
       Studying variation patterns in reduction strategies requires i) data from many children to
report on possible between-child variation, ii) a large sample per individual child to report on
possible within-child variation, iii) many group and individual observations per individual cluster
to report on possible variation between clusters. The main problem of the studies listed in (2.2) is
that they lack one or more of these three requirements. Barlow (1998) and Fikkert (1994) are
both studies involving a number of children, 6 and 12 respectively, with a detailed discussion of
both group and individual results. While they discuss individual children, they do not provide
percentages of reduction patterns per child per individual cluster. We thus do not have a clear
idea of the number of realisations and, thus, whether realisations represent highly frequent,
regular, or maybe (unwanted) incidental patterns. Both Barlow and Fikkert are aware of this fact
and present their data as “illustrational” rather than definite. Ohala (1998, 1999) on the other
hand uses statistics, but does not focus on individual results. Also, she groups her data into
sonority classes, taking only one cluster type as an example of a sonority class (for instance, she
uses only targets with an /sn/ cluster for the group of fricative-nasal clusters).
       In summary, in order to make generalisations about reduction strategies, there is a need
for studying both between-individual and within-individual variation patterns in child language
systems in a more systematic way. As far as I know, there is no study yet that focuses on
between-individual variation and within-individual variation in the reduction strategy of
individual clusters. The fact that there are no such studies is awkward since its importance is
underlined in recent literature (De Houwer and Gillis 1998, Fikkert 1994, Pater and Barlow
2002). The main goal of this thesis is to show patterns of between- and within-individual
variation in the acquisition of individual clusters.
       In the next section, I will discuss in more detail variation patterns in Fikkert’s (1994)
study. I take Fikkert’s study as the one to look at in greater detail since this is the only one in
which individual reduction patterns for Dutch word-initial clusters are discussed.
                                                                                                               14


2.3     Variation in cluster reduction in Fikkert’s data (1994)
In her thesis, Fikkert (1994) sheds light on the acquisition of prosodic structure in Dutch. She
accounts for phonological development in terms of resetting parameters on the basis of positive
evidence. The child first sets a parameter to the default setting, and then resets the parameter at a
later stage to the more marked setting, thereby getting closer to the target language. In chapter
four of her thesis, Fikkert focuses on patterns in cluster development for twelve children. She
shows that children first have just one consonant and then move on to a stage where they have
two consonants, but not necessarily the target consonants. Finally they acquire the target cluster.
The process of cluster reduction is explained by an early parameter that does not allow for
complex onsets. The maximal-onset parameter is given in (2.3).7


(2.3)   Maximal onset parameter (Fikkert 1994, p.124)
        Number of onset positions is equal or smaller than 1:                  On  



Thus initially, a child can only have one consonant member in the onset and, as parameters are
reset, the number of consonants that are realised can increase.
        Fikkert collected the data in a joint project with Levelt (1994). The database (CLPF-
database: Claartje Levelt and Paula Fikkert) consists of longitudinal data for 12 children with
beginning ages between 1;4 and 1;11 years. Children were tape-recorded every other week in a
naturalistic setting for time spans varying between 7 and 16 months.8 Realising that all studies
have both advantages and disadvantages, the great advantage of a design like this is that all the
speech in the corpus is spontaneous and spoken in a natural environment. The disadvantage is
that it is impossible to systematically target particular structures like different word-initial
clusters. Although the data provide insight into how prosodic structure is acquired, there are not

7
  Fikkert combines the maximal onset parameter with two others that have a dependent relation in which c implies b,
and b implies a. a Number of onset positions is equal to 1: On = 1
                  b Number of onset positions is equal or smaller than 1: On  
                  c Number of onset positions is equal to or smaller than 2: On  
8
  Information for children in CLPF database:
Child start       end       months           child   start     end      months
1         1;4.18 2;4.1      12               7       1;4.12 1;11.8 7
2         1;7.14 2;11.0 16                   8       1;5.11 2;4.28 12
3         1;10.10 2;2.10 13                  9       1;9.15 1;11.18 2
4         1;7.25 2;6.12 10                   10      1;10.1 2;9.2       11
5         1;6.4   2;4.29 11                  11      1;10.25 2;8.19 9
6         1;10.12 2;7.4     9                12      1;11.8 2;6.11 7
                                                                                                              15


enough data per child per session per cluster to analyze within-individual and between-individual
variation in reduction strategies. However, it is more than worthwhile to look at generalisations
in the CLPF database in the light of variation in cluster reduction.
        Fikkert groups the data in terms of sonority classes, referring to the following clusters:
PL, FL, PN, FN, PG, FG, sP, and sF clusters. PL refers to plosive-liquid (i.e., /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/,
/tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/); FL refers to fricative-liquid (i.e., /fl/, /fr/, /sl/, /xl/, /xr/); PN refers to plosive-
nasal (i.e., /kn/); FN refers to fricative-nasal (i.e., /sm/, /sn/); PG refers to plosive-glide (i.e., /tw/,
/kw/); FG refers to fricative glide (i.e., /sw/); sP refers to s-plosive (i.e., /sp/, /st/, /sk/); sF refers
to s-fricative (i.e., /sx/). The term reduction is used to describe realisation as either a sonorant or
a non-sonorant consonant, regardless of the target consonants. A crucial question that arises from
this study is if it is indeed the case that sonority classes pattern as a group in reduction, or if there
is output variation between clusters from the same sonority group (for example, whether there is
variation between the members of the /s/-plosive cluster group: /sp/, /st/, and /sk/). The appendix
of her thesis contains a detailed overview per individual child (nine children in total) regarding
development in simple and complex onsets. Again, segments are grouped here in sonority classes
and individual clusters are not given. Thus, we cannot see, for instance, if a child has the lateral
or the rhotic earlier in a PL cluster.
        In what follows, I will discuss Fikkert’s data from the perspective of between-individual
variation (section 2.3.1) and of within-individual variation (section 2.3.2). The conclusion is
given in section 2.3.3.


2.3.1   Between-individual variation
In this section I look at variation in reduction strategies, as deduced from the reduction patterns
Fikkert presents in her appendix D and chapter 4. In terms of between-individual differences in
reduction strategies, I focus on cases where a cluster is reduced to one single consonant,
regardless of whether this consonant is part of the target cluster (e.g. CC is realised as C). I
compare reduction patterns across children during the entire set of recordings, as reported by
Fikkert, i.e. between-individual variation across time across children. In Table 2.1, I present the
different patterns in cluster reduction, indicated by output 1, output 2, etc., seen across children.
Recall that this Table is not from Fikkert’s thesis, but rather is deduced from the information on
                                                                                                 16


reduction patterns that Fikkert presents in the appendix and chapter 4. Abbreviations: P= plosive;
F= fricative (including /s/); N= nasal; L= liquid; h= [h]; s= [s].


Table 2.1       Between-individual variation across children overtime in CLPF database
Target          output one      output two      output three    output four
PG              P
PL              P               L
sP              P               s
PN              P               N               L
FG              F               L               h               G
FN              N               F               L               h
FL              P               F               h               L
sF              F               P               h               N



From Table 2.1, it can be seen, for instance, that there is a difference between clusters with a
plosive and clusters with a fricative, where the former has fewer instances of variation (is more
stable) and the latter has more instances of variation (is more variable). For example, there is
only one reduced output reported for the cluster group of plosive-glide clusters (PG), namely the
plosive (P), while there are as many as four outputs reported for the cluster group of /s/-fricatives
(sF), namely F, P, h, N.
         Notice that Fikkert distinguishes between a fricative and an [h] as possible output, with
the goal of relating cluster realisations and singleton realisations. The children who first realise
an [h] do not have fricatives yet in their singleton production. Some realisations of clusters are
not attested for every child that Fikkert and Levelt recorded. Realisations of fricative-nasal,
plosive-nasal, and plosive-glide are especially rare, as Fikkert warns the reader. Although we
clearly see some generalizations, it is impossible to obtain detailed insight into between-
individual variation from these data since clusters are presented as groups and the data are from
nine children, with insufficient target clusters per session per child to draw conclusions of this
type. Recall that the data in Table 2.1 given above are across children and over time. It is
important to know if variation is also found within a child. I will discuss within-child variation in
the CLPF data in section 2.3.2.
         Fikkert assumes that the reduction strategy is always towards the less sonorous segment
except in cases where this segment has not been acquired yet. Thus, she explains the realisation
                                                                                                   17


pattern in a fricative-nasal cluster, where the nasal is realised by some children but not all, as the
result of the lack of the fricative in the child's system for those that have the N realisation and its
presence in those that have F. This is indeed the case for some children, but not for all. In
addition, we also saw that a child reduced a plosive-nasal cluster towards a liquid, while this
child has certainly acquired plosives. Fikkert stresses that her conclusions must be regarded as
tentative since little data is available. We must be cautious and check if the child's reduction
strategy is dependent on acquired singletons, and if so, to what extent.
        Summarizing the findings so far, based on the data discussed in Fikkert (1994) we see
that there is variation between clusters in the way that they are typically reduced. Some clusters
are more stable in that they are reduced more often to one target consonant class, others are more
variable in that they are reduced to both target consonant classes.


2.3.2   Within-individual variation
Regarding the issue of variation in reduction strategies within a child, it seems that the children
in Fikkert’s study are quite consistent in their reduction strategy at a certain point in time. Only
for the /sx/ cluster do we see any synchronic within-individual variation, in this for three of the
nine children: one child has instances of both a plosive and an [h] at the same time. It is difficult
to discuss individual synchronic variation in reduction strategies in Fikkert’s data, since only a
few realisations of clusters are given per recording per child.
        The important question at this point is whether a child varies in his/her reduction strategy.
Fikkert discusses cluster development in terms of continuous stages, in which a difference
between outputs is clearly seen when comparing one stage with a following stage, though
without it being true that at one particular stage there are no cases that are typical for the other
stage and vice versa. In other words, changes are gradual rather than across-the-board, and thus
are not transitional, but continuous. The stages are summarized per cluster group by Fikkert
(1994, p.103) in Tables 2.2a, b and c. Realisations between square brackets are called "optional"
by Fikkert, indicating that only some children go through that stage. Thus, these are the
realisations that are seen in some, but not all, children.
                                                                                                                     18


Table 2.2          Development of consonant clusters (in Fikkert 1994) 9
Table 2.2(a)       Development of plosive-sonorant clusters (in Fikkert 1994)
    Target   Stage 1   Stage2                   Stage 3
    PL       P         [L]                      [PG]             PL
    PG       P                                                   PL        PG
    PN       P         [L]                                       PL        PN


Table 2.2b         Development of fricative-sonorant clusters (in Fikkert 1994)
    Target   Stage 1             Stage 2                  Stage 3
    FL       P               F   [L]                      [PL]        FL
    FG                    F      [G/L]                                FL        FG
    FN                    F      [N/L]                                FL        FN


Table 2.2c         Development of /s/-obstruent clusters (in Fikkert 1994)
    Target   Stage 1             Stage 2                              Stage 3
    SP       P                   [s]                                  sP
    SF       P               F                                                              sF



In Table 2.2a, we see that a plosive-liquid cluster (PL) is realised in stage 1 as a plosive (P). In
stage 2 a liquid is realised (L) by some children. Other children skip stage 2 and go directly into
stage 3 in which two consonants are realised, regardless of whether this is the target consonant or
not. In stage 3 some children realise a plosive-glide cluster before they realise the target cluster
(PL). Others immediately realise the target cluster. For the plosive-glide clusters (PG) we see no
variation in reduction: in stage 1 all children retain the plosive (P). Then all children move on to
stage 3 where they realise two consonants. All children first realise a plosive-liquid cluster (PL)
before realising the target cluster (PG). Although it is very useful to see clear generalizations in
cluster development, I find that the notion of “stage” is sometimes misleading and that it does
not give the information we need to get insight into within-individual variation across time. This
is because the stages that are non-optional (thus that do not have square brackets) are
nevertheless not attested for every child. For example, as is seen in Table 2.2.a in the plosive-

changing their reduction strategy. They are not listed in Table 2.3, since this Table only presents
the paths in which there is a change in reduction strategy. For plosive-nasal clusters two children

strategy towards an [s] before realising clusters. In fricative-liquid clusters, out of four children

realising the fricative-liquid cluster. For fricative-nasal clusters, out of three children who start
with a nasal, two go directly into a complex cluster, whether it is a fricative-liquid cluster
(attested for one child) or a fricative-nasal cluster (attested for the other child). One child first
has a fricative and then does not develop further during the recording period. Out of two children
that start with a fricative, one child does not develop, and the other child goes into a stage
producing nasals, and does not develop further during the recording period. The other two
children do not develop after their first realisation during the recording period. For fricative-glide
clusters we see a change in reduction strategy for one child. She first has fricatives and then
liquids before she realises a complex cluster (fricative-liquid). The child that starts with a glide
then develops towards the fricative-glide clusters. The others do not develop during the recording
period. Finally, for /s/-fricative clusters, we see that the children who start with a fricative go
directly into the stage of the target complex cluster. The children who start with a plosive have a
fricative and then there is no further development during the recording period. The children that
start with [h] have different paths. One child does not develop further during the recording
period; one child realises a fricative. The other child has /s/ as the final segment in preceding
syllables and has [h] as onsets. Also, the child who starts with a nasal does not develop further

FL      4       2, 3, 4, 5                        5                1, 6, 7, 8, 9
sF      4       2, 3, 4, 9                        5                1, 5, 6, 7, 8



As we can see from Table 2.4, the majority of cluster reduction cases involve within-child stable
realisations. There is a difference between clusters: cluster class PG is stable in realisation while
cluster classes FL, sF are highly variable.


Summarizing the findings on within-individual variation over time, we see that i) there are more
cases of within-child stable reduction strategies in clusters than cases of within-child variable
reduction strategies, and ii) there is more within-individual variation (over time) in clusters with
a fricative than in clusters with a plosive.
        As noted before, it is very difficult to obtain detailed insights into within-individual
variation in reduction patterns from these data because of the limited number of items per child
per session per individual cluster. For example, we do not know whether some reductions are
simply coincidental rather than systematic. Also, we do not know whether there are differences
within a cluster group. Fikkert assumes that the regular path is from a plosive towards a complex
                                                                                                   22


cluster. Optional stages are explained as pragmatic rather than linguistic: in learning the cluster,
the child pays attention to the other member of the cluster.


There are two main conclusions to draw from this study. First, there is variation in reduction
strategies across clusters. Clusters with a plosive are in general more stable in their reduction
strategy than clusters with a fricative. Second, although in general children seem to be consistent
in their reduction strategy during a certain period of time, within-individual variation over time
occurs, but again more in the clusters that have a fricative than in the clusters that have a plosive.
       I will test whether these findings are confirmed in the present study.


2.4    Theoretical account of variation in reduction
So far we have seen that sonority classes provide a general account for which consonant of an
initial cluster is retained. However, it has been recognized lately that unexpected patterns with
the more sonorant segment realised also occur. Two accounts for this are found in the current
theoretical literature. First, some appeal to features other than sonority (Barlow 1997, Bernhardt
and Stemberger 1998, Pater and Barlow 2002). Second, others base their prediction on prosodic
position (Goad and Rose to appear, Rose 2000). Both accounts are worked out within the
framework of Optimality Theory (e.g. Prince and Smolensky 1993, see Kager 1999, McCarthy
2002 for general overviews). Currently, much work in phonological acquisition is done within
the theoretical framework of Optimality Theory (OT); see Bernhardt and Stemberger (1998) for
background and details of an OT based account of phonological processes in child language.
Before I discuss these two accounts of unexpected reductions in more detail, I will briefly
discuss how OT deals with regular sonority based reduction patterns in child language. My
purpose in this thesis is not to evaluate precise OT computations. Rather, I am more interested in
the empirical coverage of the proposed models. In other words, I will discuss which patterns are
predicted to occur and/or predicted not to occur within the OT analyses of Goad and Rose (to
appear) and Pater and Barlow (2002), and, in chapter 4, I will compare these with the actual
findings.
                                                                                                         23


2.4.1    Cluster reduction in OT
In OT, well-formedness constraints evaluate possible outputs. The constraints are violable. The
ranking of constraints is what creates variation between languages. The higher ranked constraints
are satisfied, the lower ranked constraints may not be. There are two major types of constraints:
faithfulness and markedness constraints. Faithfulness constraints require outputs to look like the
input, while markedness constraints require outputs to be unmarked. For example, a coda in an
input structure with a coda will be realised in a grammar with a constraint ranking where the
relevant faithfulness constraint dominates the constraint NOCODA. The coda of this input will not
be realised in a grammar where the constraint NOCODA dominates the faithfulness constraint.
The following constraints are of importance in accounting for reduction strategies: *COMPLEX
ONSET and MAX . The first constraint *COMPLEX-ONSET in (2.4a) is a markedness constraint. It
militates against realisations that have complex onsets (i.e. onsets with consonant clusters).12 The
second constraint MAX in (2.4b) is a faithfulness constraint and militates against deletion of any
segment from the input.


(2.4a)   *COMPLEX-ONSET: No branching structure within the onset
(2.4b)   MAX: Segments in the input must correspond with segments in the output



The difference between children who reduce consonant clusters and the children who have
acquired clusters is explained by a difference in the rankings of the constraints *COMPLEX-
ONSET and MAX, displayed in Table 2.5a and Table 2.5b. Simply speaking, the child who
reduces clusters has a grammar in which *COMPLEX-ONSET dominates MAX, thus ruling out
complex clusters, as seen in Table 2.5a. Candidate a. is ruled out because it violates the highly
ranked constraint *COMPLEX-ONSET. In accordance with OT-practice, a violation of a constraint
is indicated by an asterisk (*) and a fatal violation is indicated by an exclamation mark (!) behind
the asterisk. The evaluation of constraint MAX for candidate a. is irrelevant (indicated by gray
shading) since the candidate is already ruled out by the higher ranked constraint *COMPLEX-
ONSET. Candidate c. is ruled out because it violates the lower ranked constraint MAX twice, since
two consonants are left out of the output. Candidate b is the optimal candidate.


12
  The constraint *COMPLEX-ONSET is a more specific version of the constraint *COMPLEX (Prince and Smolensky
1993) which militates against the association of more than one C or V to any syllable node.
                                                                                               24


          The child who realises clusters has a grammar in which MAX dominates *COMPLEX-
ONSET, given in Table 2.5b. Candidate b. is ruled out because it violates the higher ranked
constraint MAX. Candidate c. is left out because it violates this constraint twice. The evaluation
by the lower ranked constraint *COMPLEX-ONSET is irrelevant (grey shading). Candidate a. is the
winner since it satisfies the higher ranked constraint *COMPLEX-ONSET.


Table 2.5 OT tableaux of grammar with cluster versus grammar without clusters
Table 2.5a OT tableau of a grammar without clusters: *COMPLEX-ONSET dominates MAX
/CCV/                                *COMPLEX-ONSET                      MAX
a. [CCV]                             *!
b. [CV]   Í                                                              *
c. [V]                                                                   **!


Table 2.5b         OT tableau with clusters: MAX dominates *COMPLEX-ONSET
/CCV/                                MAX                                 *COMPLEX-ONSET
a. [CCV]   Í                                                             *
b. [CV]                              *!
c. [V]                               *!*



The ranking of *COMPLEX-ONSET and MAX simply decides whether clusters are allowed or not,
but these constraints alone do not indicate which consonant of a cluster remains if that cluster is
reduced, i.e. *COMPLEX-ONSET is ranked above MAX.
          The sonority scale-based prediction has been translated in OT into a family of negative
harmony constraints. These harmony constraints form a hierarchy which militates against what
are known as disharmonies structures (see 2.5a). Some output structures are more harmonic (i.e.,
unmarked with respect to markedness and therefore structurally more ideal) than others, and this
is revealed by the fixed ranking of the different constraints. A possible hierarchy to account for
the preference that Dutch onsets show for obstruents is given in (2.5b).


(2.5a)    Harmony constraint (Prince and Smolensky 1993)
          *M/segment: a segment must not be parsed as a syllable margin.
(2.5b)    Margin Hierarchy: *M/l, r >> *M/ m, n >> *M/ f, v, s, z, x >> *M/p, b, t, d, k
                                                                                                   25


According to this hierarchy, liquids are least preferred in onsets and stops are most preferred.
The Margin Hierarchy accounts for the fact that preferred onsets have plosives rather than
fricatives, and fricatives rather than nasals, and so on. Thus, in a child’s grammar where complex
structures are forbidden by a dominating constraint *COMPLEX-ONSET and in which the sonority
hierarchy (2.5a) is the only relevant constraint, a cluster /fl/ will be realised as [f] rather than as
[l], since an output with a fricative is more harmonic than an output with a liquid. This
grammar’s ranking is given in (2.6) (only relevant constraints are given).


(2.6)      *COMPLEX-ONSET, *M/l >> *M/ f



The ranking in (2.6) gives an oversimplified view of rankings in child grammars. If sonority
were the only relevant constraint in reduction, there would be no between-individual or within-
individual variation. All reduced clusters would be realised as the less sonorous segment of the
two. As I discussed in section 2.2 however, variation is observed in the literature in the reduction
patterns of /s/-sonorant clusters: some children sometimes realise the sonorant, others realise the
/s/. In the next two sections I will discuss two theoretical accounts of variation in realisation
patterns within OT.


2.4.2      Variation explained by prosodic structure
Goad and Rose (to appear) hypothesize that variation in the realisation of /s/-sonorant clusters
where two patterns occur, [s] and the sonorant, is due to differences in the input representation:
we propose that the source for the two patterns of cluster reduction is the mismatch between the
head of the onset and relative prominence in rising sonority /s/-initial clusters. Goad and Rose
assume that the acquisition of a grammar involves not only constraint reranking, as discussed in
2.4.1, but also input elaboration.13 Their account of cluster reduction is purely prosodically
based, in which constraints explicitly refer to heads of syllable constituents. For example, a
constraint MAXHEAD (ONSET) requires outputs in which the segment in the head position of the
onset is realised, given in 2.7 (adopted from Goad and Rose).




13
     Goad and Rose assume that at the segmental level children have adult representations.
                                                                                                                        26


(2.7)    MAXHEAD (ONSET)              Every segment prosodified in the head of the onset in the input has a
                                      correspondent in the head of the onset in the output.



Crucial to their analysis of cluster reduction are two points. First is the assumption that branching
onsets are left-headed. Second is the assumption that in adult languages with initial sC structures,
/s/ is extrasyllabic in all word-initial /s/C clusters (i.e. a left-edge appendix), whereas other
obstruents in obstruent-initial clusters are not extrasyllabic. Many linguists have argued that in
languages that have sC clusters /s/ is extrasyllabic (see Davis 1990, Harris 1994, Treiman et al.,
1992, Trommelen 1984, and Van der Hulst 1984).14 I will use the term appendix to indicate these
extrasyllabic /s/'s. A constraint that militates against sC outputs is required in the grammar of
languages that do not have sC outputs (like Spanish). This constraint is given in (2.8) (taken from
Goad and Rose (to appear)).


(2.8)    *APPENDIX-LEFT:              A consonant at the left edge must be immediately dominated by Onset



This constraint is met if an sC cluster in word-initial position is syllabified as an onset, and is
violated if it is syllabified as (s) appendix (C) onset. Before I present the constraint rankings
Goad and Rose propose to account for variation in the realisation of /s/-sonorant initial clusters, I
will discuss what they consider to be the source of variation.
         Goad and Rose argue that children can differ in selecting heads in their inputs, depending
on their knowledge of the internal structure of the syllable and thus depending on stage in
development (for discussion of different representational prosodic structure see also Barlow
1997 (differences between children) and Barlow 2001 (differences between adult speakers)).
Goad and Rose hypothesize that initially children select heads according to what they call a
sonority pattern; at a later stage they select heads according to the so-called head pattern. Not all
children necessarily go through both stages: some children skip stage 1 and immediately follow
the head pattern. I will explain these two patterns in more detail.
         At an early stage of development, a child selects heads in the inputs on the basis of
sonority, i.e. relative prominence; this is what Goad and Rose refer to as the sonority pattern.



14
   Fikkert (1994) assumes that only sC clusters with flat or falling sonority are heterosyllabic in Dutch (i.e., /sx/, /sp/,
/st/, /sk/).
                                                                                                     27


The least sonorous consonant will always be maintained under this strategy. The selection of the
head based on a sonority pattern for three different structures is given in (2.9).


(2.9)   Inputs for sonority pattern child (stage 1) (Goad and Rose (to appear)):
a. Branching onset               b. Rising sonority /s/-initial    c. Falling sonority /s/-initial
                 1                                  1                                           1


        O        N                        O         N                                 O         N


        p   l    V                        s    l    V                       s         p         V



In (2.9a), a structure with a branching onset in the target language is given. Following the
sonority pattern, /p/ rather than /l/ in underlying structure /plV/ is prosodified as head by the
child since /p/ is lower in sonority than /l/. Segment /l/ is not prosodified. In (2.9b), a structure
with rising sonority starting with /s/ is given. In the target language every /s/ in sC clusters is
extrasyllabic according to Goad and Rose. However, the child has not yet received enough
evidence that /s/ in sC clusters is extrasyllabic. Therefore, the child follows the sonority pattern:
the head is selected based on relative sonority, thus /s/ rather than /l/ in underlying structure /slV/
is prosodified as head since /s/ is lower in sonority than /l/. In (2.9c), head selection in an sC
structure with falling sonority is given following the sonority pattern. Again, the child does not
yet know that /s/ in sC clusters is extrasyllabic and thus selects the head according to relative
sonority: /p/ is the head and /s/ is not prosodified. Notice that (2.9a) and (2.9c) are prosodified
according to the adult language. In (2.9b) the child has selected the head incorrectly.
        At a later stage, the child’s inputs develop regarding syllable-internal structure. When the
child has learned, based on distributional facts, that initial /s/C clusters are heterosyllabic, /s/ in
initial /s/C clusters is in an appendix and thus the segment other than /s/ is the head, whether this
is a sonorant or obstruent. This is what Goad and Rose call the head pattern. The selection of the
head based on the head pattern for three different structures, is given in (2.10).
                                                                                                              28


(2.10)   Inputs for head pattern child (stage 2) (Goad and Rose (to appear)):
a. Branching onset                b. Rising sonority /s/-initial            c. Falling sonority /s/-initial
                                                     PWd                                       PWd


                 1                                   1                                         1


         O       N                         O         N                               O         N


         p   l   V                  s      l         V                          s    p         V



As seen in (2.10a), there is no relevant difference with the selection in the sonority pattern except
that the child now understands that /p/ is the head and /l/ is the dependent in the onset structure.
In (2.10b) the child has now learned that /s/ in sC clusters is extrasyllabic. The child prosodifies
/s/ in the appendix. /l/ will be prosodified as head, akin to the adult language. In (2.10c), again /s/
is prosodified in the appendix and /p/ is the head.


The constraint ranking for grammars of children that reduce clusters proposed by Goad and Rose
is given in (2.11).


(2.11)   *COMPLEX-ONS, *APPENDIX-LEFT >> MAXHEAD >> MAX



This constraint ranking leads to different outputs based on differences in the input structure, i.e.
the child’s knowledge about internal prosodification. In other words, a difference in output is a
result of a difference in input rather than of a difference in constraint ranking since, in both
stages the constraint ranking is the same. In (2.12) an OT tableau for /sl/ at stage 1 (sonority
pattern) is given. In (2.13) an OT tableau for /sl/ of stage 2 (head pattern) is given.

             Notice that the constraint ranking in both tableaux is identical, but that the crucial
difference between (2.12) and (2.13) is which segment is represented in the head position in the
input. In (2.12), it is /s/, in (2.13) it is /l/.
             The differences in realisation patterns between obstruent-sonorant, /s/-obstruent and /s/-
sonorant clusters can be accounted for by different underlying representations, as displayed in
Table 2.6.
                                                                                                  31


Table 2.6        Realisations in head and sonority pattern (Goad and Rose (to appear))
Cluster type             Sonority pattern                 Head pattern
                         Retained C                       Retained C
obstruent-sonorant       obstruent                        obstruent
/s/-obstruent            obstruent                        obstruent
/s/-sonorant             /s/                              sonorant



As is seen in Table 2.6, variation in /s/-sonorant clusters is predicted in that the sonorant is
realised following the head pattern, and /s/ is realised following the sonority pattern. No variation
is predicted in obstruent-sonorant and /s/-obstruent clusters. I will discuss in chapter 5 whether
these findings are confirmed in this study.
         The analysis of Goad and Rose assumes not only that variation occurs in /s/-sonorant
clusters across children, due to different patterns, but also that one child can first realise [s],
following the sonority pattern, and then at a later stage realise the [sonorant], following the head
pattern. As I noted before, Goad and Rose assume that a child can skip the sonority pattern,
resulting in realisations in which the less sonorous segment is always realised, regardless of the
cluster. Data in which the sonorant is first realised followed by [s] in a /s/-sonorant cluster are
not predicted by Goad and Rose, since this would mean that a child first follows the head pattern
at stage 1 and then the sonority pattern at stage 2. These predictions are confirmed in the CLPF
database, except for one child, child 7. This child first realises /sn/ clusters following the head
pattern (i.e., [n]) and then following the sonority pattern (i.e., [s]). Since the reductions for this
child and for these clusters are limited to one example, Goad and Rose question how
representative these forms are. I will test in chapter 4 if reductions according to head pattern
followed by reductions according to sonority pattern are indeed non-occurring in a larger sample.
         Another claim that Goad and Rose make is that patterns could be different for /s/-nasal
clusters (/sm/, and /sn/) and /s/-lateral clusters (/sl/), accounted for by an intermediate stage. In
this stage, a child follows the head pattern (stage 2) for /s/-nasal clusters but persists in the
sonority pattern (stage 1) for /s/-lateral clusters. The rationale for this intermediate stage is that
the child has less evidence to know that /sl/ clusters are heterosyllabic, compared to /s/-nasal
clusters, since laterals are much better dependents than nasals (cf. clay, bleak, glad versus
*bneak). Goad and Rose do not go into detail regarding the exact structural difference between
/sl/ and /s/-nasal clusters, but they refer to the term “markedness”, and state that a difference in
                                                                                                    32


rising sonority plays a role for some children (namely those who have the intermediate stage).
Goad and Rose note that their prediction is attested in Fikkert’s data (child 3, child 6 and child
8). These children realise the nasal in /s/-nasal clusters, but /s/ in /s/-lateral clusters. I will test
whether this prediction is confirmed in my larger study.
         The predictions for outputs at both stages for those children who are variable over time,
i.e. who change their reduction strategy without skipping stages, are summarized in Table 2.7.


Table 2.7         Predictions made in Goad and Rose regarding change in reduction strategy
                                  Stage 1                         Stage 2
                                  /s/-nasal       /s/-lateral     /s/-nasal       /s/-lateral
predicted to occur                [s]             [s]             nasal           lateral
                                  [s]             [s]             nasal           [s]
predicted not to occur            [s]             [s]             [s]             lateral
                                  nasal           lateral         [s]             [s]
                                  nasal           lateral         nasal           [s]



I will check whether these successions of output patterns (over time) occur in my study.


In summary, Goad and Rose argue that the source of variation in the realisation of /s/-sonorant
clusters lies in the difference in underlying prosodic representations of children. At the initial
stage, children do not yet have enough knowledge of internal prosodic structure. They select the
head according to sonority (sonority pattern at stage 1). This results in realising [s] in /s/-
sonorant clusters. At a later stage (head pattern at stage 2), they have gained more knowledge
about internal prosodic structure, i.e. they know that there are heads and dependents. They select
the head according to the principle that heads are at the left edge. Furthermore, they know now
that /s/ in initial sC clusters is extrasyllabic. This results in realising the sonorant in /s/-sonorant
clusters. Children might skip stage 1. Goad and Rose thus predict that for /s/-sonorant clusters: i)
a child first can realise the [s] and at a later stage realise the nasal, but not vice versa, or ii) a
child always realises the sonorant or [s] and then acquires a cluster in the following stage. Since
laterals are better dependents than nasals, an intermediate stage might occur in which a child
realises the nasal in /s/-nasal clusters and the [s] in /s/-lateral clusters.
                                                                                                  33


2.4.3   Variation explained by other features in cluster reduction
The second account of cluster reduction is based on features rather than prosody. Bernhardt and
Stemberger (1998, p.388) observe that variation occurs in fricative-sonorant clusters. For
example, the cluster /sn/ can be realised by either [s] or [n] whereas /sp/ is more stable: it is
always realised as [p] by young children. Therefore, they argue that variation in patterns is
explained not only by the sonorant feature, but also by the interaction of additional features. In
assuming the feature [sonorant] to be important, but only one of a constellation of factors that
enters into predicting reduction strategies, Bernhardt and Stemberger (1998) differ from the
traditional account where sonority is the only force in choosing a deletion strategy (Fikkert 1994,
Gierut 1999, Gnanadesikan 1995, Ingram 1989, Jakobson 1968/1941, Ohala 1996, 1999).
Although Bernhardt and Stemberger do not go into detail on which features interact in which
ways, they suggest that variability in fricative-nasal clusters is explained by the interaction of the
features [sonorant] and [continuant]. They assume that the feature [continuant] is marked in
onset position and that nasals and stops are the least marked segments in onset position. The
prediction is that there is sometimes C1 deletion and sometimes C2 deletion: the nasal is
sometimes deleted, because the child wants to syllabify the least sonorous segment and the
fricative is sometimes deleted since the presence of the feature [continuant] in fricatives is more
marked.
        Preferred consonants are those with unmarked features or those without marked features.
In general, it has been observed that child phonology reflects universal principles, i.e. the
unmarked surface structures in child language align with unmarked structures in language
typology (Jakobson 1941/1968; Stampe, 1969). Thus, typical segments in early language such as
/p/ and /t/, and typical structures such as CV syllables occur most frequently in languages around
the world. Regarding features such as [sonorant] and [continuant], we see that these are both
marked in early language and cross-linguistically (Bernhardt and Stemberger, 1998). Thus the
child has a preference for unmarked features. In a /sn/ cluster, the /s/ is marked with respect to
the feature [continuant], while [n] is marked regarding the feature [sonorant]. This might explain
variation in the realisation of this cluster according to Bernhardt and Stemberger.
        Preferred features are derived from general patterns in consonantal development. The
order in which consonants are acquired is often explained in terms of substitution patterns.
Substitution is argued to be caused by the child's preference for unmarked structures with respect
                                                                                                                   34


to manner, place and voice features. I will now discuss regular patterns in substitution, focusing
on, respectively, differences in manner, place and voice quality between the target consonant and
the realised consonant.


Manner of articulation. Plosives and nasals are the first segments acquired in word-initial
position cross-linguistically, while fricatives and liquids are acquired relatively late (e.g. Locke
1983, Bernhardt and Stemberger 1998).15 Fikkert (1998) presents different paths of consonantal
development in Dutch in terms of manner features, based on the CLPF corpus. These paths are
given in (2.14).


(2.14)            P------Ø -------N-------------a-------G
                                                b-------F
                                                c-------L



As is seen in (2.14), in word-initial position plosives (P) are the first acquired segments. The
child fills the onset with an epenthetic stop for V-initial words, until it has learned that onsets are
optional in Dutch. In the next stage children produce nasals (N). The order of acquisition of
glides (G), fricatives (F) and liquids (L) differs from child to child.16 In Table 2.8, the
development of Dutch singletons is given, based on a study carried out by Beers (1995) and
discussed in De Houwer and Gillis (1998). Beers collected data from 45 children between ages
1;3 and 4;0.17




15
   What is marked depends on position. Cross-linguistically, there is a difference in acquisition between word-initial
and word-final position. For example, fricatives are acquired relatively late in word-initial position, but early in
word-final position (De Houwer & Gillis 1998, Ingram et al., 1980). To mark the left syllable edge, one needs a
steep rise in sonority, whereas a less steep sonority fall is allowed in codas.
16
   Beers (1995) notes that in her database liquids are acquired last.
