Table 2.8         Order of development of consonants (Beers 1995)
Age group         Initial consonants
1;3-1;8           ptmnj
1;9-1;11          k
2;0-2;2           sxh
2;3-2;5           bfw
2;6-2;8           lr
2;9-2;11          d



There are no voiced fricatives and this is explained by the trend towards neutralization of voicing
in word-initial fricatives (see chapter 1). As seen in Table 2.8, the voiceless stops and the nasals
are the first acquired segments.18 Voiced stops, fricatives and liquids are acquired later.


We see that in the acquisition of consonants, voiceless stops are found before continuants.
Furthermore, although not apparent from Table 2.8, stops substitute for continuants. Deleting the
continuant feature of a particular consonant occurs more frequently than adding the continuant
feature to a consonant. In terms of unmarked features in onset position, we see that continuant
consonants are marked at an early stage and we therefore assume that [continuant] is a marked
feature in word-initial position.


Place of articulation. According to Levelt (1994), substitutions show that coronals and labials
are less marked than dorsals.19 Dorsals are often substituted by labials and coronals initially, and
thus labials and coronals occur more often in children’s speech (Fikkert 1998). With respect to
the order of acquisition, labials are acquired before coronals and dorsals (Fikkert 1998). Fikkert
does not discuss markedness of labials versus coronals. Cross-linguistically, coronals are
assumed to be the least marked segments (see for instance papers in Paradis and Prunet 1991).
This suggests they might be acquired early.




18
   Fikkert (1998) reports that the glide /j/ is reported as acquired early according to the mastery criterion because the
sound is correctly produced in the highly frequent word /ja/ “yes”.
19
   According to Beers (1995) dorsals are acquired earlier than coronals in final position.
                                                                                                               36


Voice quality. The literature on the acquisition of Dutch describes both devoicing and voicing as
processes occurring in child language (De Houwer and Gillis 1998).20 However, in terms of
acquisition, voiceless obstruents are acquired before voiced obstruents in Dutch. As seen from
Table 2.8, /p/ and /t/ are acquired before /b/ and /d/, and they may even substitute for /b/ and /d/.
De Houwer and Gillis report that this order is generally seen in the acquisition of Dutch (see also
Kuijpers 1993).


In summary, unmarked consonants, as measured by substitution in early acquisition, lack the
features sonorant, continuant and voice. Furthermore, they lack the feature dorsal. Unmarked and
marked features are given in (2.15).


(2.15)            Marked consonants          Unmarked consonants
                  [sonorant]                 no sonorant feature
                  [continuant]               no continuant feature
                  [dorsal]                   coronal or labial feature
                  [voiced]                   no voice feature



Having proposed which features are marked based on studies of the acquisition of Dutch, I now
turn to a discussion of the OT account of variation in reduction strategies. As discussed earlier, in
an OT based framework, markedness constraints militate against outputs with marked features.
Patterns in cluster reduction in which the more sonorous segment survives are accounted for by
the dominance of certain constraints that conflict with sonority constraints. In work by Pater and
Barlow (Barlow 1997, Pater and Barlow 2002) explicit reference is made to constraints that
require outputs to have unmarked structures regarding manner, place and voice quality.
Examples of such constraints are given in (2.16). The constraint in (2.16) above the horizontal
line is a faithfulness constraint, whereas the constraints below the line are markedness constraints
(Barlow 1997).




20
  In English acquisition, we see initial C voicing. However, initial voicing is more accurately described as
deaspiration (Bernhardt and Stemberger 1998).
                                                                                                            37


(2.16)    MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL]: A labial segment in the input must surface in the output (exact segment) (p.58)
          *[-SONORANT, +CONTINUANT]: Avoid fricatives (p. 51)
          *LIQUID: Avoid liquids (p. 57)
          *DORSALS: Avoid velars (p. 53)



In (2.17), optimal outputs of target /s/-sonorant clusters (/sm/, /sn/, /sl/) are given, as an effect of
MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL], *LIQUID , *[-SONORANT, +CONTINUANT] in a grammar where each of
these constraints outranks both MAX and *M/segment (see section 2.4.1 on these constraints). A
dash indicates that no evaluations are made.


(2.17)                              /sm/                     /sn/                     /sl/
MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL]                 [m]                      -                        -
*LIQUID                             -                        -                        [s]
*[-SONORANT, +CONTINUANT]           [m]                      [n]                      [l]



For target cluster /sm/: if the constraints MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL] and *COMPLEX-ONSET
dominate *M/segment and MAX, i.e. *COMPLEX-ONSET, MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL] >>
*M/SEGMENT >> MAX and if no other constraints are active, then this grammar would lead to the
output [m].21 Obviously, the constraint MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL] is not effective in evaluating
possible outputs of target clusters /sn/ and /sl/, since there is no labial in these targets. Different
rankings of constraints such as MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL] and *M/segment between grammars can
explain between-individual variation. The difference in the realisation of /sw/ between subject 24
and subject 25 in Barlow’s study (1997) for example, is explained by referring to the constraint
MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL] and its interaction with constraint *M/SEGMENT. Recall that child 25
realises /sw/ as [s] whereas child 24 realises /sw/ as [w]. In the grammar of child 25
*M/SEGMENT dominates MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL], resulting in a candidate with the lowest
sonority, i.e. [s]. In the grammar of child 24 MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL] dominates *M/SEGMENT
resulting in an optimal candidate where the input labial segment is realised, i.e. [w].
          So far, I have limited the discussion to possible outputs in which one of the target
consonants is realised (CaCb is realised as Ca, Cb). In order to account for reduction towards
possible outputs in which another consonant than the target consonants of a cluster is realised
                                                                                                              38


(CaCb is realised as Cc), Barlow (1997) refers to IDENT (identity) constraints in which a feature
of the segment rather than the segment itself should be parsed (2.18). Notice that this constraint
differs from MAX-SEGMENT [LABIAL]. IDENT[LABIAL] only requires the feature [labial] to be
present in the output, while MAX-SEGMENT [LABIAL] requires the (exact) labial segment from the
input to be parsed. In other words, IDENT constraints are constraints that require the maintenance
of particular input features in the output. MAX-SEGMENT is connected with segments; IDENT with
features (McCarthy and Prince 1995).


(2.18)   IDENT (p.51):
         If an input segment has the feature [F], then the corresponding output segment must also have the feature
         [F].



For example, if IDENT[LABIAL] is ranked high, then the feature [labial] will be kept in the output.
Referring to this constraint, a possible realisation [p] of target cluster /fl/ can be accounted for by
assuming a grammar with the constraint ranking given in (2.19).


(2.19)   *COMPLEX-ONSET, *[-SONORANT, +CONTINUANT], *LIQUID, IDENT-LABIAL >> *M/SEGMENT >> MAX



Pater and Barlow (2002) remark that a positive feature of OT is that constraints such as those
discussed above relate to patterns often seen in a child’s language. In other words, cluster
reduction and stopping (e.g. cases where fricatives are avoided) are both captured by the
constraints given above. In addition, they remark that their analysis is preferable to an analysis in
which head faithfulness plays a role (Goad and Rose’s analysis in section 2.3.2). According to
Pater and Barlow this is because head faithfulness only accounts for deletion of /s/, whereas their
analysis accounts for avoidance of all fricatives, and relates this avoidance to the process of
stopping. For example, the fact that the effect of the high ranked constraint *[-SONORANT,
+CONTINUANT] results in output [p], which is the optimal candidate for inputs such as /fl/ and /f/,
directly relates to the stopping process often seen in a child’s grammar at an early stage.
         In their paper, Pater and Barlow (2002) refer to the principle of factorial typology in OT
in which all rankings of constraints should be attested in possible languages. Along these lines,

21
  Output [p] would be ruled out by MAX-SEGMENT[LABIAL] since the constraint requires the exact labial segment,
rather than a segment with the feature labial to be parsed.
                                                                                                        39


they discuss the effect of the constraint *FRICATIVE in cluster reduction within different conflict
situations with sonority constraints. In Table 2.9, constraint rankings plus predicted outputs are
given, where G=glide, L=Liquid, N=Nasal, F=Fricative).


Table 2.9       Constraint *FRICATIVE in conflict situations with onset sonority constraints
                (Pater and Barlow 2002)
Constraint ranking                                                /sw/     /sl/    /sn/        /st/
A       *G-ONS >> *L-ONS >> *N-ONS >> *F-ONS >> *FRIC              s       s        s          t
B       *G-ONS >> *L-ONS >> *N-ONS >> *FRIC >> *F-ONS              s       s        s          t
C       *G-ONS >> *L-ONS >> *FRIC >> *N-ONS >> *F-ONS              s       s        n          t
D       *G-ONS >> *FRIC >> *L-ONS >> *N-ONS >> *F-ONS              s       l        n          t
E       *FRIC >> *G-ONS >> *L-ONS >> *N-ONS >> *F-ONS              w       l        n          t



Comparing /sl/ with /sn/ clusters we see that there are no instances where the former is realised
as [l] whereas the latter is realised as [n], which is in accordance with the prediction Goad and
Rose make (see section 2.4.2).
        Besides predictions about which realisations are possible in a grammar, Pater and Barlow
predict which realisations in cluster reduction are impossible, as is seen in Table 2.10.


Table 2.10      Patterns of cluster reduction predicted to be impossible according to Pater and Barlow
                (Pater and Barlow 2002)
        /sw/    /sl/    /sn/     /st/                     /sw/     /sl/    /sn/     /st/
a.      w       l       n        s               f.       s        l       n        s
b.      w       l       s        t               g.       w        s       s        s
c.      w       l       s        s               h.       s        l       s        s
d.      w       s       s        t               i.       s        s       n        s
e.      w       s       n        s



As is seen in the constraint ranking in Table 2.9, the predictions regarding one cluster have
consequences for the realisation patterns in other clusters. For example, Pater and Barlow predict
that if /st/ is realised as /s/, /s/ should be realised in all other sC clusters (/sw/, /sl/, /sn/). This is
clearly seen in Table 2.10, where patterns a., c., e., f., g., h., i. have a realisation of /s/ for /st/
clusters but not for all other clusters. Another implication seen in Pater and Barlow’s predictions
is that if /sw/ is realised as [w], then there should be no /s/ realised in the other clusters (/sl/, /sn/,
                                                                                                      40


/st/). Thus patterns a., b., c., d., e., g. are not predicted to occur. Pater and Barlow omit two
patterns that they predict not to occur. These patterns are given in Table 2.11:


Table 2.11     More patterns of cluster reduction predicted to be impossible according to Pater and Barlow
        /sw/   /sl/    /sn/    /st/
j.      w      s       n       t
k.      s      l       s       t



Although the authors remain cautious in claiming that some of the patterns might occur due to
the influence of other active constraints, I will discuss in chapter 4 whether indeed these patterns
are not occurring.
        In the next section, I will compare the predictions made by Goad and Rose on the one
hand and Pater and Barlow on the other.


2.4.4   Comparing predictions
The main difference between the theoretical account of Pater and Barlow on the one hand and
that of Goad and Rose on the other is that the former authors account for different reduction
patterns by different constraint rankings (in which some higher ranked constraints militate
against certain features in the output), while the latter authors account for different reduction
patterns by different prosodic representations in the input.
        In what follows, I will investigate whether patterns predicted to be impossible by Pater
and Barlow are also unpredicted by Goad and Rose. I will focus only on the effect of the
constraint that militates against fricatives. Recall the patterns in Table 2.12 that are predicted not
to occur.
                                                                                                      41


Table 2.12      Patterns of cluster reduction predicted to be impossible according to Pater and Barlow
                (adapted from Pater and Barlow 2002)
        /sw/    /sl/    /sn/    /st/                      /sw/   /sl/    /sn/    /st/
a.      w       l       n       s               g.        w      s       s       s
b.      w       l       s       t               h.        s      l       s       s
c.      w       l       s       s               i.        s      s       n       s
d.      w       s       s       t               j.        w      s       n       t
e.      w       s       n       s               k.        s      l       s       t
f.      s       l       n       s



Comparing the patterns given in Table 2.12 with the predictions Goad and Rose make (see Table
2.7 in section 2.4.2), we see many similarities. Patterns a., c., e., f., g., h., i. are not predicted to
occur by Goad and Rose since they predict that the obstruent is retained in /s/-obstruent clusters
regardless of whether children follow the sonority or head pattern. Focusing on patterns b., d., j.
and k., we see that Pattern b. and pattern k. are not predicted by Goad and Rose because of the
interrelationship between /sl/ and /sn/ clusters. Goad and Rose predict these clusters to be
realised as either [l] and [n], or as [s] and [n], but not as [l] and [s], since it is not likely that the
child will follow the head pattern in /sl/ clusters but the sonority pattern in /sn/ clusters. The child
could however follow the sonority pattern in /sl/ (/l/ is a perfect dependent) and the head pattern
in /sn/ clusters (/n/ is not a good dependent). Regarding pattern d. and pattern j., Goad and Rose
predict these patterns do not occur since it is not likely that a child will follow the head pattern
for /sw/ and the sonority pattern for /sl/ and/or /sn/.
        In summary, the patterns in sC clusters that Pater and Barlow predict not to occur are also
not predicted by Goad and Rose.


A major difference between Pater and Barlow as opposed to Goad and Rose is that Pater and
Barlow predict that a child might avoid fricatives in all clusters with a fricative, while Goad and
Rose predict that a child might avoid only the fricative /s/ in sC clusters. Pater and Barlow do not
list unpredicted patterns with fricatives other than /s/, but all predicted patterns become
unpredicted if there is different patterning in /sl/ and /fl/ or /xl/. In Table 2.13, I repeat the
predicted patterns of Table 2.12, adding predicted outputs for cluster /fl/.
                                                                                                        42


Table 2.13     Patterns of cluster reduction predicted to be possible (outputs for /fl/ are added)
        /sw/   /sl/    /fl/     /sn/     /st/
B’      s      s       f        s        t
C’      s      s       f        n        t
D’      s      l       l        n        t
E’      w      l       l        n        t



Goad and Rose predict that for clusters with a fricative other than /s/, the fricative is realised
since these clusters are normal branching onsets. This means that all patterns where either /xl/ or
/fl/ is realised as [l] are not predicted by Goad and Rose. Thus patterns D' and E' in Table 2.13
are not predicted to occur by Goad and Rose, while they are predicted to occur by Pater and
Barlow.
        All cases in which the patterning of /sl/ and /fl/ is not similar are unpredicted by Pater and
Barlow. Examples of unpredicted patterns are given in Table 2.14.


Table 2.14     Patterns of cluster reduction predicted to be impossible according to Pater and Barlow
        /sw/   /sl/    /fl/     /sn/     /st/
l.      s      s       l        s        t
m.      s      l       f        s        t
n.      s      s       l        n        t
o.      s      l       f        n        t
p.      w      l       f        n        t
q.      w      s       l        n        t



The difference with Goad and Rose is that they predict pattern o. to occur, as well as pattern p.


I now summarize the comparison between patterns predicted by Goad and Rose and patterns
predicted by Pater and Barlow. The main difference is that Goad and Rose single out /s/-sonorant
clusters from other fricative-sonorant clusters, while Pater and Barlow predict that fricative-
sonorant clusters pattern together as a class, all other things being equal. Both the hypotheses of
Goad and Rose and Pater and Barlow are remarkably similar in that by far the majority of
patterns are not predicted to occur by both sets of authors. There is however one exception,
which is the occurrence of [l] in /sl/ clusters and the occurrence of [x] and [f] in /xl/ and /fl/.
                                                                                                    43


Goad and Rose predict that this relationship occurs while Pater and Barlow predict that this
pattern is impossible due to the assumption that all or none of the fricative-liquid clusters are
realised as either [l] or as the fricative.
        In chapter 4, I will check which assumption is confirmed in the study.


2.5     Conclusion
In this chapter, I have discussed the literature on variation in cluster reduction, focusing on the
reported patterns of variation both within and between children and on theoretical explanations
for variation.
        Although cluster reduction in child language has been investigated in several languages,
variation patterns in reduction strategies have never been subject to close scrutiny. The data
indicate that in most clusters the less sonorous segment is realised but that variation is seen in /s/-
sonorant clusters. However, the trend in reduction patterns within the group of /s/-sonorant
clusters regarding between-individual and within-individual variation remains unclear. Studies
that focus on cluster reduction lack quantitative data of i) individual children and large groups of
children, and of ii) individual clusters. The first is needed in order to get a clear insight into child
between-individual and within-individual reduction patterns. The second is needed in order to
see whether there are patterning differences within groups of clusters.
        Analyzing Fikkert’s data (1994) in light of between-individual and within-individual
variation, it seems that clusters that have a plosive (e.g. PL, PN, PG, sP) are less variable in the
way they are reduced than clusters that have a fricative (e.g. FL, FN, FG, sF). Furthermore, it
seems that children are consistent in their reduction strategy during a certain period of time.
Third, over time there is more within-individual variation in reduction strategies for the fricative
clusters than there is for the plosive clusters.


Cluster reduction patterns are generally explained by making reference to sonority. Recent
theoretical explanations for unexpected patterns (i.e. non-sonority) in the reduction of these
clusters refer to other features (Barlow 1997, Bernardt and Stemberger 1998, Pater and Barlow
2002) or refer explicitly to differences in prosodic representations (Goad and Rose to appear).
Both accounts make some claims regarding possible and impossible patterns in reduction.
                                                                                                   44


       Goad and Rose claim that /s/-sonorant clusters are variable, whereas obstruent-sonorant
clusters and /s/-plosive clusters are not. Furthermore, they predict that in an /s/-sonorant cluster a
child may first realise [s] and then realise a cluster, or first realise [s] and then the sonorant but
not vice versa, because the sonority pattern, if it occurs, precedes the head pattern. They also
predict that /s/-sonorant clusters do not always pattern as a group. Cases in which a child realises
the nasal in /s/-nasal clusters, but [s] in /sl/ clusters are very common (but not vice versa). This is
explained by the fact a child has more evidence to syllabify /s/ in /sn/ clusters in the appendix
position rather than in the head position of an onset, since a nasal is not a good dependent. The
lateral, on the other hand, is a good dependent, which results in a syllabification in which /s/ is in
the head position.
       Pater and Barlow (2002) discuss the OT principle of factorial typology. The grammar
predicts the occurrence of certain patterns but it also predicts that certain patterns are impossible
in a grammar, seen in Table 2.12 and Table 2.13.
       Looking at individual clusters (rather than cluster groups) that occur in Dutch, in my
study I will examine both within-child and between-child variation as well as within-cluster and
between-cluster variation with the goal of obtaining more definitive answers to the questions
raised in this chapter. In the next chapter, I will discuss the methodology of the study.
                                                                                                45


Chapter 3 Method: Main study


3.1      Introduction
This chapter describes the methodology of the study on variation in the production of word-
initial consonant clusters. The main study was conducted in 2001 in the Netherlands. Prior to this
study, a pilot study was done in 1999. I will first discuss the findings of the pilot study and then
explain the changes that were made in the setup of the main study.
         The pilot study involved 11 Dutch L1 learners with a mean age of 2;10 years (for details
see Jongstra 2000, and Jongstra 2001a). These children were followed for about ten weeks. They
were tested with a picture-pointing test on a weekly basis on the production of 27 different word-
initial consonant clusters. Out of the 27 clusters, 24 clusters were CC targets and three were CCC
targets. The results of the pilot study showed that there is variation in the reduction patterns of
word-initial consonant clusters. Some clusters were always or almost always reduced in the same
way; others were more variable in their deletion pattern and some displayed random distribution
(see Appendix 3.1 for information on subjects and for the percentages in reduction strategy per
cluster). Furthermore, the study showed that a child was highly consistent in his/her deletion
strategy at a particular stage in acquisition.
         There were several aspects in the setup of the pilot study that were improved in the setup
of the main study, cf. Table 3.1.


Table 3.1         Differences between pilot study and the main study
                                  Pilot study                      Main study
Number of children                11                               34
Mean age of children              2;11                             2;3
Time span                         10 weeks                         21 weeks
Token words per cluster           5                                3
Target clusters                   incl. CCC                        excl. CCC
                                  incl. /vl/ and /vr/              excl. /vl/ and /vr/
                                  excl. /sk/                       incl. /sk/
                                                                                                      46


The most important aspects that were changed in the main study were the number of children and
their ages at the beginning of the study. In the pilot study 11 children were followed.22 Of these
11 children, only 7 reduced clusters; the others had already mastered most clusters. In order to
discuss between-individual variation patterns in reduction, data is needed from at least twice as
many children with lower starting ages. The mean age difference between the pilot study and the
main study turned out to be 8 months. Also, I planned to follow the children at least twice as
long as the 10 weeks that I followed the children in the pilot study. This was done with the
purpose of obtaining more developmental data. In terms of cluster reduction strategies, the main
study would enable me to look at whether children would possibly change their direction strategy
during development. Furthermore, fewer token words per cluster are used in the main study; only
63% were spontaneously produced data in the pilot study. This outcome was probably due to the
high number of words that the children did not know. Each cluster had five token words in the
pilot study. In order to increase the number of spontaneous data, only three token words per
cluster were used in the main study. It turns out that the percentage of spontaneous data is much
higher in the main study. Finally, I changed some target clusters that were used in the pilot study.
The C0C1C2 clusters were left out (e.g. /spr/, /str/, /sxr/), because they did not show different
results compared with the results of identical C1C2 clusters (e.g. /pr/, /tr/, /xr/). Furthermore, the
voiced initial labio-dental fricative clusters were left out (e.g. /vl/, /vr/), since they did not pattern
differently from their voiceless counterparts. This is probably due to the fact the initial voicing of
fricatives is neutralizing in the Netherlands, so that these clusters are phonologically the same as
clusters with voiceless initial labio-dental fricatives (see chapter 1). One cluster was added as a
target in the main study, i.e., /sk/, to have the full contrast of places of articulation in the /s/-
plosive clusters.
           Of the 11 children in the pilot study, some had many instances of cluster reduction. For
the purpose of addressing the issue of variation in cluster reduction, I recoded the database of the
pilot study children so that it became identical to the coding of the database of the main study
(see section 3 in this chapter) and in doing so I added the CC cluster data of these children to the
other cluster reduction data of the main study (see chapter 4).




22
     One child did not complete the full 10 weeks.
                                                                                                47


In the remainder of this chapter, I will discuss several relevant aspects in the setup of the main
study (2001), including subject information, target words, test procedure, testing schedule and
general considerations in the design of the main study. Then I will discuss how the data are
transcribed. Third, I explain how the database was set up. For the methodology used in the
perception study, I refer to chapter 6.


3.2     Procedures
3.2.1   Subjects
In the production study 34 children (15 boys and 19 girls) from 1;11 to 2;10 years old at the start
of the study participated. The mean starting age is 2;3 years. The children were located in six day
care centres in Groningen, a city in the north of the Netherlands.
        The children were selected according to linguistic and medical criteria. The medical
criteria required normal development, i.e. no background of cognitive, behavioural, hearing or
physical impairment compared to other children of the same age. I asked the parents whether
their child had problems with hearing or had other health problems. I also asked the day care
staff if they knew whether the child had possible health problems. All 34 children participating in
the study were reported by both parents and staff as being healthy. The linguistic criteria required
that i) the subject’s native language be Dutch, ii) the child be raised in a monolingual
environment for the majority of the time, iii) the child be around 2 years old at the beginning; iv)
the child behave more or less according to his/her age with respect to vocabulary size, and have
in general segmentally accurate forms. Last, but most important: the children had to be willing to
participate in a picture naming task, and the children’s parent(s) had to give their consent for
their child to participate in the study.
        I followed the same procedure in all day care centres: I first spent a long time playing
with all the children in a group and participating as if I were a staff member (helping the children
during snack and lunch time, following the children with the staff during the whole day, talking
to the parents in the morning and evening, etc.). By doing this, I soon became familiar to parents,
staff, and most importantly, to all the children. Then I searched for children who could
participate in the study. These children had to meet the medical and linguistic criteria discussed
above. I based my impression of whether a child was suitable to participate on the child’s overall
performance: the child liked reading a picture book, had enough attention to read quite a few
                                                                                                     48


pages of the book, and produced more or less recognizable words. Children who appeared to be
suitable subjects were then taken to a separate quiet room where the test was carried out (see
section 3.2.3).


Subject information is given in Table 3.2. From left to right the columns indicate: (1) the child’s
number in the study (correlated with ascending age); (2) the child’s sex; (3) the child’s sibling
position (for example, “2” indicates that the child is the second child in the family); (4) the
child’s age at the first and at the last session; (5) the number of weeks that the child was
followed; (6) the number of sessions the child went through; (8) whether the child participated in
the perception study (see chapter 6).

3.2.2    Targets
The target clusters that I tested for in the production study are given in Table 3.3.
                                                                                                                  49


Table 3.3         Target clusters in main study
1 /sp/            7 /sl/            13 /dr/           19 /xl/
2 /st/            8 /pl/            14 /kl/           20 /xr/
3 /sk/            9 /pr/            15 /kr/           21 /tw/
4 /sx/            10 /bl/           16 /kn/           22 /kw/
5 /sm/            11 /br/           17 /fl/           23 /sw/
6 /sn/            12 /tr/           18 /fr/



Comparing the inventory of clusters in adult Dutch (see chapter 1) with the clusters that were
tested in the study, it is obvious that not all clusters in adult Dutch were tested: i) infrequent
clusters (except for /sk/) were omitted, since the children were unlikely to produce words that
have these clusters.23; ii) only CC clusters were tested; iii) only the voiceless fricative-liquid
clusters /fr/, /fl/, /xl/ /xr/ were tested and the voiced fricative-liquid clusters were ignored since
many speakers neutralize the voice contrast in fricatives in word-initial position (see chapter 1).
         Most targets are monosyllabic words with simple codas, with some exceptions involving
disyllabic words with simple codas and initial stress.24 Most of the target clusters have three
tokens. Thus, the target /sp/ has three words with an initial /sp/ cluster (/sp/ook ghost, /sp/in
spider, /sp/iegel mirror). In cases where it was difficult to come up with three familiar words
starting with the same cluster, some words were re-used. For example, while /sp/ has three
different words, cluster /WÙ/ has only one word, re-used twice. The target words with the English
gloss and syntactic category are given in Table 3.4.

words occurred in the stressed position of the syllable. In the only trisyllabic word (/sk/ippiebal skippy ball), the

Although most words are well chosen targets in that they are easily recognizable in pictures, two
target clusters are less attractive: /kw/ and /tw/. These clusters occur only in a few words in adult
Dutch. From this limited list of adult words, /kw/ak quack and /tw/ee two are the only two words
that are i) well known by children, ii) meet the structural criteria (simple codas). However, these
words differ from the other target words in the following ways: /kw/ak is onomatopoeic (it is the
sound of a duck). It therefore can be rather unstable as a lexical representation. The other
problematic word, /tw/ee is a counting word. It is possible that a child who does not yet have a
clear idea of numbers and numbering, has no separate lexical representation for this word, but
rather imitates a string of counting words, such as /entwedri/ one, two, three.26 Although the

26
     Generally, children do not understand the concept of “two” until 35-40 months (Owens 1996).
                                                                                                52


words /kw/ak and /tw/ee are problematic, they are indeed real words that do appear in the child’s
language. Since the aim of the study is to investigate real language rather than imitations of
nonsense structures, I chose to test real words instead of nonsense words that start with these
clusters. However, we have to keep in mind that /kw/ and /tw/ are not ideal target words.


3.2.3   Procedure
Each child was tested individually in a separate quiet room. A session lasted about ten minutes,
depending on how fast the child went through the pictures. The data were collected by elicitation
in a picture-naming task. Each word was shown as a picture on one page. The pictures were
designed to be attractive to children: they were simple and very colourful with thick black
frames. They were presented in a lively though limited context in order to both please the
imagination and keep the child’s attention on the word to be elicited. All pictures were in A-4
format, put in a plastic sheet cover. The set of pictures was divided into two, a red file book and
a blue file book. In each book, there were approximately 34 pictures that the child was expected
to name. In order to complete one session, the child had to go through both books. The pictures
were randomly ordered and the order within and between the books was changed many times
during the study. Children usually worked through one file book within one recording, with the
other file book occasionally introduced later in the day or in the week, preferably the next day.
Sometimes, if the child wanted, children went through both file books in one setting (thus
through one full session) with a short break between the books.
        Children were asked to name what they saw. If the child did not respond, an introduction
was given, referring to elements in the picture that are related to the elicited word. If the child
produced the word, it was reported as “spontaneous”. If the child still did not name the picture,
the child was told what the picture was and the child was asked to repeat the word, preferably in
an indirect question ("So what is it that you see?"). Directly and indirectly imitated realisations
were reported as “non-spontaneous”. Apart from the spontaneous versus imitation condition, one
can also distinguish the initial versus non-initial condition. Some studies have shown that
children have significantly different results within this condition, especially in cluster reduction
(see Mc Leod, Hand, Rosenthal and Hayes 1994). In order to account for possible differences in
realisations due to influences of adjacent words, the data were reported as either "sentence
initial" or "not sentence initial".
                                                                                                    53


        All sessions were tape-recorded on a Sony TC-150 recorder.


3.2.4   Testing schedule
Children were tested approximately once per a three week period. As shown in Table 3.2, the
number of sessions and the number of weeks that the children were followed differed due to
different circumstances. Most children were followed for about five months with at least eight
sessions, with exceptions of children with more or fewer sessions and a longer or shorter time
span. See Appendix 3.2 for a weekly overview of recordings per child (time span in sessions). In
one session all words are tested (i.e., all three examples of each of the 23 clusters). In other
words, each session, in the ideal situation, contained 69 target words for each child.


3.2.5   Considerations regarding the design of the study
This experiment falls somewhere between longitudinal and cross-sectional, and somewhere
between naturalistic and experimental. The study is designed to focus on individual as well as
group variation in realisations of clusters over a time span. In order to get fruitful insight into
how a child deals with clusters, one needs quantitative data for each individual child. In order to
look at between-individual variation, one needs data from quite a large group of children, more
than 30. In order to look at within-individual variation across time, one needs the quantitative
data for a long time span, for as long as the child continues to change the production of clusters.
This developmental aspect was included in order to investigate the order in which clusters
appear. In this way, the setup of the study is longitudinal. Cross-sectionally, there is a time span
of about 1.5 years (i.e., from the start age of the youngest child 1;11 until the end age of the
oldest child, which is 3;4).
        Although the study can be characterized as experimental, in which the realisation of
clusters is systematically followed, there are also naturalistic aspects. First, reading a book, with
the child on the investigator’s lap or on a chair next to the investigator, is a familiar activity for a
child in a day care centre. Furthermore, there were spontaneous conversations between the child
and the investigator before, during and after the activity (all tape-recorded) with the intention of
distracting the child from focussing too much on the target words. Furthermore, the words were
chosen to keep the activity as natural as possible, with a preference for fewer words (just three
tokens per cluster) but familiar tokens: only words that were likely to be known by the child
                                                                                                  54


were included in the study. Also, direct imitation was avoided as much as possible (sometimes
resulting in empty cells) with the purpose of limiting the amount of unnatural conversation,
though to follow as much as possible the child without losing the main goal of the study.
       Since spontaneous conversations between child and investigator were a planned part of
the study, the production of singletons was not systematically tested. The rationale for this is that
words with singletons would be produced anyway, since there were enough word items in the
pictures (as background) that had singletons. For example, the picture of the target word
<bl>okken blocks, displays four coloured blocks: yellow, blue, red and green. I asked for the
colours as well, without forcing the child to imitate me if she/he did not respond to it. Also,
children spontaneously used words that have singletons in initial position. It turned out that in
most cases there are up to three examples of a particular word-initial target singleton, spoken per
child per session. Avoiding testing singletons systematically leaves more room for testing as
many cluster words as possible.


3.3    Transcription
All the child data were phonetically transcribed on paper by the author. The transcription was
done in a fairly broad way in order to reduce the difficulties in phonemically categorizing the
child’s idiosyncratic pronunciation of sounds, though without the loss of attention to subtle
differences in consonant cluster quality. An independent transcription check was done by a
monolingual Dutch speaking student who was well trained in doing transcriptions. Seven percent
of the data were cross-checked. In particular, the transcriptions of half a session for each child
were checked. Since the analysis will be based on a phonemic level, a phonemic comparison was
done. For example, it did not matter exactly what the articulatory and acoustic properties of a
voiceless coronal sibilant were, as long as both transcribers characterized it as /s/ rather than as
another phoneme. The consistency rate between the two transcriptions, focusing on the reduction
data, turned out to be 93.2%. Almost half of the inconsistencies involved a difference in voice in
either /b/ versus /p/ or /d/ versus /t/. In chapter 4, I will show that whether an output is voiced or
voiceless does not have an effect on the reduction strategy.
                                                                                                             55

All target words of each cluster were numbered 1-3 (see Table 3.4). Thus, for example, the first

cluster /sp/, subtype 2. All other words that were not targets but start with the same cluster were
put under number 4, as a miscellaneous category, except for words with more than three
syllables and main stress on a syllable other than the one starting with the cluster relevant to the

           Each variable is put in a column in excel. The data were then coded according to type of
process, given in Table 3.6 (C1, C2 refer to consonant 1 and to consonant 2, respectively in the
target cluster).

     Incidental cases of target words with three word initial consonants are not included in the database.

Table 3.6          Codes used to indicate processes 28
Nr       Process                             Nr        Process
0        C1 and C2 deletion                  5         CC other realisation
1        C1 realisation                      6         Epenthesis in C1C2

Process 0 indicates that the child has no onset.29 Process 1 indicates all cases where the child
realises the first consonant of the target cluster. Process 2 is related to process 1, in that in this
process the child realises only the second consonant from the target cluster. Process 3 indicates
that there is one consonant realised but this consonant is not in the target. For example, target
word /dr/inken drink, realised as [l]inken, is labelled process 3, and the [l] is added in the output
information column. Process 4 labels all target realisations (thus, adult realisations). Process 5
indicates that the child has two consonants, but not the target ones. One or both consonants are
different from the target cluster. For example, the target word /sn/oep candy realised as [sl]oep is
labeled as process 5, and [sl] is added as output information. Process 6 indicates epenthesis
between the two target consonants. Process 7 labels the realisations in which the child has more
than two consonants, whatever the consonants are. For example, /sx/aap sheep, realised as
[sxl]aap is labeled as process 7 and [sxl] is added as output information. Process 8 indicates
cases of epenthesis in which a vowel is inserted between the two consonants, where one or both
of the consonants are not part of the target cluster. For example, the target word /tr/ein train
realised as [t?l]ein is labeled as process 8, and the realisation [t?l] is added as output information.
Finally, process 9 indicates all cases in which there is a pause within the cluster, or epenthesis
before the cluster. For example /sp/ook ghost, realised as [s/p]ook, where / indicates a pause in
between [s] and [p], is labeled as process 9, as is realisation [?VS]ook for /sp/ook. In all process 9

cases, output information is added. Special output information was only added for the
unpredictable cases. An example of one page of data in Excel format is given in Appendix 3.3.



28
   Cases where there is a transitional schwa leading into the second consonant are not considered to be epenthetic.
These cases are analyzed as target realization (process 4), or as CC other (process 5), or as extra complex clusters
(process 7).
29
   Cases where a clear glottal stop was heard were not labeled as process 0, but as process 3.
                                                                                                     57


        The following data were left out of the database: First, all data in which it was not clear
what the child referred to. Second, repetitions of words within the same phrase. Third, all words
that were not sentence-initial and that accidentally started with the same consonant as the final
consonant of a preceding word in the sentence. For example, a realisation such as: [GnWÖ8§DS]

for /GnWÖ88§DS/ that is sheep, is left out since it is not clear if the [s] belongs to the verb [Ö8] or

to the noun [8§DS]. Fourth, I excluded all words that were sung instead of spoken. All other
word-initial cluster data were included in the database, regardless of whether the words were
target words of the study or not.
        The next chapter presents the results of the study.

Chapter 4 The results

4.1      Introduction
In this chapter, I present the patterns of variation in reduction strategies between and within
children and between and within individual consonant clusters. During the study, described in
chapter 3, 75 hours of conversation between child and investigator were collected. From this
corpus, the words that were spoken by the child and that have word-initial CC clusters were put
into an Excel database. This database has over 23.000 pieces of data with word-initial CC
clusters. The distribution under three conditions i) target word versus non-target word, ii)
spontaneous versus non-spontaneous, and iii) sentence-initial versus sentence medial is given
below.


                                   CC child language database: 23,167 data
         i                Target                                              Non-target
                          84.4%                                               15.6%
         ii     spontaneous        imitation               spontaneous                imitation
                71.1%              13.3%                   13.9%                      1.6%
         iii    initial   medial initial   medial          initial   medial           initial   medial
                52.3% 18.8% 11.5% 1.8%                     9.2%      4.7%             1.4%      0.2%


As can be seen, 52.3% of the entire cluster data occurs in the ideal situation: in only target
words, spontaneous and sentence-initial (see chapter 3 for discussion of these conditions).
However, we would lose almost half the database if we were to analyze only the data in this
perfect condition. In this chapter, I begin by examining whether it is possible to make use of a
larger portion of the database. I do this by systematically taking each of the three factors
identified into account to see if there are any significant differences in the data depending on
these conditions. In presenting the data in this step-by-step fashion, I am able to show that the
various factors that might affect the conclusions, are in fact not, over all, significant.
         The first cut to make is the distinction between target and non-target words. As is seen
above, 84.4% of the data are target words (N=19,564). Since the experimental method of the
study involves target clusters and cluster tokens, in order to control for possible lexical effects on
the child's production, I will first base the analysis on variation in production on only the target
                                                                                                                   59


words that were realised in the perfect conditions: spontaneous and initial. The number of target
words per child per cluster is given in Appendix 4.1.


4.1.1    Target words; description
The distribution of the target words in the spontaneous vs. imitation and sentence-initial versus
sentence medial condition is given below.


                                              Target words: 19,564 data
         1                   spontaneous                                              imitation
                             84.2%                                                    15.8%
         2         initial           medial                             initial               medial
                   62.0%             22.2%                              13.6%                 2.1%




As is seen above, 84.2% of the target word corpus consists of spontaneous data. Furthermore,
75.6% is sentence-initial data. More than half of the data are both spontaneous and sentence-
initial (62.0%). The distribution of data in the two conditions per child, across processes, is given
in Appendix 4.2a. The distribution of data in the two conditions per cluster, across processes is
given in Appendix 4.2b. I will consider per process the effect of the conditions in production, in
order to know which part of the data to analyze.


The distribution of the nine processes for realising a cluster, described in chapter 3, is given in
Table 4.1, focusing on a. spontaneous and initial target word data, and on b. all target word data,
regardless of whether they are spontaneous or imitation. (See section 4.1.3 for a definition of
process 4’’).

                                                                                                  60


As can be seen in Table 4.1, overall percentages within a certain process between data sets are
highly similar. For example, 21.83% of the database that consists of spontaneous and initial data
are realised as C1 (i.e., process 1). 23.80% of the database that consists of all target words are
realised as C1. Thus there are slightly more data in process 1 when conditions are collapsed.
        The concern at this point is not so much comparing percentages between a. (spontaneous
and initial words) and b. (all target words) (that are highly similar), but rather to see which
processes occur frequently and which less frequently in the database. Focusing on all target data
(b.), we see that the processes that occur most frequently are C1 realisation (23.8%), C2
realisation (8.8%), Cother realisation (7.4%), C1C2 realisation (46.1%) and CCother realisation
(10.8%) (See McLeod, Van Doorn and Reed 2001b for an overview of trends in acquiring
consonant clusters by two year olds). Less frequent processes are C1C2 deletion (0.1%), C1\C2
(i.e., epenthesis between C1 and C2: 0.8%), CCC realisation (1.0%), C\Cother (i.e., epenthesis
between C1 and Cother: 0.8%), and C/C realisation (i.e., pause between C1 and C2: 0.5%). As
discussed in chapter 1, I will limit my discussion to variation in reduction strategies, thus looking
at cases where one of the two target consonants or another single consonant is realised (see
section 4.1.4 for further discussion). In section 4.1.3, I will briefly discuss differences between
clusters regarding scores in correct realisation (CaCb realisation) in order to show which clusters
have more and which have less data in reduction processes (due to respectively less and more
data in correct realisation). 40.1% of the non-target database (over 7800 cases) involves data in
which the cluster is reduced to either a null onset (0.1%), or to one of the target consonants
(32.5%) or to another consonant (7.5%). I leave aside the very few cases (22) in which the whole
cluster is reduced (e.g. null onsets, i.e., process 0).


4.1.2   Singletons
As discussed in chapter 3, singletons were not systematically tested for, but in most cases there
are up to three examples of a particular word-initial target singleton, spoken per child per
session. We have to be careful though, since the numbers of target singletons per session might
be low for some children and because the token words per singleton might sometimes be limited.
However, since in general substitutions are persistent over eight sessions, we can make claims
regarding which singletons are difficult to produce for a child. The overview of persistent
                                                                                                    61


substitutions of singleton production per child, together with a detailed overview of outputs of
target word realisations per child per cluster per session are given in Appendix 4.3.
       Almost every child has problems producing the singleton /r/. Children substitute /r/ with
either/or [l], [h] or [j]. Furthermore, we see that some children have problems with fricatives,
especially /f/ and /x/. Finally, some children have problems with /l/ and/or /w/, and substitute
these sounds with [j]. Plosives and nasals are produced correctly.
       Before I start with the discussion of reduction strategies, I will first briefly discuss results
for correct realisation in the next section, since results in correct realisation have an effect on the
amount of data in reduction processes.


4.1.3 Correct realisation
In this section, I look at percentages of correct realisation. Although this thesis is concerned with
reduction strategies rather than correct realisation, an overview of how well some clusters are
realised would be desirable since clusters that are generally realised correctly have fewer
examples of reduction. In other words, percentages in processes are dependent rather than
independent.
       A crucial question is how to define correct score. The most straightforward is process 4,
in which both of the target consonants are realised. However, some children have difficulties
with some singletons, especially with singleton /r/. In order to abstract away from articulatory
difficulties that may lead to some realisations falling into category 5 rather than category 4, we
have to adjust the coding in category 5 (recall that category 5 represents data in which a cluster is
realised which is not the target cluster). There are two ways to go about this: changing the coding
in a stricter way (I will call this process 4'), or changing it in a more liberal way (I will call this
process 4''). The first (the stricter one, process 4’) considers all and only substitutions of a
singleton produced in a cluster as correct. For example, if a child says [l] for /r/, a realisation of
[pl] for /pr/ would be phonologically correct. Coding correct realisation in the more liberal way
(process 4’’) is the same as the stricter way, except that all possible realisations of the incorrect
singleton with the same sonority value count as correct. In this way, a realisation of [pj] for /pr/
would also be counted as correct, since the child cannot produce an [r] and will be likely to
substitute this consonant with another sonorant. Also, a realisation of [tl] for /sl/ would be correct
for a child who does not produce singleton /s/ correctly. Also [tl] for /fr/ would be correct for a
                                                                                                                    62


child who does not produce singleton /f/ and /r/ correctly. The three different ways of coding are
summarized in (4.1).

(4.1)   Recoding process 4
        4         Realising both target consonants.
        4'        Realising both targets or realising a cluster with one or two consonants, identically to substitution
                  of singleton.
        4''       Realising both targets or realising a cluster with one or two consonants, within the same sonority
                  value as the substitution of the singleton.


Coding in the more liberal (process 4’’) way is –in my view- preferable, as it also includes the
realisations of [pl] for /pr/ for a child that uses [h] for [r]. A realisation [ph] would be marked
both phonologically in Dutch (e.g. /h/ does not occur in clusters in Dutch) and cross-
linguistically. Since I am concerned with phonologically difficult clusters, I will from now on
consider correct realisation as I defined above under process 4''.
        Recall from Table 4.1 that almost half of the target data is realised correctly. This means
that clusters which are realised correctly have fewer instances of reduction processes (process 0,
1, 2 or 3). Which clusters have high scores in correct realisation? Table 4.2 gives the order in
percentages of correct realisation in process 4’’ (for only the spontaneous and initial data).


Table 4.2         Order in correct score (process 4'') (only spontaneous and initial data) N=5562
CC            N               4’’             CC                N          4’’
kn            111             17.51           br                281        46.99
sx            144             21.05           tr                226        47.08
sn            155             25.58           kw                251        49.90
dr            164             32.28           pr                316        54.77
sw            168             33.94           st                293        55.81
sk            136             36.07           kr                190        58.46
fr            157             38.67           fl                306        61.20
tw            210             38.82           sl                337        62.29
sp            212             42.15           bl                403        62.77
sm            203             43.10           pl                223        73.60
xl            256             45.39           kl                546        73.68
xr            274             45.44           Total             5562       45.86
                                                                                                     63


As is seen in Table 4.2, clusters with higher correct scores are /kl/, /pl/, /bl/, /sl/, while clusters
with lower correct scores are /kn/, /sx/, /sn/, /dr/. This means that a cluster like /kn/ will be likely
to have more data in reduction processes rather than in processes where a cluster is realised
(process 5 or process 4’’), and clusters such as /kl/ will have less data to analyze in reduction
processes.
        It is interesting to see whether there is a correlation between frequency of clusters and the
percentage of correct realisation (see chapter 1 for frequency percentages in Childes and Celex).
Comparing the percentages of correct realisation (process 4’’) with percentages of frequency in
Childes and Celex, it turns out that there is no correlation between the frequency of correct
realisation (process 4'') and frequency of target words in Celex (R(23) = .090, p= .684), nor is
there a correlation between frequency of correct realisation and frequency of target words in
Childes (R(23) = .173, p= .430). These results indicate that cluster frequency does not determine
the distinction between easy and difficult clusters. In other words, frequently occurring clusters
are not necessarily clusters that are easier to realise. As discussed before, I will not go into detail
in developmental issues related to the process of correct realisation. I thus leave aside discussion
of why some clusters are easier than others.
        In summary, in Table 4.2 I showed that some clusters have higher scores in correct
realisation (resulting in less data in reduction processes) and other clusters have lower scores in
correct realisation (resulting in higher scores in reduction processes).
        In the next section, I discuss different definitions of cluster reduction.


4.1.4   Definitions of cluster reduction
There are several ways in which the term “cluster reduction” could be defined. The three
definitions that I consider are summarized in (4.2). In a strict definition of cluster reduction, only
one of the target consonants is realised (the definition is given in 4.2a, where the numbers 1, 2
refer to process one and two, respectively). In this definition, a realisation such as [b] for /bl/ is
counted as C1 realisation, while a realisation such as [p] for /bl/ is listed under process three. In a
less strict definition of cluster reduction, the realised consonant forms a natural class in some
way with one of the input consonants. For example, in one definition, voice specification is not
relevant in the analysis (see 4.2b, where “Natvoice” means that realised consonant and input
consonant form a natural class with respect to voice specification). In this definition, both [p] and
                                                                                                        64


[b] for input /bl/ count as C1 realisation. In another possible definition, only sonority value is
relevant (see 4.2c where Natson means that the consonant realised and the input consonant form
a natural class with respect to sonority value). Here, a realisation such as [p] or [t] for /bl/ is
counted as an instance of C1 realisation, in which the obstruent is realised.


(4.2)   Relevant definitions of cluster reduction
(a)     Process. 1,2:    Realised consonant must be identical to one of the input consonants
(b)     Natvoice:        Same as process one and two, disregarding voice specification
(c)     Natson:          Realised consonant must have same sonority value as input consonant


I base the analysis on the strict definition of cluster reduction, in which only one of the target
consonants is realised (e.g. C1C2 is realised as C1 or C2). In this definition, a realisation such as
[b] for /bl/ is counted as C1 realisation, while a realisation such as [p] for /bl/ is listed under
process 3 (e.g. C1C2 is realised as Cother). In chapter 5 (section 5.3), I discuss whether and how
results differ if I adopt a less strict definition of cluster reduction, such as Natvoice and Natson.


The outline of the remainder of this chapter is as follows. In section 4.2, I focus only on target
data, first only on spontaneous and initial target data (section 4.2.1), and later on all target data
(section 4.2.2). Within a certain focus, I first present the results on variable and stable clusters
across the data and later present results focusing on between-child and within-child variation in
cases with at least eight reduction data per cluster per child. Then, in section 4.3, I compare the
results, adding more data from the entire main study and the pilot study. In section 4.4, I
compare patterns in reduction strategies between clusters per child. In section 4.5, I summarize
the results on variation in reduction strategies.


4.2     Results: Target words
In this section, I present the results focusing only on target data. I will first base the analysis on
only the spontaneous and initial target data (section 4.2.1). In section (4.2.2), I will increase the
size of the database, adding target word data that were coded as imitation and/or as occurring in
medial position (the effect of conditions is discussed in section (4.2.2.1)).
                                                                                                     65




4.2.1     Results: Spontaneous and initial target data
I start with a discussion of variation focusing on the target words that were realised
spontaneously and in initial position. I will first discuss variable and stable clusters over all
(section 4.2.1.1). In section (4.2.1.2), I will discuss between-child and within-child variation.


4.2.1.1 Variable and stable clusters: Overall data
A major question addressed in this thesis is which clusters are variable in their deletion strategy
(i.e., both C1 deletion and C2 deletion occur for a given cluster), and which clusters are more
stable (i.e., either C1 is realised almost all the time or C2 is realised almost all the time). First, I
consider the deletion patterns for different clusters across children. In Table 4.3, the sum of C1
and C2 realisations and the percentages of, respectively, C1 and C2 realisations per cluster across
children (out of the sum of C1 and C2 realisations), of target data in the spontaneous and initial
condition are given.

(out of the total of processes C1,C2) (only spontaneous and initial target data)

As can be seen in Table 4.3, for the cluster /sp/ there are 230 reduction cases (i.e., cases in which
either C1 or C2 is realised). Out of this total of C1,C2 reduction cases, 4.35% are realised as C1
and 95.65% are realised as C2. Notice that the number of reduction cases differs per cluster. For
                                                                                                             66


example, while cluster /sx/ has 423 reduction cases, cluster /pl/ has 69 reduction cases. This is
because cluster /pl/ is more often realised correctly while cluster /sx/ is not (see section 4.1.3).
        Deletion strategies vary per cluster in that some clusters have high percentages in either
one of the columns while others have a more even distribution. For example, cluster /tr/ is
realised as either [t] or [r] 158 times. The cluster is realised as [t] in 100%, and as [r] in 0% of all
C1 and C2 realisations. If we order the C1 and C2 realisation in terms of highest percentage in
either C1 or C2 in 6 different percentage groups, regardless of whether the deletion strategy
retains either the first or the second consonant, then the following pattern is seen, given in 4.3
(across data and across children).


(4.3)   1       51-60     /sm/, /kw/                                                      unstable pattern
        2       61-70     /sx/
        3       71-80     /st/, /kn/
        4       81-90     /tw/, /fl/, /xl/, /sn/, /kl/, /bl/, /sl/
        5       91-100 /sw/, /sk/, /fr/, /xr/, /sp/, /dr/, /pl/, /kr/, /pr/, /br/, /tr/   stable pattern



As seen in (4.3), most clusters are grouped in category five, i.e. where the percentage of a
particular deletion strategy is at least 91. These clusters are almost always deleted towards the
less sonorous segment and therefore they follow the main predictions in the literature for cluster
reduction (see chapter 2).
        So far, we have seen that there are differences across clusters with respect to between-
child variation in reduction strategies. Some clusters are always reduced in one particular
direction (either to C1 or C2), while other clusters are reduced in more variable ways with
examples of both C1 and C2 realisations. Notice, however, that the percentages given above in
(4.3) represent collapsed data (i.e., across children). A crucial question at this point is how the
individual child deals with clusters. I will discuss this issue in the next section.


4.2.1.2 Between-child and within-child variation
In this section, I look at deletion strategies for individual children, restricting discussion to C1
and C2 realisations. Several issues require attention. The first issue to consider is what would be
a sufficient number of instances of reduction per child for each cluster to analyze variation in
strategies. Recall that not all children have the same amount of reduction data. Although all
                                                                                                   67


children reduce some clusters during the recordings, not all children reduce all target clusters.
Some -more advanced- children were able to realise some clusters correctly; for such a child and
on such a cluster there are few or no reduction data available. The number of observations per
child for each cluster for realising C1 and/or C2 is given in Appendix 4.4a. No reduction is
indicated by blank cells. If there are reduction data available, then a sufficient amount of data is
needed in order to arrive at an analysis about consistency. The term consistent will be used when
at least 91% of the deletion strategy falls in one direction (either to C1 or to C2). For example, if
a child reduces cluster /pl/ one time as [p] and one time as [l], and then realises the target cluster
[pl] in the remainder of the study, then it would not be reasonable to assume that this child has
50% C1 realisations and 50% C2 realisations and thus that consistency is 50%. I decided that at
least eight instances of reduction per child per cluster would be the required number to perform
analyses (the 8 criterion). Thus, all situations where there are fewer than eight instances of
reduction per cluster are not considered. The percentages of C1 or C2 realisations per child for
each cluster are given in Appendix 4.4b. The cases that are omitted are indicated in the appendix
by grey shading. There are 192 cases left to analyze (to clarify: “one case” represents the child’s
reduction strategy for a cluster in which there are at least eight instances of reduction to either C1
or C2). Table 4.4 gives an overview of the realisation patterns seen in these 192 cases. There are
five columns. The second and third columns indicate consistent realisation of either C1 (first
column) or C2 (second column). For example, a child who has 5% C1 and 95% C2 realisations is
consistent in realising C2. A child who has 100% C1 realisations and 0% C2 realisations is
consistent in realising C1. The third column indicates within-child variation in realisation
patterns (i.e. C1/C2 realisation). The term within-child variation will be used when a child
reduces a cluster to either C1 or C2 and when the percentages of C1 and C2 fall between 10%
and 90%. For example, a child who realises 30% C1 and 70% C2 for a given cluster is counted
as variable for this cluster, as is a child who has 90% C1 and 10% C2. The fifth column indicates
the number of children included in the group. These children reduce a cluster towards C1 and/or
C2 at least eight times.
                                                                                                              68


Table 4.4        Number of consistent C1, C2 or variable C1/C2 realisation strategies (N), out of the total of
                 cases analyzed (each case meets the 8 criterion) (spontaneous + initial target data: 192 cases)
Cluster     C1       C2         C1/C2      N          Cluster    C1          C2         C1/C2      N
pr          13       0          0          13         sm         2           0          2          4
tr          12       0          0          12         fl         2           0          0          2
br          11       0          0          11         sp         0           13         0          13
dr          10       0          1          11         sk         0           7          2          9
xr          8        0          1          9          sn         13          1          1          15
kl          7        0          1          8          st         1           10         3          14
sw          6        0          1          7          kn         6           1          2          9
kr          4        0          0          4          tw         4           1          1          6
fr          4        0          0          4          kw         3           1          0          4
sl          3        0          0          3          sx         7           12         4          23
pl          3        0          0          3          Total      125         46         21         192
bl          3        0          1          4          %          65.1        24.0       10.9
xl          3        0          1          4



Table 4.4 provides a lot of information. First we see that 89% of all 192 cases that were analyzed
are consistently reduced to either the first consonant (65.1%) or to the second consonant
(24.0%). Recall that a case refers to a reduction strategy in which there are at least eight
instances of reduction. Only 10.9% involve cases with within-child variation. In other words, in
by far the majority of cases the individual child is consistent in his/her reduction strategy.
          Second, focusing on within-child variation (column four), some clusters do not have
cases where the individual child has a variable realisation pattern (/pr/, /tr/, /br/, /kr/, /fr/, /sl/, /pl/,
/fl/, /sp/, /kw/). These clusters are consistently reduced to only one direction, either C1 or C2,
without any cases of within-child variation. In the clusters /pr/, /tr/, /br/, /kr/, /fr/, /sl/, /pl/, /fl/,
/sp/ it is always the less sonorous segment that is realised, without any cases of within-child
variation. Other clusters show more cases of within-child variation. Cluster /st/ for example, is
reduced to either [s] or [t] by three children during the recording period.
          Third, focusing only on the consistent realisations (C1 and C2 in columns two and three
respectively), some clusters are always consistently reduced towards the less sonorous segment
without any cases in which the more sonorous segment is consistently realised (/pr/, /tr/, /br/,
/dr/, /xr/, /kl/, /sw/, /kr/, /fr/, /sl/, /pl/, /bl/, /xl/, /sm/, /fl/, /sp/, /sk/). Other clusters have one or
                                                                                                         69


more cases in which a child realises the more sonorous segment (/sn/, /st/, /kn/, /tw/, /kw/).
Furthermore, cluster /sx/ is variable too.


A major problem in the discussion of the data from Table 4.4 is that some clusters have very few
cases to analyze. Although the average number of cases to analyze per cluster is 8.3, some
clusters have fewer than eight cases (e.g. /sw/, /kr/, /fr/, /sl/, /pl/, /bl/, /bl/, /xl/, /sm/, /fl/, /tw/,
/kw/). For example, looking at cluster /fl/, there are only two cases in which a child realises the
cluster as C1 or C2 at least eight times in the spontaneous and the initial condition. It is therefore
difficult to compare clusters in terms of variation. In the next section, I increase the data set by
adding target word data that were either imitated and/or produced in medial position. I will thus
consider all target word data regardless of any conditions. The average number of cases to
analyze (i.e., reduction strategies with at least 8 instances of reduction) will then be 13.2. I will
first discuss the effect of the spontaneous vs. imitation and initial vs. medial conditions.


4.2.2 Results: All target words
4.2.2.1 Conditions
The first issue to consider is whether there is a significant effect of imitation versus spontaneous
and medial versus initial in the percentage of reduction and in the reduction strategy. There are
high percentages in the preferred conditions: 74% of the C1 and C2 realisation data is initial and
83% of the C1 and C2 realisation data is spontaneous. In the ideal situation we would distinguish
between four sub-conditions: medial- imitation; medial- spontaneous; initial-imitation and
initial- spontaneous. By doing this we could also see whether there is an interaction between the
conditions. However, there are too few data available in the sub-condition medial-imitation (only
2.1%). See Appendix 4.5 for an overview of distribution of data in the four sub-conditions. The
unwanted result is that all analyses on the effect of conditions would be based on too few
children that happen to have data in all four sub-conditions. Therefore, I will analyse the effects
of each condition separately. The distribution of C1 and C2 realisations in the spontaneous
versus imitation conditions per cluster is given in Appendix 4.6a. The distribution of C1 and C2
realisations in the initial versus medial conditions per cluster is given in Appendix 4.6b. Since
there are so many data available per cluster, I will consider possible effects per cluster, rather
than across clusters. These results are summarized in the following discussion.
                                                                                                             70


          There are two main questions regarding C1 and C2 realisations that need to be answered
at this point: i) do the conditions have an effect on the number of cluster reductions and ii) do the
conditions have an effect on the reduction strategy (e.g. the direction of deletion)? Analyses of
variance (Mancova) were performed for each condition for each cluster, and across clusters. The
results are given in the appendices 4.7a-d.
          The distribution of data in the 10 processes is given in Table 4.5a for the condition
spontaneous versus imitation and in Table 4.5b for the condition sentence initial versus sentence
medial:


Table 4.5           Distribution of processes for both conditions

spontaneous     0.12      23.60     8.45     6.91      46.88    10.94     0.81     1.00     0.80   0.49


Table 4.5b          Distribution of processes for initial versus medial conditions (in %)
