between CC's.



In order to see whether children reduce more clusters within a certain condition I first consider
the sum of process 1 (e.g. C1 retention) and process 2 (e.g. C2 retention) out of all processes.
Focussing on the medial versus initial condition, reduction to C1 or C2 occurs slightly more
often in the medial condition than in the initial condition: 32.4% in the medial condition versus
31.3% in the initial condition. See Appendix 4.7a for the details. Taking all clusters into account,
there is no significant effect of medial vs. initial condition F(1,711)= 1.33, p=.2500. Considering
each cluster separately, there is a significant difference in only three clusters, with slightly more
C1 + C2 reduction in the medial condition (/br/ and /dr/ in two cases) and more reduction in the
initial condition (/sp/) in one case):
                                                                                                        71


sp        F(1,32)= 4.86, p=.0349             .383 medial          .454 initial
br        F(1,32)= 6.83, p=.0136             .428 medial          .357 initial
dr        F(1,33)= 4.52, p=.0411             .498 medial          .404 initial


As can be seen for cluster /sp/ for example, there is a significant effect of condition. The
calculations are based on 32 children who have data in both conditions for this cluster. Out of all
processes /sp/ is reduced to either C1 or C2 in 38.3% of cases in the medial and 45.4% of cases
in the initial condition. Thus, there are more C1, C2 reduction cases in the initial condition. This
effect is significant (p=.0349). While cluster /sp/ has a higher reduction percentage in the initial
condition, clusters /br/ and /dr/ have a higher reduction percentage in the medial condition. Out
of a total of 23 clusters, there are three clusters that have a significant effect (/sp/, /br/, /dr/). Over
all clusters, there is no significant effect. It might be surprising that there is no significant effect
of whether the cluster is sentence initial or not. McLeod, Hand, Rosenthal and Hayes (1994)
found that children reduce more clusters in connected speech than in isolated words. However,
unlike the authors named above, I followed a stricter definition of initial data, excluding all data
with non-initial clusters, such as simple NP's starting with determiners. For example, if I asked
the child: “What is that?” (pointing at a picture with a flower) and the child answered: een bloem
a flower, then I would code flower as realised in medial condition, since there is a preceding
determiner (“een”) . The fact that I used a very strict definition of the condition might explain
why I do not find an effect of the medial versus initial condition.
        Regarding the condition spontaneous versus imitation, there are more C1 and C2
reductions in the imitation condition (35.3) than in the spontaneous condition (29.8), which is
surprising. See Appendix 4.7b for the details. Over all, this difference is significant: F(1,674)=
30.30, p=.000. However, looking at individual clusters, we see that the significant difference lies
in six clusters.


sp        F(1,32)= 5.87, p=.0212             .541 imitation       .441 spontaneous
st        F(1,25)= 13.01, p=.0013            .622 imitation       .440 spontaneous
sx        F(1,12)= 5.32, p=.0397             .605 imitation       .417 spontaneous
pr        F(1,30)= 5.83, p=.0221             .413 imitation       .342 spontaneous
fl        F(1,31)= 4.31, p=.0463             .173 imitation       .105 spontaneous
fr        F(1,32)= 5.90, p=.0210             .318 imitation       .236 spontaneous
                                                                                                  72


As can be seen for cluster /fl/ for example, there is a significant effect of the condition imitation
versus spontaneous. Out of all processes, there are more reductions for this cluster in the
imitation condition (17.3%) than there are in the spontaneous condition (10.5%). This effect is
significant (p=.0463). The reason why all clusters with a significant effect have more reductions
in the imitation condition is unclear to me. It could be because of the fact that there are more
imitation data in the earlier recordings, together with the fact that there are more reduction cases
in the earlier recordings.
         Summarizing the above discussion regarding the first concern, i.e., whether children
reduce more clusters within a certain condition, there is an overall effect of the condition
spontaneous versus imitation: children reduce more clusters in the imitation condition. There is
no overall effect in the condition initial versus medial.


The second concern is whether conditions have an effect on the deletion strategy. In other words,
does a condition have an effect on the child's change in reduction direction? For instance, for the
cluster [sp], does a child realise [s] more often in initial position, but [p] more often in medial
position? In order to compute this, the numbers of C1 realisations out of the total of C1 and C2
realisations within different conditions is compared.
         Regarding the medial versus initial condition, children have more C1 realisations than C2
realisations in the medial condition (75.7) than in the initial condition (72.8). See Appendix 4.7c
for the details. Overall, this effect is significant: F(1,369)= 6.66, p=.0102. However, per separate
cluster this effect turns out to be significant in only /xr/ clusters:


C1 realisation for xr                      F(1,20)= 5.18, p=.0339   .952 medial   .927 initial


As is seen for cluster /xr/ there are more C1 realisations in the medial condition (95.2%) than
there are in the initial condition (92.7%). This effect is significant (p=.0339). However, in the 22
other clusters there is no significant effect.
         Regarding the imitation versus spontaneous condition, we see that children have slightly
more C1 realisations in spontaneous data (69.9%) than in imitation data (69.8%). See Appendix
4.7d for the details. Overall this difference is not significant: F(1,359)= .01, p=.9311. We see a
significant effect for cluster /sn/ and an almost significant effect for /bl/.

C1 realisation for sn                 F(1,21)= 5.98, p=.0234           .675 imitation     .835 spontaneous
C1 realisation for bl                 F(1,12)= 4.48, p=.0557           .985 imitation     .883 spontaneous


In this section I have considered two important conditions, spontaneous versus imitation and
initial versus medial. Since significance for both conditions is only found for a few clusters, I
will base my analysis from now on on cluster reduction using all target data, regardless of
whether they are spontaneously or non-spontaneously produced and regardless of whether they
are sentence-initial or sentence-medial, but it must be kept in mind that in some cases the
reduction results for particular clusters might be different due to these conditions.

A major difference compared with Table 4.4 is that Table 4.7 includes many more cases to
analyze (111 more cases), and thus there is a higher average number of cases per cluster (8.4 in
the spontaneous and initial target data versus 13.2 cases in all target data). We see that 85% of all
cases are consistently reduced to either the first consonant (61.7%) or to the second consonant
(23.7%). Only 14.5% involve cases with within-child variation. Although this percentage is
higher compared to the 10.9% variation cases in the spontaneous and initial target data, we still
can claim that in by far the majority of cases the individual child is consistent in his/her
reduction strategy.
          Focusing on within-child variation (column four), some clusters do not have any cases
where the individual child has a variable realisation pattern (/pl/, /pr/, /tr/, /kr/, /fl/, /fr/, /sl/).
These clusters are consistently reduced in only one direction, either C1 or C2, without any cases
of within-child variation. In the clusters (/pl/, /pr/, /tr/, /kr/, /fr/) it is always the less sonorous
segment that is realised, without any cases of within-child variation. Other clusters show more
cases of within-child variation. Cluster /sm/ for example, is reduced to either [s] or [m] by four
children during the recording period.
                                                                                                      76


        Focusing only on the consistent realisations (C1 and C2 in columns two and three
respectively), some clusters are always consistently reduced towards the less sonorous segment
without any cases in which the more sonorous segment is consistently realised (/pl/, /pr/, /bl/,
/br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kr/, /fr/, /xr/, /sw/, /sk/, /sp/). Other clusters have just one case in which a child
realises the more sonorous segment (/kl/, /xl/, /fl/, /sn/, /sl/, /kn/, /tw/). Clusters /st/, /kw/, /sm/
have more than one instance where the more sonorous segment is realised. Cluster /sx/ in which
both members are equally sonorous is highly variable: 10 children realise C1 consistently while
14 children realise C2 consistently.


Although there are 303 cases of reduction to analyze, the number of cases (thus children) to
analyze differs greatly per cluster. For example, the cluster /sx/ is reduced to C1 or C2 at least
eight times by 29 children, while cluster /pl/ is reduced towards C1 or C2 at least eight times by
only six children. Recall that the cluster /pl/ has high scores in correct realisation, resulting in
less data involving reduction processes, such as C1 and C2. We have to be cautious in defining
clusters as being between-cluster stable or between-cluster variable. In section 4.3, I will discuss
the results when the non-target data and the data from the pilot study are added. The total number
of cases in this large study that meet the 8-criterion is then 401 cases.


In what follows, I will discuss within-child variation results for all target data in more detail.
Regarding the issue of within-child variation, we see that by far the most cases show consistent
realisation patterns: 85% of all reduction cases have a consistency of at least 91% towards one of
the two target consonants. In order to look in a more detailed way at consistency in within-
individual variation, I distinguish among five subcategories: i) cases where the child has 100%
reduction towards one particular target consonant (A), ii) cases in which the highest percentage
towards a particular consonant is between 91 and 99 (B), iii) cases in which the highest
percentage towards a particular consonant is between 81-90 (C), iv) cases in which the highest
percentage towards a particular consonant is between 71-80 (D), v) cases in which the highest
percentage is lower than 71 (E). Table 4.8 presents the numbers of cases within each category
(across clusters). The number of cases with percentages per cluster within each category is given
in Appendix 4.9.
                                                                                                                                   77




Table 4.8             Overview of number of cases within each category (categories A-E distinguish among
reduction strategies in which the highest percentage of reduction towards a particular consonant is: A) 100;
B) between 91 and 99; C) between 81 and 90; D) between 71 and 80; E) lower than 71).
N cases       A                 B                         C                        D                   E
              n (A)     %A      n (B)        %B           nC           %C          n (D)      %D       n (E)     %E          N

As can be seen from Table 4.8, out of the 303 reduction cases analyzed, 221 cases show a
reduction strategy that is 100% consistent (category A). This is 73%. Considering the percentage
of cases in category A per individual cluster (see Appendix 4.9), the average percentage across
clusters in this category is 76%. The important information to be gleaned from this table is that
by far the most cases of reduction strategies fall within the 100% category (73% of all cases). In
other words most cases involve absolutely consistent reduction strategies.
          As discussed above, I consider realisation patterns as stable within-children if the highest
percentage in a particular strategy is 91% or higher. All cases where the highest percentage is
90% or lower are considered as variable within-children. The percentages of within-child stable
versus within-child variable realised clusters are given in Appendix 4.10, ordered from low to
high in within-child stable realised clusters. A distribution of percentages regarding within-child
variation is given in 4.5 (percentage refers to the highest realisation strategy).


(4.5)     1           51-60%            /sw/                                                    most within-child variable
          2           61-70%            /sm/, /xl/
          3           71-80%            /sk/, /bl/, /tw/, /kl/, /sn/
          4           81-90%            /sx/, /kw/, /kn/, /st/, /br/, /sp/
          5           91-100%           /xr/, /dr/ /pl/, /pr/, /tr/, /kr/, /fr/, /fl/, /sl/     less within-child variable



At first glance, it looks like the within-child variable clusters are also the between-child variable
clusters in Table 4.7. In other words, clusters that are realised towards C1 by some children and
towards C2 by other children seem to also be realised as C1 and C2 respectively by the same
child. For example, cluster /sm/ is both between-child variable as well as within-child variable (5
children consistently realise [s] and three children consistently realise [m], and four children
                                                                                                        78


realise either [s] or [m]). Although a relationship between between- and within-child variation
holds for /sm/, /sx/ and /kw/ (all three clusters are between-child variable and have instances of
within-child variation) and for clusters /pl/, /pr/, /tr/, /kr/ (all clusters are not within-child variable
and not between-child variable), for other clusters this is not the case. For example, clusters /sl/
and /fl/ are between-child variable, but not within-child variable. Clusters /sw/, /bl/, /sk/, /br/, /sp/
have cases of within-child variation, but within the group of consistent reductions (C1 or C2),
these clusters are reduced in one direction (either C1 or C2) and are thus not between-child
variable.


So far, we have seen that there is between-child variation: some clusters are always realised in
the same way, other clusters are consistently reduced to C1 by some children and to C2 by
others. We also saw that there is only a limited amount of within-child variation (15%).
Questions that need to be answered at this point involve the status of the 44 cases of within-
individual variation.
        First, is within-child variation seen with some children rather than with all children? In
other words, are the 44 cases of within-child variation seen in some children and not in others, or
are they spread across all children? Recall that the results in Table 4.7 are based on individual
reduction strategies, but without a specification per child (but rather per cluster). It could be the
case that some children are highly variable in their reduction strategy of a certain cluster and that
other children are consistent.
        Second, is within-child variation seen synchronically (i.e., at the same point in time) or
over time? In other words, are some cases of within-child variation rather stable within sessions,
so that there is a session effect? Recall also that the results presented so far are collapsed across
sessions. Most children were followed for about five months. It is possible that a child is
consistent at a particular time, but then changes his/her realisation direction. For example a child
can realise /sn/oep candy first as [s]oep and then as [n]oep, being variable over time but
consistent within a point in time.
        Third, is there variation between target words rather than within target words, so that
there is a possible lexical effect? In other words, do some cases of within-child variation involve
stable realisations within a particular target word? Recall that almost every test cluster has three
target words, and recall that the results presented so far are collapsed across tokens. It could be
                                                                                                    79


that children realise a particular word in one direction, and another token of the same cluster in
the other direction. For example, a child can realise /sn/oep candy as [s]oep consistently, and
realise /sn/eeuw snow as [n]eeuw consistently. This child is variable across items, but within a
single word s/he is stable.
        I will examine the 44 cases of within-child variation in more detail in the next section.

The first aspect to look at is if the individual child is consistent in his/her deletion strategy. In
other words, are some children more variable than others? In Table 4.9, the numbers and
percentages of stable and variable reduction strategies per child across clusters are given. The
column Stable refers to cases in which the reduction towards either C1 or C2 is at least 91%. The
column Variable refers to cases in which the highest percentage of reduction between C1 and C2
is 90% and under. Column N (C1,C2) indicates the total number of clusters that reach the
8 criterion for a particular child.

As is seen from Table 4.9, there are differences between children in the number of cases to
analyze (column C1,C2) and in the number of variable and stable clusters. Some children have
no, or only one or two cases where they realise one of the input consonants eight times or more
during recording (i.e. children 7, 17, 19, 24, 25, 26, 32). Child 24 does not have sufficient data to
analyze whether clusters are consistently reduced towards a particular reduction direction.
Fortunately, most children have sufficient reduction data for almost all clusters. Child 28 and
child 34, for example, have sufficient data to analyze in 22 clusters. Thirteen of the 34 children
reduce consistently with no examples of variable clusters (children 7, 9, 11, 16, 19, 22, 23, 25,
26, 28, 31, 32, 34). These children are stable in their reduction strategies across clusters that are
counted. Twenty children show variation. In total there are 44 cases of within-child variation.
                                                                                                     81




Although there are 44 cases of variation, some of these cases could be attributed to a lexical or
session effect. Thus a child can realise a given target word consistently reducing the cluster in
one direction, but realising another target word with the same cluster consistently in the other
direction, or a child can change his/her deletion strategy in a later session, but be consistent
within a particular session. In order to look for possible session and/or lexical effects, variation
in the 44 cases was analyzed in depth, per child, per target word and per session. Results are in
Appendix 4.11. In Table 4.10, an overview of the 44 cases of within-child variation is given.


Table 4.10        Analysis of 44 cases of within-child variation
                                                                    44          14.5% (N=303)
lexical effect                                                       6          2.0%
session effect across target words                                   4          1.3%
variation in only one target word with one counter example           9          3.0%
variation in only one target word with more than one counter example 13         4.3%
variation in more than one target word                              12          4.0%


As is seen in Table 4.10, out of the 44 cases of within-child variation, 10 cases involve a lexical
or session effect. I will discuss the cases with a lexical or session effect later in more detail.
         There are 34 cases of within-child variation without a lexical or session effect. These 34
cases are presented in Table 4.10 beneath the horizontal line. Out of these 34 cases, there are 12
cases of variation in more than one target word and 22 cases with variation in only one target
word. For example, child 1 reduces cluster /sw/ in the following way: i) as [s] in /sw/aaien to
wave in sessions 2 and 6; in /sw/embad swimming pool in session 1 (twice); in /sw/emmen swim
in sessions 3 (twice), 4, 6, 7 (three times); and in /sw/aan swan in session 3, 6 (twice), 7, ii) as
[w] in /sw/aan swan in sessions 2 and 5. Out of the 22 cases that have within-child variation in
only one target word, nine cases involve variation with only one counterexample. Thus these are
cases where there is only one example found with a reduction in the other direction. Recall that a
realisation strategy is defined as variable when the percentage of the highest deletion strategy
(towards either C1 or C2) is less than 91%. Cases with only eight, nine, or ten data points, need
to have only one counterexample to count as variable. We have to be cautious here. It could well
be that with more data, these cases would become defined as stable rather than as variable. For
                                                                                                     82


example, child 30 realises /sk/ as [k] in target word /sk/ippiebal in sessions 1, 2, 5, 6, 7, 9. She
realises /sk/ as [k] in target word /sk/ieen in session 1, and as [s] in session 3. Thus, although
there is only one counterexample with variation, this case is counted as variable.
        Only seven cases involve within-session and within-word variation, i.e. these are cases
where a cluster is realised differently within the same session and within the same target word.
Thus out of the 303 cases, 2.3% involve within session/word variation. The cases with within-
word and within-session variation are given in Table 4.11.


Table 4.11     Seven cases with within-target word and within-session variation (out of 303 cases)

As is seen from Table 4.11, child 3 realises the target word /sm/eren spread as [s] and [m] at the
same point in time (within the same session). However, notice that such cases of within-target
word and within-session variation are very rare in the study (2.3% of 303 cases).


As is seen in Table 4.10, 10 cases involve variation with either a lexical or session effect. There
are six cases where there is a lexical effect. I will discuss these cases in more detail in the next
subsections.


4.2.2.4.1      Lexical effects
Focusing on the within-child variable cases from Table 4.10 with a lexical effect, there are six
cases, presented in Table 4.12. In all these cases a child is consistent in his/her reduction strategy
within a target word, but not across target words with the same cluster.

As seen in Table 4.12, child 33 realises cluster /sx/ sometimes as [s], and sometimes as [x]. In
other words, the realisation strategy for this cluster is variable for this child. However, looking at
target words with [sx], there is a lexical effect: /sx/aapjes sheep and /sx/ommel swing is realised
with [x]; /sx/oentje shoe is realised with [s]. Variation is similarly lexical for other children.


4.2.2.4.2           Session effects
Focusing on the within-child variation, Table 4.13 summarizes session effects, providing type of
output and number of examples of a particular output. Recall that a session effect means that
across sessions a cluster is realised in a variable way, but comparing sessions, we see that a
cluster is first realised in a particular direction and then later, the direction is changed. In Table
4.13 we see that there are four cases with a session effect. Child 13 has data where the deletion
strategy changes during the recording period. Cluster /br/ is first realised as [b] in sessions 1-6,
then as [r] in session 7. Furthermore, cluster /kn/ is first realised as [k] in sessions 1-7, then as [n]
in session 8.


Table 4.13          Session effect in variation
Child               Cluster              First output            Following output
                                         output N                output N
Child 4             /sx/                 [s]      18              [x]      2
                    /tw/                 [t]      6              [w]       2
Child 13            /br/                 [b]      13             [r]       2
                    /kn/                 [k]      8              [n]       1
                                                                                                  84




As is seen in Table 4.13, in all session effects the child first realises the less sonorous segment,
and then the more sonorous segment. Furthermore, there is one instance of a session effect for
cluster /sx/ (child 4) in which the cluster is first realised as [s] and later as [x]. We have to be
cautious though: although each of these clusters has in total at least eight instances of reduction,
as can be seen from Table 4.13, for each session effect there are fewer than eight reductions for
the following output (i.e., first output versus following output). In other words, we do not know
for sure if these realisations are systematic or rather accidental (i.e., performance).


4.2.3   Conclusion
In this section I first used the spontaneous and initial target data and later all target data in the
main study to examine variation in the way clusters are reduced to either the first target
consonant or the second target consonant (strict definition of cluster reduction; see definition in
section 4.1.4). Before I presented the results on variation looking at all target data, I discussed
the effects of the conditions spontaneous versus imitation and initial versus medial. These effects
were only seen in some clusters. Out of the entire database with target words, one of the input
consonants is realised in 6373 words (i.e., process 1 and process 2 which is 32.6% of the target
words). The main conclusions are that i) across children, some clusters are stable and some are
variable in the way they are reduced, ii) individual children are in general highly or even
absolutely consistent in their reduction strategy.
        First, I looked at variation in realisation strategies for all data of either C1 or C2
reduction. Five categories (from variable to stable) were defined in which percentages refer to
the highest deletion direction between C1 and C2, repeated in (4.6):


(4.6)   1      51-60%          /sm/, /kw/                              unstable pattern
        2      61-70%          /sx/
        3      71-80%          /st/, /fl/
        4      81-90%          /sn/, /sl/, /kn/, /tw/, /sw/, /xl/
        5      91-100%         /sp/, /sk/, /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/,     stable pattern
                               /tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/, /fr/, /xr/
                                                                                                        85


Clusters in category 5 have a stable reduction strategy across children, whereas clusters in the
other categories have a variable reduction strategy.
        Second, I looked at variation in reduction strategies per child in which there are at least
eight C1,C2 reductions per cluster. 303 individual clusters had sufficient reduction data to be
analyzed. Within-child stable clusters were defined as those in which the highest percentage of
deletion is 91% or higher. Within-child variable clusters were defined as clusters where the
highest percentage of deletion is 90% or lower. 85% of the clusters (e.g. 303 cases) were realised
with a within-child stable pattern. Of all the clusters that were realised in a stable way, 73% are
realised in an absolutely consistent way (100%) across children. In other words, in general,
children are highly or even absolutely consistent in their deletion strategies.
        Clusters with between-child consistent realisations in only one direction are: /pl, /pr/, /bl/,
/br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kr/, /fr/, /xr/, /sw/, /sk/, /sp/. In these clusters, it is always the less sonorous
segment that is realised. Clusters with between-child consistent realisations in both directions
(between-child variable clusters) are: /sn/, /sl/, /kl/, /kn/, /fl/, /xl/, /tw/, /kw/, /st/, /sm/, /sx/. It
turns out that clusters that are between-child variable are not always within-child variable (and
vice versa). There are differences between children regarding the percentage of within-child
variation.
        There were 44 cases (15%) defined as within-child variable between sessions and
between target words. It turned out that of the 44 variation cases, 6 cases (2.0% out of 303 cases)
involve stable realisations within a target word (e.g. lexical effect). In these cases a target word is
consistently reduced to one input consonant, but another target word with the same cluster is
reduced to the other input consonant. Thus, the cluster is variable, but the target word is not
variable. Furthermore, there were 4 cases (1.3%) in which all target words were reduced in one
direction and in a later session, all target words were reduced in the other direction (e.g. session
effect). In all four cases it involved a change from realising the less sonorous to realising the
more sonorous segment. The rest of this group (33 cases) involve cases where there is variation
within one or more target words either across or within sessions. There were seven cases where
the same target word was realised differently in the same session. In other words, only 2.3% of
the total number of 303 cases are variable within sessions and within target words.
        In the next section, I present results on variation adding more data, including the non-
target words from the main study and the reduction data from the pilot study.
                                                                                                     86




4.3      Comparing results adding more data
In this section, I increase the size of the database by showing that the overall results are not
affected if the results of the pilot study are included (see chapter 3) and if the non-target data is
also included. I present the variation results adding the non-target reduction data from the main
study, plus the reduction data from the pilot study (see Jongstra 2000, 2001a). An overview of
the number of reduction data (C1, C2) in the target- and non-target data of the main study and in
the pilot study is given in Table 4.14.


Table 4.14       Overview total number of data with cluster (N (CC data)) and number of C1,C2 reductions
                 (n (C1,C2))
                                 N (CC)                         n (C1,C2 data)
Main study target data           19,565                         6,373
Main study non-target data       3,118                          1,213
Pilot study                      5,029                          1,844
entire Main study +pilot study   27,712                         9,430



Although it would be interesting to compare variation between data sets, we have to be cautious
since the number of observations (and the number of children) are quite different in the data sets.
The non-target data cannot be compared with the target data, because the non-target data is not a
controlled corpus. For example, there is only one instance of cluster /sm/ in the non-target data.
Comparing the results from the pilot study with the results from the main study we should keep
in mind the differences in the set up of the studies (see chapter 3). We compare a large data set
from 34 children in the main study with a smaller one from 11 children in the pilot study.
Comparing overall variable and overall stable clusters (over all data and over all children) we see
the following categorizations, given in Table 4.15.

         d        Variable in M; stable in P:                   /sm/, /tw/



The differences in (4.7c) and (4.7d) are due to the difference in the number of children between
both studies. For example, in the pilot study, in almost all data, cluster /sm/ is realised as [m],
because all the children in this study consistently reduced /sm/ to [m]. In the main study, with
more children, /sm/ can also be consistently reduced to [s], resulting in more overall variable
clusters.31


31
  A possible explanation regarding the consisent reduction pattern for /sm/ from the children from the pilot study
(all realize /sm/ as [m]) might be due to the fact that these children were, in general, older and thus were, unlike
some children in the main study who realize /sm/ as [s], in the head stage (see hypothesis of Goad and Rose (to
appear) in section 2.4.2).
                                                                                                                     88


          Comparing percentages in individual reduction strategies in individual clusters between

number of children with sufficient data (eight per cell) in respectively the non-target data and the
pilot study. However, it is interesting to compare the overall percentage of within-child variation
across all clusters in all three data sets. We will see that these percentages are highly similar.
          In what follows, I will present the variation results by adding more data, thus including

4.3.2   Between-child and within-child variation
Reduction cases that are analyzed for within-cluster and within-child variation should meet the
“8-criterion” discussed in section 4.2.1.2. In other words, in order to be included in the analysis,
a cluster should have at least eight instances of C1/C2 reduction for a particular child. Applying
the eight-data-per-cell condition, there are 318 cases to analyze from the main study, including
non-target data (compared to 303 cases from the target data only). The number of observations
per child for each cluster for realising C1 and/or C2 for the entire main study is given in
Appendix 4.13a. The percentage of C1 realisation out of all C1,C2 data, per child, per cluster for
the entire main study is given in Appendix 4.13b. There are also 89 cases from the pilot study to
analyze. The number of data per child for each cluster for realising C1 and/or C2 for the pilot
study is given in Appendix 4.14a. The percentage of C1 realisation out of all C1,C2 data, per
child, per cluster for the entire pilot study is given in Appendix 4.14b. In total, there are 407
cases, which is 104 cases more than the number of cases from the target data (about 20% more
cases). The most interesting aspect is that the percentage of variable data is highly similar in all

4.3.3   Conclusion
When the pilot study and entire study are taken into account, the total number of reduction cases
for the analysis of between- and within-child stable and variable clusters is increased. In the
analysis, the 8-criterion was used. The results are highly similar to the target data looking at
within-child stable clusters and at between-child stable clusters. First, the percentage of within-
child stable cases was almost the same as in the target data study, namely around 85% (85.5%
for the target words versus 84.3% in the entire main study + pilot study). Second, looking at the
243 clusters that were consistently realised, almost all between-child stable clusters from the
target study remain between-child stable (except /xr/). Furthermore, regarding the between-child
variable clusters, some clusters added more instances of C1 and C2 realisation (/sn/, /sl/, /kn/,
/fl/, /st/, /sm/, /sx/). In other between-child variable clusters, instances of C1 were added without
adding more instances in C2 (/kl/, /tw/ and /kw/). If we group the percentages in the highest
deletion strategy (out of consistent C1,C2 cases), we see the following distribution, in Table
4.20.


Table 4.20       Overview of between-child stable and variable clusters
                 (percentage refers to the highest percentage in realisation strategy)
             60-69%     (kw), sm, sx                                 between-child variable
             70-79%     kn
             80-89%     sn, sl, fl, st, xl
             90-91%     tw, kl, xr                                   between-child stable
             100%       pl, pr, bl, br, tr, dr, kr, fr, sw, sk, sp


I will take the percentages in Table 4.20 as valid in showing between child variation. I will not
discuss the very incidental cases of consistent C2 realisations in /tw/, /kl/ and /xr/. For each
cluster there is only one instance in which a child consistently realises C2. In the many other
cases C1 is realised. Therefore, I will categorize these clusters as between-child stable. Notice
from Table 4.19 that cluster /xl/ also has one instance of C2 realisation. However this cluster
differs from /tw/, /kl/, and /xr/ in that the percentage of variation is higher, as can be seen in
Table 4.20. Furthermore, I will not discuss the high between-child variable status of cluster /kw/,
since I doubt whether this cluster is really variable. /kw/ only occurred in one target word in the
main study (e.g. /kw/ak quack which is an onomatopoeic word). It might be that some children
                                                                                                         93


who are reported as realising C2 actually have /w/ak rather than /kw/ak as the underlying
representation. The following observation leads to this assumption: In the pilot study, /kw/
occurred in words other than /kw/ak, such as /kw/aad angry, /kw/al jelly-fish with the result that
there were more C1 realisations in these words as opposed to C1 and C2 realisations in target
word /kw/ak. There are two children from the pilot study who have at least eight reductions for
/kw/ak. There is within-child variation for both children. However, this is due to the realisations
for /kw/ak as opposed to the realisations for other words. Since the between-child variation in
/kw/ is probably due to a lexical effect, I will leave this cluster out of the discussion.33
           Summarizing my assumptions: I consider the following clusters to be stable across
children: /sp/, /sk/, /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/, /fr/, /xr/, /tw/, /sw/. I consider the
following clusters as variable across children: /sx/, /st/, /sm/, /sn/, /sl/, /kn/, /fl/, /xl/.
           In the next session, I examine patterns of within-child stable clusters for each child.

otherwise useless to compare reduction strategies. In order to get more realisations per child, I

for clusters /pr/, /bl/, /br/, etc. Therefore, there are empty cells for these clusters.
        In what follows, I will discuss possible patterns in reduction strategies per sonority class,
focussing on the relationships among between-child variable clusters. In the analysis, I will
include cases with between five and seven observations per cell.


4.4.1   Patterns in between-child variable /s/-sonorant clusters
In this section I consider three clusters, /sn/, /sm/, and /sl/. Based on the findings reported in
section 4.3 these are variable clusters. Here I ask if there are any implicational relationships
between how these different clusters are reduced, or if they are independent of one each other.
My goal here is ultimately to see if we can talk about patterns (e.g. retain the less sonorant
consonant in a stop-nasal cluster) or if individual clusters can vary.


•   /s/-nasal clusters: /sm/ versus /sn/: 12 cases
There are 12 cases in which a child has consistent realisations for both /sn/ and /sm/ (children 1,
29, M1, M5, M6, M9, M10, 4, 5, 23, 28, 34). Realising [s] in both clusters occurs five times (by
children 4, 5, 23, 28, and 34). The nasal is realised six times for both clusters by children (29,
M1, M5, M6, M9, and M10). Realisations of [m] for /sm/ but [s] for /sn/ are observed once (by
child 1). The overview of reduction patterns is given in Table 4.22.


Table 4.22 Reduction patterns for /sn/ and /sm/
                /sm/             /sn/             Cases
                [s]              [s]              5
                [m]              [n]              6
                [m]              [s]              1



As is seen in Table 4.22, in 11 out of 12 cases either C1 or C2 is realised for both /sm/ and /sn/.
Note that no child realises [n] for /sn/ and [s] for /sm/.
                                                                                                       96




•   /s/-sonorant clusters: /sm/ versus /sn/ versus /sl/: 5 cases
There are five cases in which a child has consistent realisations for both /sm/, /sn/, and /sl/
(children M6, M10, 1, 28, 34). Twice [s] is realised in all three clusters (by children 28 and 34);
twice the sonorant is realised in all three clusters (by children M6 and M10). One time [m] is
realised for /sm/, and [s] is realised for /sn/ and /sl/ (by child 1). The reduction patterns for /sm/,
/sn/, and /sl/ are given in Table 4.23.


Table 4.23      Reduction patterns for /sn/, /sm/, /sl/
                /sm/              /sn/              /sl/   Cases
                [s]               [s]               [s]    2
                [m]               [n]               [l]    2
                [m]               [s]               [s]    1



Note from Table 4.23 that there are no data in which /sl/ is realised as [l], and /sm/ or /sn/ as [s].
        Summarizing the reduction patterns for /s/-sonorant clusters, we find that i) either /s/ or
the sonorant is realised in all three clusters, or ii) the nasal in /s/-nasal clusters and /s/ in /sl/
clusters is realised, or iii) [s] is realised in /sl/ and /sn/ and [m] is realised in /sm/. Thus, there is
an implicational relationship between /sm/, /sn/, and /sl/: If C2 is realised in /sl/, then C2 is
realised in /sn/, and C2 is realised in /sm/, but not vice versa.


4.4.2   Patterns in between-child variable fricative-sonorant clusters
In this section, I consider five fricative-sonorant clusters /sm/, /sn/, /sl/, /fl/ and /xl/. In section
4.3 we saw that these clusters are between-child variable. I ask here if there is any implicational
relationship between the reduction patterns in these clusters. Note that although /sw/ belongs to
the group of fricative-sonorant clusters, this cluster patterns differently compared to the clusters
listed above, in that /sw/ is stable (always consistently realised as [s] and never consistently as
[w]) while the above clusters are between-child variable.


•   fricative-lateral clusters (other than /sC/): /fl/, /xl/: 7 cases
There are seven cases to analyze (children 28, 34, 21, M11, 18, 2, M9). The fricative is realised
in both clusters five times (by children 28, 34, 21, M11, 18). Segment /l/ is realised in both
                                                                                                      97


clusters once (by child 2). Realisations [l] for /fl/ versus [x] for /xl/ occur once (by child M9).
The reduction patterns for /fl/ and /xl/ are given in Table 4.24.

                 [l]               [x]                1



As is seen in Table 4.24, in most cases either the fricative or /l/ is realised for both clusters.
There is one case with an exceptional pattern, in which /fl/ is realised as [l] and /xl/ as [x].

fricative, as can be seen in Table 4.26a. In order to compare outputs between the /s/-sonorant

Summarizing the findings for fricative-sonorant clusters, we see that /fl/ and /xl/ pattern as a
group and /sm, /sn/ and /sl/ pattern as a group. There is an implicational relationship between /fl/,
/xl/ and /sm/, /sn/, /sl/. If C2 is realised in /fl/ and /xl/, then C2 is realised in /sl/ (and thus also in
/sn/ and /sm/), but not vice versa.


4.4.3 Patterns in between-child variable obstruent-sonorant clusters
In this section, I compare the patterns between the obstruent-sonorant clusters that are between-
child variable (see section 4.3). These clusters are /sm/, /sn/, /sl/, /fl/, /xl/, /kn/. I will first look at
implicational relationships between different obstruent-nasal clusters (/sm/, /sn/, /kn/). Then I
look at possible implicational relationships between /sl/ versus /kn/ versus /fl/ and /xl/.


•    Obstruent –nasal clusters: /sm/ versus /sn/ versus /kn/: 8 cases
There are eight cases in which there are consistent realisations for all three clusters (children M9,
28, 34, 5, 23, M6, M10, M5). C1 is realised in all three clusters four times (by children 5, 23, 28,
                                                                                                   99


34); C2 is realised in all three clusters three times (by children M5, M6, M10); [m] for /sm/, [n]
for /sn/ and [k] for /kn/ occurs once (by child M9). Thus seven out of eight cases involve a
strategy in which either C1 or C2 is realised. The patterns are given in Table 4.27a.
        Looking at cases where there is at least one realisation of /kn/ versus one of either /sm/
and/or /sn/, there are 15 cases to analyze (children 2, M9, 28, 34, 5, 23, M6, M10, M5, M7, 6,
M8, 15, 31, 12). These patterns are given in Table 4.27b. There are nine cases in which C1 is
realised in /kn/ on one hand and in either /sm/ and/or /sn/ (by children 5, 23, 28, 34, 6, 15, 31,
M7, M8). There are four cases in which C2 is realised in /kn/ and in either /sm/ and/or /sn/ (by
children 12, M5, M6, M10). There are two cases in which C1 is realised in /kn/ and C2 is
realised in either /sm/ and/or /sn/ (by children 2 and M9). No cases occur in which /kn/ is
realised as [n] and /sm/ and/or /sn/ is realised as [s].


Table 4.27      Reduction patterns for /sm/, /sn/, /kn/
                /sm/    /sn/              /kn/     Cases
a.              [s]     [s]               [k]      4
                [m]     [n]               [n]      3
                [m]     [n]               [k]      1
b.              [       C1]               [C1]     9
                [       C2]               [C2]     4
                [       C2]               [C1]     2


As is seen in Table 4.27, there is an implicational relationship between /sm/, /sn/, and /kn/:
Realising C2 in /kn/ implies that C2 is realised in /sm/ and /sn/, but not vice versa.


•    /kn/ versus /sl/: 10 cases
There are ten cases to analyze (children 2, 28, 34, 6, 15, 21, M8, 12, M6, M10). C1 is realised in
both clusters six times (by children 28, 34, 6, 15, 21, M8). C2 is realised in both clusters twice
(by children M6, M10). Once /sl/ is realised as [l] and /kn/ as [k] (by child 2). Once /sl/ is

As is seen in Table 4.28, in most cases either C1 or C2 is realised in /sl/ and /kn/. However, cases

As is seen in Table 4.29, in most cases C1 is realised. There is no implicational relationship
between /kn/ on the one hand and /fl/ and /xl/ on the other. In both groups either C1 or C2 is
realised independently from the other.
        Summarizing the findings for between-child variable obstruent-sonorant clusters, we see
that there is an implicational relationship between /kn/ and /sm/ and /sn/: Realising C2 in /kn/
implies that C2 is realised in /sm/ and /sn/, but not vice versa. There is no relationship between
/kn/ and /sl/, nor is there a relationship between /kn/ and /fl/ and /xl/.


4.4.4 Patterns in between-child variable /s/-obstruent clusters
In this section, I compare the reduction patterns in two between-child variable /s/-obstruent
clusters: /st/ and /sx/.

There are 19 cases in which a child has consistent realisations for both /st/ and /sx/ (children
M10, 3, M1, 2, 15, M5, 22, 28, 34, 14, M9, 1, 5, 23, 13, 20, 4, 16, 27). C2 is realised in both
clusters 11 times (by children 28, 34, 14, M9, 1, 5, 23, 13, 4, 16, 27). C1 is realised in both
clusters 2 times (by children 3 and M5). [t] for /st/ versus [s] for /sx/ occurs five times (child

for /st/ and /sx/ are given in Table 4.30.

                 /st/             /sx/              Cases
                 [t]              [x]               11
                 [t]              [s]               5

                 [s]              [x]               1


As is seen in Table 4.30, out of the three cases where /st/ is realised as [s], twice the child
realises /sx/ as [s] as well. But we need more realisation cases to see whether this pattern occurs
often. It seems that /st/ and /sx/ pattern independently from each other.
                                                                                                             102


4.4.5     Conclusion
In the sections given above, I compared reduction patterns between individual clusters that are
consistently realised by individual children, but variable between children. I discussed whether
there are possible implicational relationships between these clusters. The consistent reduction
strategies per individual child show the following implications:
•     Realising C2 in /sl/ implies C2 in /sn/ which implies C2 in /sm/, but not vice versa
      (section 4.4.1).
•     Realising C2 in /fl/ and /xl/ implies realising C2 in /sl/ (and thus also realising C2 in /sn/ and
      /sm) but not vice versa (section 4.4.2).
•     Realising C2 in /kn/ implies C2 in /sn/ which implies C2 in /sm/ but not vice versa (section
      4.4.3).


In chapter 5, I will discuss how the implicational relationships discussed above meet the
predictions of the theoretical models introduced in chapter 2. In the next section, I summarize the
results on variation in reduction strategies.


4.5       Summary of the main findings in reduction strategies
In this chapter, I looked at between-child and within-child variation in reduction strategies in a
step-by-step fashion. I first considered the smallest part of the database (target words in the
spontaneous and initial condition only) (section 4.2.1). Then, I considered all target words,
regardless of condition (section 4.2.2). Then, I considered more data, including the nontarget
data from the main study and the reduction data from the pilot study (regardless of condition
(4.3)). I found that the various factors were not, over all, significant. The main findings on
variation in reduction strategies are summed up below in three main points.


1         Variation between and within clusters
A         Clusters vary in the way they are typically realised in reduction: some are between-child
          stable, and thus realised in one direction (/sp/, /sk/, /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/,
          /fr/, /xr/, /tw/, /sw/) while others are between-child variable, and thus realised in both
          directions (/st/, /sx/, /sm/, /kn/, /sn/, /sl/, /fl/, /xl/).
                                                                                                   103


B       Between-child stable clusters are not necessarily within-child stable clusters and vice
        versa. For example, clusters /sl/ and /fl/ are between-child variable, but not within-child
        variable. Clusters /sw/, /bl/, /sk/ /br/, /sp/ are within-child variable, but not between-child
        variable.


2       Variation between and within children
A       There is variation between children in how they reduce clusters (see 1A).
B       Within-child variation is very limited. Out of the 303 cases analyzed, 85% are highly or
        absolutely consistently realised within one reduction strategy during the entire recording
        period. 3.3% of the cases involve a lexical or session effect. In these cases, the child is
        consistent within a target word or within a certain period of time. Only 2.3% of the cases
        involve variation within the same session and within the same target word.


3       Implicational relationships in reduction patterns
Comparing reduction patterns in individual children, we see that there are implicational
relationships between some clusters. First, realising C2 in /sl/ implies C2 in /sn/ which implies
C2 in /sm/, but not vice versa. Second, realising C2 in /fl/ and /xl/ implies realising C2 in /sl/
(and thus in /sn/ and /sm/) but not vice versa. Third, realising C2 in /kn/ implies C2 in /sn/ which
implies C2 in /sm/ but not vice versa.


In the next chapter, I discuss the theoretical implications of the results given above (section 5.2).
I also briefly discuss the results when using a less strict definition of cluster reduction (section
5.3).
                                                                                                    104



Chapter 5 Discussion

5.1    Introduction
In chapter 4, the patterns of reduction strategies were presented. One of the major findings was
that some clusters are between-child stable and others are between-child variable (section 4.3). In
addition, it was found that there are implicational relationships between clusters (section 4.4). In
this chapter, I discuss the theoretical implications of the variation patterns in reduction (section
5.2). Such a model should be able to account for the differences between variable and stable
clusters and explain the implicational relationships between some clusters.
       So far, I have used the term cluster reduction to refer to the processes in which one of the
target consonants is realised, i.e., C1 or C2. In section 5.3, I briefly discuss the results involving
variation in reduction strategies when using a less strict definition of cluster reduction. I will
show that results are identical regardless of the voice specification in the output, but that results
differ when reduction is defined by which sonority class (obstruent or sonorant) is realised.


5.2    Theoretical implications
A major contribution in the setup of the main study in this thesis is that variation patterns in
individual clusters rather than sonority groups was tested for. By doing this, differences in
patterning between individual clusters can be investigated, and thus also the patterning within
groups of clusters that form a natural class regarding sonority (i.e., test the sonority hypothesis).
One of the major findings is that regardless of the type of cluster, within-child variation is very
limited. Thus, although output realisations might be very different in terms of processes and
segments, in terms of realising C1 or C2 children are in general highly consistent in their
realisation strategies. In chapter 4 (section 4.2), it was found that 2.3% of the reduction cases
with at least eight reduction observations (303 cases) involve variable reductions within the same
target words and within the same session. The fact that within-child variation is so limited
indicates that it is highly likely that children realise clusters according to a certain system. This
section focuses on the system in reduction strategies.
       Important questions related to a theoretical explanation of between- and within-child
variation in the reduction strategy of word-initial clusters are: To what extent does sonority by
itself explain reduction strategies? Do clusters within a sonority group pattern as a group with
                                                                                                           105


respect to cluster reduction in that all members are either stable or variable? What leads a child
to a certain reduction strategy? In other words, how do we account for different reduction
strategies?
        In section 5.2.1, I will discuss the results of variation in the light of sonority. In section
5.2.2, I will seek an answer to the question whether it is legitimate to group clusters together in
terms of sonority classes. In section 5.2.3 I will examine which of the hypotheses discussed in
chapter 2 can best explain the attested patterns (see chapter 4): the prosodically-based hypothesis
(see section 2.4.3), or the feature-based hypothesis (see section 2.4.3). In section 5.2.4, I will
present a preliminary account of the difference between between-child stable and between-child
variable clusters. In section 5.2.5, I will compare individual realisation strategies between
clusters per child and discuss how a child’s grammar can be accounted for. The conclusion is
given in section 5.2.6.


5.2.1   Sonority based prediction
Unlike many studies where results in reduction strategies are discussed in terms of sonority
classes rather than as individual clusters, in this study, the realisation strategies of 23 individual
clusters were tested. In section 4.3, we saw that some clusters are variable and that some are
stable in the way they are realised. Clusters /st/, /sx/, /sm/, /sn/, /sl/, /kn/, /fl/, /xl/ are between-
child variable. Clusters /sk/, /sp/, /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/, /fr/, /xr/, /tw/, /sw/ are
between-child stable. (Recall that /kw/ is omitted, see section 4.3). Although it is by far the
majority of the cases in which the less sonorous segment is realised (89.8% in the target study
data of all consistently realised clusters reduce to the less sonorous segment), cases also occur in
which the more sonorous segment is realised consistently by a child. The major conclusion
regarding a theoretical account of cluster reduction is that the features compromising sonority on
their own explain many realisation patterns in which the less sonorous segment is realised, but
not the realisations in which the more sonorous segment is realised. In other words, although it
seems to be true that sonority plays an important role in cluster reduction strategies, it is not
always the case that strategies are driven by sonority in terms of which consonant is less and
which consonant is more sonorous.
        In the next section, I evaluate whether clusters within the same sonority group pattern
similarly, in that all clusters are either between-child variable, or between-child stable.
                                                                                                   106




5.2.2 Treating clusters as separate or as sonority classes
An important question addressed in this thesis is whether individual clusters indeed pattern
similarly compared to other clusters that belong to the same sonority group in terms of either
between-child stable or between-child variable clusters. Recall from chapter 2 (section 2.3) that
Fikkert discusses the reduction strategies referring to the following sonority groups: plosive-
liquid (PL), fricative-liquid (FL), plosive-nasal (PN), fricative-nasal, plosive-glide (PG),
fricative-glide (FG), /s/-plosive (sP), and /s/-fricative (sF). Notice that in this study most of these
groups, except sF, PN, and FG, consist of more than one target cluster. Sonority groups sF, PN,
and FG have only one target cluster, /sx/, /kn/, and /sw/, respectively. Sonority group PG also has
only one member in my study, namely since /kw/ is omitted. The sonority groups with more than
one cluster member are presented with their cluster members in (5.1).


(5.1)   PL      /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/
        FL      /fl/, /fr/, /sl/, /xl/, /xr/
        FN      /sn/, /sm/
        sP      /st/, /st/, /sk/


I now discuss whether the clusters in each of these groups (except sF, PN, FG, PG) indeed
pattern similarly in terms of being between-child variable or between-child stable.


•   Plosive-liquid clusters (PL):                 /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/
These clusters are all between-child stable and thus pattern as a group.
•   Fricative-liquid clusters (FL): /fl/, /fr/, /sl/, /xl/, /xr/
/fl/, /sl/, and /xl/ are between-child variable; /fr/ and /xr/ are between-child stable. FL clusters do
not pattern as a group.
•   Fricative-nasal clusters (FN):                /sn/, /sm/
These clusters are between-child variable and thus pattern as a group.
•   /s/-plosive clusters (sP): /st/, /st/, /sk/
/st/ is between-child variable and /sp/, and /st/ are between-child stable. sP clusters do not pattern
as a group.
                                                                                                   107


In summary, clusters in sonority groups PL, FN pattern as a group, while clusters in sonority
groups FL, sP do not pattern as a group. It therefore is not always right to discuss reduction
strategies referring to sonority groups. The main question at this point is what leads a child to a
particular realisation strategy. Is it a preference for certain features (other than sonority), or
certain prosodic representations of a cluster, or both, or something else?
        In the next section, I discuss whether the patterns given in chapter 4 are best explained by
a difference in prosodic representation (see section 2.4.2) or by a difference in constraint ranking
in which avoiding certain features plays an important role (see section 2.4.3).


5.2.3   Predictions and attested patterns
In this section I compare predictions and attested patterns in reduction. In section 5.2.3.1 I
discuss whether the patterns predicted by Goad and Rose (section 2.4.2) are attested in this study.
In section 5.2.3.2 I discuss whether the patterns predicted by Pater and Barlow (see section 2.4.3)
are attested in this study. The conclusion is given in section 5.2.3.3.


5.2.3.1 Testing the predictions of Goad and Rose
Recall from chapter 2 that Goad and Rose (to appear) claim that a difference in prosodic
representation in the input may lead to different realisations for /s/-sonorant clusters. Their
account makes predictions on three dimensions. First, it explains the difference between clusters
that are reduced in a variable way and clusters that are reduced in a stable way. Second, their
account predicts some sort of a developmental path in which children can go from stage 1
(sonority pattern) to stage 2 (head pattern), or skip stages, but never go from stage 2 to stage 1.
Third, their account predicts differentiation between clusters within the group of /s/-sonorant
clusters. I will discuss these aspects in more detail, and with each aspect check whether this
prediction is attested by the reduction patterns found in chapter 4.


•   Variation in /s/-sonorant clusters
Goad and Rose predict that /s/-sonorant clusters should be variable in their deletion pattern: /sm/,
/sn/, /sl/, /sw/. Except for cluster /sw/, these clusters are indeed between-child variable. However,
the following clusters are between-child variable as well: /st/, /sx/, /kn/, /fl/, /xl/. These clusters
are not predicted to be variable by Goad and Rose.
                                                                                                   108


•    Developmental path
Recall from section 2.4.2 the possible scenarios in stages regarding the realisation of /s/-sonorant
clusters predicted by Goad and Rose. These scenarios are given in Table 5.1, where output 2
indicates a realisation at a certain point in time that is different from an output at an earlier point
in time (output 1), etc.


Table 5.1       Outputs of /s/-sonorant clusters in different scenarios
Scenario        Output 1         Output 2         Output 3
1               [s]              [s-sonorant]     -
2               [sonorant]       [s-sonorant]     -
3               [s]              [sonorant]       [s-sonorant]
*4              [sonorant]       [s]              [s-sonorant]


In scenario 1. a child starts with stage 1, skips stage 2 and then realises two segments, regardless
of whether these are in the input. In scenario 2. a child skips stage 1, and goes through stage 2
before realising two consonants. In scenario 3. a child goes through stage 1, then through stage 2
and then realises two consonants. In scenario 4. a child goes through stage 2, then through stage
1 and then realises two consonants. This scenario is not predicted to occur (indicated in Table 5.1
with a star).
        Recall from chapter 4 that session effects (see 4.2.2.4.2) were limited in my study (only
four cases). This is probably because of the relatively short time span the children were followed
for (i.e., half a year for the children in the main study and ten weeks for the children in the pilot
study). Most children reduced a particular cluster in the same way throughout the recording time.
Thus most data are examples of either scenario 1 or 2 in Table 5.1. Examples of scenario 3 were
not found in my data. However, there were four cases of a session effect in which first the first
consonant was realised and later the second consonant (in clusters /sx/, /tw/, /br/, and /kn/).
Examples of scenario 4 were not found in my study. This is in accordance with the prediction
Goad and Rose make. Recall that these data should be viewed with caution since it could be that
there are fewer than eight reductions for each sort of output (see number of reductions for each
output in Table 4.13 in section 4.2.2.4.2).
                                                                                                     109


•   Differentiating between clusters within /s/-sonorant group
Goad and Rose claim that there can be an intermediate stage where /sm/ and /sn/ are realised as
[m] and [n] (head pattern), but where /sl/ is realised as [s] (sonority pattern). This is because of
the better dependent status /l/ has, compared to nasal segments (see section 2.4.2). This
prediction was borne out in the data. I found an implicational relationship between /sm/, /sn/
versus /sl/ (see chapter 4, section 4.4): realising C2 in /sl/ implies realising C2 in /sn/ which
implies realising C2 in /sm/, but not vice versa. In other words, if /sl/ is realised as [l] then /sn/ is
realised as [n] and if /sn/ is realised as [n], then /sm/ is realised as [m]. But realising the nasal in
/sm/ and /sn/ does not imply realising [l] in /sl/. Although the implicational relationship between
/sl/ and /s/-nasal clusters is attested in my data, there are other implicational relationships (i.e.,
between /kn/ and /s/-nasal clusters and between /sl/ and /fl/ and /xl/) that are not predicted by
Goad and Rose. This is because the authors assume that variation is found only in /s/-sonorant
clusters and not in other clusters.
        In summary, the predictions Goad and Rose make regarding variable clusters (all /s/-
sonorant clusters are variable) are, except for cluster /sw/, indeed true, but there are more
variable clusters, outside the group of /s/-sonorant clusters (/sx/, /st/, /kn/, /fl/, /xl/). The
prediction regarding developmental stages i.e., the prediction of what does not occur (scenario
4), was borne out as well, but the other scenarios were not found either, at least for /s/-sonorant
clusters. The prediction of Goad and Rose regarding the difference in patterning between /sl/ and
/s/-nasal clusters is borne out by the data: Some children realise /sl/ as [s] and /s/-nasal clusters as
nasal, but no child realises /sl/ as [l] and /s/-nasal clusters as [s].
        In the next section, I will test whether the predictions made by Pater and Barlow (2002)
are attested in the data.


5.2.3.2 Testing the predictions of Pater and Barlow
Recall from chapter 2 (section 2.4.3) that Pater and Barlow (2002) claim that a difference in the
reduction pattern of clusters with a fricative is due to a difference in constraint ranking where
constraints militating against certain features play an important role. The first thing to note is that
we have found that not only fricative-sonorant clusters are variable. The cluster /kn/ is variable
as well. However, since the discussion in Pater and Barlow is focused on fricative clusters, I will
evaluate their predictions regarding these sorts of clusters. Recall that Pater and Barlow focus on
                                                                                                    110


the effect of the constraint *FRICATIVE in different conflict situations with sonority constraints,
explaining the variation seen in fricative-sonorant clusters. The results of the different positions
of this constraint within rankings are repeated in Table 5.2.


Table 5.2         Reduction patterns that are predicted to be possible
           /sw/   /sl/    /sn/     /st/
B          s      s       s        t
C          s      s       n        t
D          s      l       n        t
E          w      l       n        t



All patterns except for pattern E occur in the data. Pattern E does not occur since /sw/ is always
realised as [s]. The patterns that are predicted not to occur by Pater and Barlow are repeated in
Table 5.3.
