a.         w      l       n        s                 g.      w        s      s      s
b.         w      l       s        t                 h.      s        l      s      s
c.         w      l       s        s                 i.      s        s      n      s
d.         w      s       s        t                 j.      w        s      n      t
e.         w      s       n        s                 k.      s        l      s      t
f.         s      l       n        s



Patterns a., e., g., and j. do not occur since /sw/ is always realised as [s]. Patterns f., h. and i. do
not occur. Notice that in all these patterns /st/ is realised as [s]. This can be clearly seen in the
reduction patterns of the three children that realise /st/ as [s], given in Table 5.4.


Table 5.4         Reduction patterns of children who realise /st/ as [s]
                  /st/    /sl/     /fl/     /xl/     /sn/    /sm/     /sw/   /sp/   /sk/   /sx/
child 3           [s]     [s]      (f)      -        [s]     -        [s]    -      -      [s]
child 20          [s]     -        -        -        -       (s)      -      -      -      [x]
child M5          [s]     -        -        (x)      [n]     [m]      -      -      -      [s]
                                                                                                           111




As is seen from Table 5.4, patterns f., h., and i. from Table 5.3 do not occur in the patterns of
children 3, and 20. However, child M5 realises /st/ as [s] and /s/-nasal clusters as [nasal], which
is predicted by pattern i. not to occur. Pattern k. does not occur since we have seen in section 4.4.
in chapter 4 that no child realises /sl/ as [l] and /s/-nasal clusters as [s].
        So far we have seen that some patterns predicted to occur by Pater and Barlow indeed
occurred in the data (patterns B-D in Table 5.2), and some patterns predicted not to occur indeed
do not occur in the data (patterns a.-k. in Table 5.3). Pattern i. occurs once.
        The main problem with the account of Pater and Barlow focusing on fricative clusters is
that it does not differentiate between clusters within the same sonority group. First, we have seen
that there is a difference between /sl/, /fl/, /xl/ on the one hand and /xr/ and /fr/ on the other,
where the first group is between-child variable and the latter is between-child stable. Recall that
Pater and Barlow refer to the constraint *L-ONS in Table 2.9 in chapter 2., assuming similar
patterning of all liquids rather than different behaviour within that group. Only fricative clusters
with a sonorant that is a lateral or a nasal are variable (i.e., /sl/, /sm/, /sn/, /sl/, /fl/, /xl/). Fricative
clusters with a rhotic are not variable. Also they cannot explain why cluster /st/ is variable and
clusters /sp/ and /sk/ are not, since they must predict similar patterning in all /s/-plosive clusters.
The second problem in Pater and Barlow’s account concerns the lack of differentiation within
cluster groups between /sl/ and /fl/ and /xl/. Pater and Barlow do not discuss the difference in
patterning between /sl/ and other clusters with /l/ as the second consonant. Assuming that these
clusters are predicted to pattern the same within a ranking of constraints that refer to sonority, the
following patterns are predicted not to occur by Pater and Barlow (repeated in Table 5.5).


Table 5.5 Reduction patterns that are predicted to be impossible according to Pater and Barlow
        /sw/     /sl/    /fl/     /sn/    /st/
l.      s        s       l        s       t
m.      s        l       f        s       t
n.      s        s       l        n       t
o.      s        l       f        n       t
p.      w        l       f        n       t
q.      w        s       l        n       t
                                                                                                  112


Patterns p. and q. do not occur for the reason discussed above (/sw/ is always realised as [s]).
Focusing on the realisations for /sl/, /sn/, and /fl/, I note that Pattern l. does not occur since all
children who realise /fl/ as [l] also realise /sl/ as [l] (see implicational relationships in section
4.4). Pattern m. does not occur since all children who realise /sl/ as [l] also realise /sn/ as [n].
Pattern o. however, does occur. There are children who realise /sl/ and /sn/ as [l] and [n]
respectively, but who realise /fl/ as [f] (M10). Child M6 also realises /sl/ as [l], but does not
provide information on the realisation of /fl/, or /xl/. Thus pattern n., predicted not to occur by
Pater and Barlow, occurs in the data. There must be a reason why /sl/ patterns differently
compared to /fl/ and /xl/. Notice however, that there is one child who realises C2 in /sl/, /sm/, and
/fl/. This is not predicted by Goad and Rose, but it is predicted by Pater and Barlow. Finally the
variable status of /sx/ is not explained by Pater and Barlow. The constraint *FRICATIVE cannot
explain why some children realise one fricative (i.e., [s]), and others the other (i.e., [x]).
         In the next section, I will conclude the comparison between the predictions made by
Goad and Rose and those made by Pater and Barlow.


5.2.3.3 Conclusion
The differences in assumptions regarding between-child variable and between-child stable
clusters between Goad and Rose and Pater and Barlow are given in Table 5.6 for between-child
variable and between-child stable clusters, as defined in chapter 4.


Table 5.6             Predictions made by Goad and Rose and by Pater and Barlow
Findings study                                  Goad and Rose       Pater and Barlow
a. Variable clusters:        /st/               stable              stable
                             /sx/               ?                   ?
                             /sl/, /sm/, /sn/   variable            variable
                             /fl/, /xl/         stable              variable
                             /kn/               stable              not discussed
b. Stable clusters:          /sw/               variable            variable
                             /fr/, /xr/         stable              variable
                             other clusters     stable              stable


As is seen in Table 5.6, both the accounts of Goad and Rose and Pater and Barlow yield some
correct and some incorrect predictions. Goad and Rose incorrectly assume that clusters /st/, /fl/,
                                                                                                           113


/xl/, and /kn/ are stable while they are variable. Furthermore, they incorrectly assume that cluster
/sw/ is variable whereas the data in this study suggest this cluster is stable. Pater and Barlow
incorrectly assume that cluster /st/ is stable. They do not discuss cluster /kn/. Furthermore, they
incorrectly assume that clusters /sw/, /fr/, and /xr/ are variable while these clusters are stable.
         Regarding the implicational relationship between /sl/, /sn/, and /sm/ (i.e., if /sl/ is realised
as [l], then /sn/ is realised as [n], and /sm/ is realised as [m], but not vice versa), we see that both
sets of authors predict that patterns in which /sl/ is realised as [l] and /s/-nasal is realised as [s] do
not occur, and this is borne out by the data. Regarding the implicational relationship involving
/fl/, /xl/, and /sl/ (i.e., if /fl/ and/or /xl/ is realised as [l], /sl/ is realised as [s] but not vice versa)
Pater and Barlow make incorrect predictions since they predict that all fricative-liquid clusters
pattern similarly. Goad and Rose do not predict C2 realisation for /fl/ and /xl/, since they assume
that only /s/-sonorant clusters are variable, rather than other fricative-sonorant clusters. The third
implicational relationship that involves /kn/, /sn/, and /sm/ (i.e., if /kn/ is realised as [n], then /sn/
is realised as [n], and /sm/ is realised as [m], but not vice versa) is not discussed by Goad and
Rose or by Pater and Barlow, since Goad and Rose assume this cluster to be stable and Pater and
Barlow do not discuss it at all.
         In the next section, I present the beginnings of an account for the difference between
stable and variable clusters, and for the implicational relationships in reduction strategies,
discussed in chapter 4. I will adopt a prosodic approach assuming the basic concept proposed by
Goad and Rose (to appear) that children begin with the sonority strategy and then move to the
head strategy. I claim that in the head stage, children start to make full use of the structural
properties of two consonants in terms of sonority and place of articulation and this might lead to
different prosodifications.


5.2.4 Explaining the difference between stable and variable clusters
In (5.2a), I repeat the inventory of between-child variable clusters. In (5.2b) the inventory of
between-child stable clusters is given (see section 4.3).


(5.2a)   Between-child variable
         /st/, /sx/, /sm/, /sn/, /sl/, /fl/, /xl/, /kn/
(5.2b)   Between-child stable clusters
         /sp/, /sk/, /pl/ ,/pr/, /bl/, /br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/, /fr/, /xr/, /sw/, /tw/
                                                                                                         114




Why are clusters in (5.2a) variable and clusters in (5.2b) stable? One might think that a child
chooses to retain the consonant which s/he is able to realise articulatorily, and deletes the
consonant that s/he cannot realise. Ease of articulation could play a role. Although this might
seem true for clusters with an /r/ as second member (see section 4.1.2), this is definitely not true
for the variable clusters. For example, from singleton behaviour (see Appendix 4.3) we know
that the children who realise /kn/ as [n] are able to realise both /k/ and /n/ correctly. The same is
true for several other of the variable clusters. Realising the more sonorous consonant is not
caused by being unable to realise the less sonorous segment in singletons correctly or vice versa.
In other words, ease of articulation does not seem to be what is going on here.


In the following discussion, I argue that the difference between stable and variable clusters
relates to similarity in sonority and, secondarily, in place of articulation, between the consonants
involved. Overall, stable clusters are of two types: sonority distance is large (e.g. /pl/, /pr/) or
they have different places of articulation and sonority reversals (/sp/, /sk/). The variable clusters
are identical (/sx/) or similar in sonority (/st/, /sn/, /sm/, /sl/, /kn/, /fl/, /xl/). I propose that moving
to the head strategy involves two things: a beginning of making full use of the sonority scale and
a development of structure in the phonological output.
        Recall that I am assuming the basic concept proposed by Goad and Rose (to appear), that
children begin with the sonority strategy, and then move to the head strategy. At the sonority
stage, they simply realise the less sonorous of the two consonants. The child has a simple
syllable structure that accommodates only one consonant in the onset, and the head (i.e. the
consonant that gets realised) is completely identified by sonority relations. This notion is
sufficient to select the head according to relative sonority. This means that children in the
sonority stage syllabify as follows: s[t], [s]m, [s]l, etc., where the segment between the brackets
indicates the selected head.
        The head strategy is of greater complexity. I assume that in the head stage children make
full use of the sonority scale, given in (5.3), in which rhotics (sonority group “R”) are more
sonorous than laterals (sonority group “L”) and both are more sonorous than nasals. The letters
indicate the sonority group. The numbers indicate a notion of sonority distance between the first
and second consonant in a cluster (see, for instance, Steriade 1982, and Selkirk 1984 on sonority
                                                                                                   115


distance; this is often discussed under the term “similarity” in more recent literature (e.g. Côté
2000)).


(5.3)     oral stop <   fricative <    nasal <         /l/ <   /r/ <   glide < vowel
          P             F              N               L       R       G       V
          0             1              2               3       4       5       6



In (5.3) a full scale of sonority is presented. I propose this is what children in the head stage start
to make full use of. A child knows for example that the two consonants in cluster /fl/ have a
closer sonority distance than the consonants in cluster /fr/, since the rhotic is more sonorous than
the lateral. The relationship between two consonants in terms of sonority structure is thus of
importance (see Jongstra 2001b for a structural account of word-initial clusters in adult Dutch).
In the head stage, a simple sonority comparision is insufficient to identify the head. The child
makes full use of the sonority scale, but head selection becomes more difficult for some children
in those clusters with a structural relationhip between the consonants where the consonants can
be characterized as similar in some way. These consonants make less good onsets compared to
the consonants that are dissimilar. A good onset has a large sonority distance between the first
and the second consonant, with the sonority increasing from the first to the second consonant.
The further apart in sonority the consonants are, the more dissimilar they are, and thus the better
onset they form. Head-dependent relations are easy to establish when the sonority distance is
large, with the less sonorous being the head, and the one realised. The closer in sonority, the less
good onset the consonants form, the more difficult it becomes to identify which consonant is the
head and which the dependent.
          In (5.4), clusters are grouped according to sonority distance between C1 and C2. FF=
fricative-fricative; FP= fricative-plosive; FN= fricative-nasal; PN= plosive-nasal; FL= fricative-
lateral; PL= plosive-lateral; FR= fricative-rhotic; PR= plosive-rhotic; FG= fricative-glide; PG=
plosive-glide clusters. FP clusters (/sp/, /st/, /sk/) are given between parentheses since these
clusters have reversed sonority.
                                                                                                   116


(5.4)   distance        cluster groups   clusters
        0               FF               sx
        1               (FP), FN         (sp, st, sk), sm, sn
        2               PN, FL           kn, sl, fl, xl
        3               PL, FR           pl, bl, kl, fr, xr
        4               PR, FG           pr, br, tr, dr, kr, sw
        5               PG               tw



As can be seen in (5.4) cluster /sx/ has no distance in sonority between the Cs since both
consonants are fricatives. Clusters /kn/, /sl/, /fl/, /xl/ have a distance of 2. (5.4) shows that the
further apart two consonants are in an increasing sonority relationship, the more likely the child
will realise C1 as head and C2 as dependent. These clusters make good onsets and the head, C1,
is uniformally realised. The closer in sonority, the more likely the possibility the child will
identify C2 as the head rather than C1. These clusters do not make good onsets and C1 is
syllabified as appendix. This then leads to syllabification as C1][C2, where C1 is in the appendix
(]) and C1 is the head ([). Thus, /fr/, with a greater distance in sonority, is uniquely identified as
[fr, while /fl/, with a lesser sonority difference, varies at the different stages, [fl at the sonority
stage ([f] selected), and f][l at the head stage ([l] selected).
        The determination of head and dependent is thus relatively easy for clusters with sonority
distances of three and more. This is the case for PL, FR, PR, FG, PG clusters. Recall that these
clusters are between–child stable. I will call clusters with a sonority distance of at least three
essentially dissimilar in terms of sonority. For example, PL clusters have a sonority distance of
three, while PR clusters have a sonority distance of four. Since the relationship between the
consonants in these clusters is dissimilar with respect to sonority structure, the cluster makes a
good onset, and the determination of which consonant is the head and which is the dependent is
thus relatively easy. Thus, consonants of between-child stable clusters have a relationhip that is
characterized by dissimilar structure in terms of sonority. Clusters with a sonority distance of
either 0, 1, or 2 do not have essentially dissimilar sonority structure. The clusters FF, FN, FL,
and PN have a sonority distance less than three apart, as is seen in (5.4). It is more difficult to
determine head and dependent in these clusters for some children. This results, for those children
who have problems in sorting out the internal structure, in syllabifying C1 as extrasyllabic
(appendix). These children then select C2 as the head and realise C2.
                                                                                                   117




An important question is what causes the transition from the sonority stage to the head stage? I
propose that the fact that /s/-plosive clusters are right-headed and that their internal structure is
characterized as close in sonority distance leads some children to the hypothesis that clusters
with essentially similar sonority structure are right headed.
        In the sonority stage the child chooses the less sonorous of the two consonants and
identifies this as the head. The child will note that sometimes the head is on the left (which is
usually the case; for example [s for /sl/), and sometimes the head is on the right (e.g. [p for /sp/).
For /s/-plosive clusters the head is on the right. In learning the cause of the difference between
heads on the right and heads on the left, some children will hypothesize that when consonants are
close in sonority, the consonant on the right can be the head. This hypothesis extends the class of
possible clusters with heads on the right in the head stage for some children. This accounts for
/kn/, /fl/, /xl/, /sl/, /sn/, and /sm/ being variable. The head can change in these clusters.
        Recall that Goad and Rose (to appear) assume that variation in the reduction strategy of
/s/-sonorant clusters is caused by the difference between following the sonority or the head
pattern. Children who follow the sonority pattern will realise [s] in /sn/; children who follow the
head pattern will realise [n] in /sn/. I assume that this variation is caused by a difference within
the head stage when children are ready to determine internal structure within the cluster. The
difference between the sonority and head stages is not so much that children are refining their
notion of head, but rather refining their notion of structure. Not all C1s are to be considered as
potentially part of the onset, but some are in an appendix position. This can happen when the two
consonants are close in sonority. Children in the head stage are thus slowly refining their notion
of the relationship between C1 and C2 by realising that not all C1s are part of the onset. Thus,
within the head stage, children begin to prosodify on both consonants. This structure in C1C2
can either be head-dependent, in which C1 is head and C2 is dependent, or it can be appendix-
head, in which C1 is appendix and C2 is head. The former I refer to as head pattern 1, the latter I
refer to as head pattern 2. The knowledge with respect to sonority structure is summarized in
(5.5.) for different stages (I will ignore cluster /sx/ for the moment). In the sonority stage, head
selection is determined by relative sonority. The heads are underlined. There is no dependent
structure. In the head stage, all clusters with essentially dissimilar sonority structure (i.e., stable
clusters) are prosodified as left-headed, thus head-dependent structure (e.g. [fl for /fl/). /s/-
                                                                                                            118


plosive clusters are prosodified as right-headed, as was the case in the sonority stage. The
difference with the sonority stage is that in the head stage the child has learnt that /s/ in /s/-
plosive clusters must be in an appendix position (e.g. s][p for /sp/). Some clusters that have
consonants with closer sonority structure are prosodified by some children as head-dependent
(e.g. [sm for /sm/)) and by others as appendix-head clusters (e.g. s][m for /sm/).


(5.5)   Sonority stage                    Head Stage
        sonority pattern                  head pattern 1                      head pattern 2
        no dependent structure            head-dependent structure            appendix-head structure
        head based on sonority            head is C1, except in reversed      head is C2 in clusters with
                                          sonority cases                      similar sonority structure
        sp; st; sk; sm; sn; sl; pl; pr;   [br; [tr; [dr; [kl; [kr; [kn;       s][m; s][n; s][l; f][l
        bl; br; tr; dr; kl; kr; kn; fl;   [sm; [sn; [sl; [pl; [pr; [bl;       x][l; k][n
        fr; xl; xr; tw; kw; sw            [fl; [fr; [xl; [xr; [tw; [kw; [sw
                                          s][p; s][t; s][k


Following Goad and Rose (to appear), and as is seen in (5.5), children move from the sonority
stage to the head stage. In the head stage, children start to make full use of the sonority relations
between the consonants. The further apart in sonority, the better onset the cluster forms, and the
more likely the cluster is prosodified as head-dependent (head pattern 1). The closer in sonority,
the less good onset the cluster forms, and the less likely the cluster is prosodified as head-
dependent, but rather as appendix-head (head pattern 2). Whether a child prosodifies a cluster
according to head pattern 1 or head pattern 2 depends on how sensitive the child is to onsets with
similar structure. Children who are sensitive to this avoid these onsets and prosodify these
clusters with similar structure as heterosyllabic syllables, i.e., with the first C as an appendix.
        The account so far leaves us wondering about why /st/ is variable, while /sk/ and /sp/ are
not. Also, one could question whether it is possible to account for implicational relationships as
discussed in the previous chapter. Although they are not as important as sonority relations, it
appears that place relations are also required to account for what becomes an appendix (head
pattern 2). If two consonants in a cluster have similar place features (i.e., the same place of
articulation) as well as similar sonority, the cluster forms a less good onset, and the child has
more difficulties choosing the internal head, that is identifying which is the head and which the
dependent. If a cluster has dissimilar sonority and place structure, the better onset it is and head-
                                                                                                                                  119


dependent relationships are thus easier to determine. Consonants can have the place feature
labial, coronal or dorsal.34 I further adopt the assumption that segment /r/ does not have a Place
node (for arguments see Rice 1992, and Goad and Rose to appear). The place structure in
clusters is given in (5.6a) for clusters that start with a labial, in (5.6b) for clusters that start with a
coronal and in (5.6c) for clusters that start with a dorsal.


(5.6a)   labial-coronal              pl, bl, fl
         labial-no place             pr, br, fr
(5.6b)   coronal-labial              sp, tw, sw, sm
         coronal-dorsal              sk, sx
         coronal-coronal             st, sn, sl
         coronal-no place            tr, dr
(5.6c)   dorsal-labial               kw
         dorsal-coronal              kl, kn, xl
         dorsal-no place             kr, xr


All target clusters have dissimilar place structure except for coronal-coronal clusters, given in
(5.6b). These clusters (i.e., /st/, /sn/, /sl/) have consonants with identical place structure. The
distribution in clusters with identical place structure versus different place structure is
summarized in (5.7).


(5.7)    same place structure                     /st/, /sn/, /sl/
         different place structure                /sx/, /sp/, /sk/, /sm/, /kn/, /fl/, /xl/, /pl/, /bl/, /kl/, /fr/, /xr/, /pr/,
                                                  /br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kr/, /sw/, /tw/



As is seen in (5.7) most clusters have different place structure. The clusters /st/, /sn/, /sl/ have
similar place structure. These are clusters that are both close in sonority and close in place. The
inclusion of place as a factor involved in similarity differentiates /st/ from /sp/ and /sk/. Prosodic
structuring is more difficult for the child who begins to learn about internal structure for
consonants with similar place structure (/st/, /sn/, /sl/) than for consonants with dissimilar place
structure.


34
  I leave aside discussion of the internal organization of place structure. I am interested here only in whether
consonants have similar or dissimilar place structure. See Jongstra 2001b for details.
                                                                                                  120


         In summary, in the head stage children start to make full use of the sonority scale and
thus start to evaluate the relationship between two consonants in the cluster rather than simply
identifying the less sonorous in a cluster. In good onsets the sonority distance between the two
consonants is essentially dissimilar. If the consonants have different place structure, then it is
highly likely that a child will prosodify the cluster as a tauto-syllabic onset with C1 as head and
C2 as dependent (head pattern 1), realising C1 in cluster reduction. If there is a lack of
essentially dissimilar structure with respect to sonority distance and, secondarily, with respect to
place of articulation, then a child might have trouble identifying the head uniquely and
prosodifies the cluster as hetero-syllabic, with C1 as an appendix and C2 the head. This
difference in prosodification accounts for the variation in reduction strategies.
         There is a difference with Goad and Rose’s account. They take prosodic structure as part
of the input. There is no need to do this in my account. Constraints evaluating sonority
relationships as well as place of articulation determine which consonant surfaces.
         So far, I have explained the differences between between-child variable and between-
child stable clusters refering to sonority and place of articulation. Recall the three implicational
relationships between clusters that were discussed in chapter 4. The first one is /sl/ versus /sn/
and /sm/; the second is /fl/ and /xl/ versus /sl/; the third one is /kn/ versus /sn/ and /sm/. In the
discussion below, I discuss in more detail the explanation for the implicational relationships seen
in these clusters.


•   /sl/ versus /sm/, /sn/
Recall the implicational relationship discussed in chapter 4 is: if /sl/ is realised as C2, C2 is
realised in /s/-nasal clusters, but not vice versa. The /s/-sonorant clusters in Dutch are /sm/, /sn/,
/sl/, and /sw/. Recall that /sw/ is the only between-child stable cluster (always realised as [s]). In
(5.8a), I compare the structural properties of all /s/-sonorant clusters (/sm/, /sn/, /sl/, and /sw/),
with respect to sonority distance and place of articulation.


(5.8a)                         /sm/            /sn/            /sl/           /sw/
         sonority distance     1               1               2              4
         place                 different       same            same           different
                                                                                                      121


As can be seen in (5.8a), cluster /sw/ is essentially dissimilar regarding sonority distance and has
dissimilar place structure. The cluster makes a good onset. Therefore, children prosodify this
cluster as a tauto-syllabic onset with C1 as head and C2 as dependent, and thus realise the head,
[s]. The consonants of cluster /sl/ have similar structure with respect to sonority (the sonority
distance between the fricative in C1 and the lateral in C2 is two), as is the case for clusters /sm/
and /sn/, although the latter clusters have a sonority distance of one. The consonants in /s/-nasal
clusters are thus closer in sonority than the consonants in /sl/. If a child follows head 2 pattern in
/sl/ ([s] is appendix and [l] is head), then the same child will be likely to follow head 2 pattern for
/s/-nasal clusters, since this cluster is even closer in sonority structure.
         Regarding the difference between /sn/ and /sm/, I suggest that there might be another
factor that plays a role in selecting the head, namely the factor of labiality being a better head.
The labial nasal is a better head than dependent (Van der Torre personal communication). In
(5.8b), I compare the structural properties of all /s/-sonorant clusters (/sm/, /sn/, /sl/, and /sw/),
with respect to sonority distance and place of articulation, showing the possible influence of the
preference to have dissimilar place features (indicated on the second line: "place") and the
possible influence of having a labial in the head position (indicated on the third line: "labial").


(5.8b)                           /sm/           /sn/             /sl/          /sw/
         sonority distance       1              1                2             4
         place                   different      same             same          different
         labial                  m              -                -             w



One could question whether labiality of C2 in cluster /sw/ plays a role. I propose that this
influence is overshadowed by the influence of the sonority distance between the consonants in
these clusters (i.e., a distance of 4 for /sw/ in the child’s decision of which consonant to select as
head). If the sonority distance is three or more then C1 is selected as head (with C2 in the
dependent position).
         Notice that I mention two possible analyses regarding the influence of the feature place,
namely the preference for adjacent dissimilar place features and the preference to have the place
feature labial in the head position. There is no empirical evidence available which of the two
factors is of most influence since /s/-sonorant clusters in Dutch are either /s/-coronal (adjacent
place features) or /s/-labial.
                                                                                                                        122




•    /fl/, /xl/ versus /sl/
Recall from chapter 4 that /fl/ is realised as [l] by child 2, and M9, and that cluster /xl/ is realised
as [l] by child 2. Although there are only a few cases in which a child realises /fl/ and/or /xl/ as
[l], I included these clusters in the group of between-child variable. The most striking aspect in
reduction pattern is that both child 2 and child M9 realise the sonorant in the other /s/-sonorant
clusters (except /sw/). Recall from chapter 4 that I noted an implicational relation between /fl/
and /xl/ on the one hand and other /s/-sonorant clusters on the other: realising C2 in /fl/ and /xl/
implies realising C2 in /sl/ (and thus in /sn/ and /sm/), but not vice versa. Child 2 realises /sl/ as
[l]; child M9 realises 2 out of 18 /sl/ clusters as [s] (11%) and 16 as [l] (89%), and formally does
not meet the criterion for a consistent realisation pattern (i.e., 91% and higher). Recall
furthermore from Table 4.21 in chapter 4, that both children realise /fr/ as [f] (there is insufficient
data for /xr/) In (5.9), I compare the structural differences in sonority distance and place between
consonants in clusters /sl/, /fl/ and /fr/.


(5.9)                                 /sl/               /fl/               /fr/
         sonority distance            2                  2                  3
         place                        same               different          different
         labial                       -                  f                  f



As can be seen from (5.9), /fr/ has dissimilar structure with respect to sonority and place of
articulation. Head-dependent structuring for children in the head stage is relatively easy: C1 is
chosen as head and thus this consonant is realised. Recall from chapter 4 that /fr/ is stable.
Clusters /sl/ and /fl/ are variable. The sonority distance in these clusters is less than that in /fr/.
However, if a child realises /fl/ as [l] it will be likely to realise /sl/ as [l]. This is explained by the
fact that /sl/ has similar place structure while /fl/ does not, or by the fact that /fl/ has a left-edge
labial, while /sl/ does not. Since /sl/ is a less good onset than /fl/, /sl/ is more likely to be
prosodified as appendix-head.35




35
  It could be that the feature [labial] is more likely to be a head than the feature [coronal]. In order to sort this out,
one would need more robust data on /xl/.
                                                                                                               123


•    /kn/ versus /sn/, /sm/
Recall from (5.2) that /kn/, /sm/, and /sn/ are all between-child variable. Looking at the sonority
shape of cluster /kn/, we see that a plosive is followed by a nasal. Linguists claim that many
languages do not allow for obstruent-nasal clusters in onsets, since these clusters are ill-formed
with respect to sonority distance (see Clements 1990, Selkirk 1984, Van der Hulst 1984).36
Recall that in chapter 4, I note that there is an implicational relationship between /s/-nasal
clusters and /kn/. If a child realises /kn/ as [n], then, s/he also realises /s/-nasal clusters as [nasal],
but not vice versa (child 12, M5, M6, M10). Recall furthermore that other clusters starting with
/k/ are between-child stable (e.g. /kl/ and /kr/).37 In (5.10), I compare the structural differences in
sonority distance and place between consonants in clusters /sm/, /sn/, /kn/ and /kr/.


(5.10)                             /sm/              /sn/             /kn/              /kr/
         sonority distance         1                 1                2                 4
         place                     different         same             different         different
         labial                    m                 -                -                 -



As is seen in (5.10), prosodification in cluster /kr/ is relatively easy since this cluster has
dissimilar structure with respect to sonority and place. The child prosodifies this cluster as head-
dependent, and thus realises [k]. The consonants in clusters /sm/ and /sn/ are close in sonority
distance, closer than the consonants of cluster /kn/. This explains the implicational relationship
between /kn/ versus /sn/ and /sm/. Children who realise C2 as head (and C1 as an appendix) in
/kn/ will realise C2 as head (and C1 as appendix) in /sn/ since fricative-nasal clusters are closer
in sonority than plosive-nasal clusters. Although cluster /sm/ has dissimilar place structure, the
fact that the sonority distance is small together with the fact of labiality being a better head
explains why C2 is so often selected as head. Thus, if C1 is syllabified as an appendix when two
consonants are further apart in sonority it will also be syllabified as an appendix when the
consonants are closer in sonority.




36
   Some linguists assume an extrasyllabic position for /k/ in /kn/ in adult Dutch (e.g., Van der Hulst 1984,
Trommelen 1984). Others argue against this (e.g., Grijzenhout 1998). My analysis assumes that [k] is in an appendix
in /kn/ clusters.
37
   There is one example in which a child reduces /kl/ to [l] in a consistent way. See section 5.2.5.
                                                                                                    124


In what follows, I discuss clusters with flat or reversed sonority (reversed: decreasing rather than
increasing sonority). I follow Goad and Rose in claiming that in the head stage children generally
know that /s/-obstruent clusters are hetero-syllabic. However, I go further and claim that children
might get confused in cases where these clusters have similar place structure. The result is that
the head is chosen by default: the left most segment is chosen as the head.


•   /sx/
So far, I have left out cluster /sx/ from the discussion. This cluster is realised as both [s] and [x].
Out of the 27 children with at least five reduction data for this cluster, 16 realise [x] and 11
realise [s] (see Table 4.21 in chapter 4). This cluster is thus highly variable. In terms of sonority
distance this cluster is the most similar (i.e., fricative-fricative). Therefore, many children
experience difficulty in selecting the head in this cluster. In the sonority stage, relative sonority
does not give a solution. I propose that default left-headedness is the result since most clusters
are left headed. In the head stage some children notice that there is no sonority distance between
the two consonants, with the result that they restructure the cluster and chose C2 as the head akin
to the other clusters with small sonority distances. This assumption implies that a realisation [s]
for /sx/ precedes a realisation [x]. This was attested for child 4 (see section 4.2.2.4.2).


•   /st/
This cluster is usually realised as [t]. Recall from chapter 4 that there are some cases in which [s]
is realised for this cluster (children 3, 20, M5). I claim that the variability of /st/ might be caused
by its similar sonority and place structure. When children follow the sonority pattern, they realise
[t] since /t/ is less sonorous than /s/. I argue that in the head stage, the selection of the head might
change for some children. Some children (those who do not change) will select C2 as head and
realise [t], others (those who do change) will select C1 as head and realise [s]. In (5.11), I
compare the structural properties of /sp/, /sk/, and /st/ with respect to sonority distance and place.


(5.11)                 /sp/            /sk/            /st/
sonority distance       1 (reversed)    1 (reversed)    1 (reversed)
place                   different       different      same
labial                 p               -               -
                                                                                                   125


As can be seen in (5.11), clusters /sp/, /sk/, and /st/ have a reversed sonority distance of 1.
Clusters /sp/ and /sk/ have dissimilar place structure. In the sonority stage children select C2 as
the head since this consonant is the less sonorous one compared to C1. Cluster /st/ has similar
place structure. In the head stage, children notice that /st/ with similar place structure is different
from /sp/ and /sk/, and for some children this might be a problem. These children select the head
on the basis of position: the left most consonant (C1) is selected as the head by default. Note that
another option is that the realisation [s] for /st/ involves fusion. This might be the case for
children 20 and M5, who also merge cluster /sp/ (realised as [f]). I will leave this for future
research.
       In summary, the difference between stable and variable clusters and the implicational
relationships in reduction strategies between clusters has its origin in the structural relationship
between the two consonants in the cluster. If a relationship between two consonants in a cluster
is characterized by similar structure in terms of sonority distance and place, then this can result
in different prosodifications. The further apart the consonants in a cluster are in terms of sonority
and place structure (=dissimilar structure), the better onset the cluster forms, and the easier it is
to prosodify the cluster as head-dependent. The closer together the consonants in a cluster are in
terms of sonority (and place) (=similar structure), the less good onset the cluster forms, the more
difficult it is to prosodify as head-dependent. As a result of this, some children will prosodify
(some of) these clusters with similar structure as appendix-head. Clusters with flat or reversed
sonority are generally prosodified as hetero-syllabic in the head stage. However, in the case of
similar place structure children might experience problems sorting out and select heads by a
default rule: the left most segment is the head.
       In the next section, I account for the individual reduction patterns, presented in chapter 4.


5.2.5 Explaining individual patterns in reduction
Out of the 39 children given in Table 4.21 in chapter 4 (section 4.4.), 22 children always realise
the less sonorous segment (children 4, 5, 6, 9, 11, 14, 15, 16, 17, 18, 19, 21, 23, 27, 28, 30, 31,
32, 33, 34, M7, M11). These children might be in the sonority stage and select the head
according to relative sonority (sonority pattern), or they might have started to make full use of
the sonority scale and be in the head stage. In the latter case, they select C1 as head and C2 as
                                                                                                  126


dependent (head pattern 1), except in the sonority reversed clusters /sp/, /st/, /sk/, where C2 is the
head.
        I have left out some children who always realise the less sonorous segment with one
exception. First, child 25 realises the less sonorous segment everywhere except in /dr/ (although
there are only six reduction data for this cluster available). However, I think that this is a lexical
effect, since this child realises all target words with /dr/ correctly except for /dr/ie three, a
counting word that is always spoken in combination with counting words one and two. This child
always counted using the same string of words: [entweri] one, two, three. Earlier we saw that
there is a significant effect in the condition sentence initial versus sentence medial for cluster
/dr/. I think that this might be the cause of realising [r] for target word /dr/ie. Second, there are
two children who always realise the less sonorous segment except for cluster /st/ (child 3, and
child 20). These children select the head by default (see discussion above).
        There are 25 children (children 3, 4, 5, 6, 9, 11, 14, 15, 16, 17, 18, 19, 20, 21, 23, 25, 27,
28, 30, 31, 32, 33, 34, M7, M11) who always follow either the sonority pattern, or -if they are
more advanced and started to use their knowledge about the structural relationship between the
two consonants in a cluster- they follow head pattern 1 for all clusters (except for /sp/, /sk/ and
possibly except for /st/, /sx/). They prosodify the clusters as tauto-syllabic with C1 as head and
C2 as dependent.
        I have not yet explained the reduction patterns of 14 children (children 1, 2, 8, 10, 12, 13,
22, 29, M1, M5, M6, M8, M9, M10). These children do not realise the less sonorant segment in
all clusters, rather in some, the more sonorous segment is realised. The realisation patterns for
these children are repeated in Table 5.9.

A few comments have to be made at this point that involve the cases in which a child realises the
second consonant in clusters that were defined as stable in chapter 4. As is seen in Table 5.9,
clusters /kl/, /xr/, /tw/, and /kw/ have incidental cases of C2 consistent realisation. I have defined
/tw/, /kl/, and /xr/ as between-child stable, since there were many cases in which the first
consonant was realised in these clusters (between 90 and 91%). I have left /kw/ out of the
discussion, since I assume that there is a lexical effect in cases where this cluster is realised as
[w] (see section 4.3.3 in chapter 4), I will discuss the individual exceptional realisation patterns
of the clusters named above in more detail.
           First, regarding clusters /kw/ and /tw/. As is seen in table 5.9, child 10 realises clusters
/tw/ and /kw/ as [w] and child 22 realises cluster /kw/ as [w]. I claim that these three cases where
C2 is consistently realised in either or both of the plosive-glide clusters (e.g. /tw/ and /kw/) are
best analyzed as a lexical effect (see section 4.4.).
           Second, regarding cluster /kl/: Child 8 realises /kl/ consistently as [l] (out of 11 C1,C2
reduction cases, [k] is realised once and [l] is realised ten times). It is not clear to me why child 8
realises /kl/ as [l]. This is the only child that realises /kl/ as [l]. This child seems to have a
                                                                                                                       128


preference for [l] since more obstruent-liquid clusters are realised as [l] (e.g. /tr/, /dr/, /kr/, /xl/,
/xr/: see section 5.3.3).38
         Third, cluster /xr/: Child M8 realises /xr/ as [r]. This is the only child that realises C2 in
this cluster. /sn/, /sl/, /kn/, /fl/ are realised as C1. For /sm/, /xl/ there are not sufficient data to
analyze. Unlike most children, this child has no problems realising [r] in clusters. She realises
almost all obstruent-[r] clusters correctly (/pr/ (16/19), /br/ (16/17), /kr/ (13/16), /fr/ (24/33), /xr/
(16/33)). As I have said before, results for cluster /xr/ should be handled with caution, since the
underlying representation of /xr/ might not be a cluster.
         Leaving aside the exceptional cases of C2 realisations in /tw/, /kw/, /kl/, /xr/, I now

As is seen in Table 5.10, children 10, 13, 22, 29, M5, M6 are consistent in following the head
pattern 2 for whatever clusters they have or they show variation. These children have trouble
with the fact that some clusters have similar sonority distance and similar place. Child 1 follows
a head pattern 2 for /sm/ since this cluster is similar in sonority and has a labial as C2, and makes
[s] as an appendix (realising [m]). The child follows the head pattern 1 for /sn/ and /sl/, realising
[s] in both clusters as the head. /sl/ is further apart with respect to distance in sonority compared
to /s/-nasal clusters and /sn/ does not have a labial in C2. Child 2 follows a head pattern 2 for
/sm/, /sl/, /fl/, /xl/ and follows the head pattern 1 for /kn/. Cluster /sn/ is listed as variable. I have
not discussed a possible account for the (few) instances of within-child variable clusters. For
cluster /sn/, there are eight reduction data. [s] is realised once; [n] is realised seven times. So it
seems that this child is following the head pattern 2 for /sn/ as well. However, since the child is
in the process of acquiring internal structure, there might be incidental cases where the head
pattern 1 is followed. Variation might thus be an effect of an intermediate stage, in which the
child struggles with selecting the head. I will leave this issue for future research. In the next
section, I conclude the findings regarding the theoretical account for reduction patterns.
                                                                                                  130


5.2.6   Conclusion
In this chapter, I discussed the implications of the results on variation for a theoretical account of
reduction strategies. The fact that within-child variation is highly limited implies that reduction
strategies are systematic. However, sonority as used in the literature discussed in chapter 2
cannot on its own explain the cases in which a child systematically realises the more sonorous
segment in a cluster, nor can it explain why clusters within the same sonority group do not
always pattern similarly regarding i) being either between-child stable or between-child variable
clusters, and ii) implicational relationships. Instead, cluster reduction strategies are to be
explained with a much finer tuned notion of sonority in which the sonority distance between two
consonants in a cluster plays an important role in selecting heads. In the initial stage (the sonority
stage) the child does not yet uses detailed information about sonority distance, but selects the
head based on relative sonority, choosing the less sonorous consonant as head. In the head stage,
the child starts to make full use of the sonority scale and evaluates the relationship between the
two consonants in a cluster. The further apart the two consonants are with respect to sonority and
place of articulation, the better onset they form, and the easier it is to prosodify the clusters as
head-dependent (head pattern 1) (realising the head). The closer the relationship between the two
consonants is, the less good onset, and the more difficult it is to prosodify the cluster as an
(tauto-syllabic) onset. Clusters with similar structure are therefore more likely to be prosodified
as hetero-syllabic (appendix-head) (head pattern 2). Variation in reduction strategies is thus
explained by a difference in prosodification, which is caused by the fact that children start to
make full use of the structural relationship between two consonants with respect to sonority
distance and place of articulation. Notice that the prosodic structure is not a primitive (i.e.
present in the underlying representation), but derives from relationships between the two Cs. I
have thus started from Goad and Rose’s account, recognizing different stages and the notions of
head and dependent. I have argued that /s/ is not arbitrarily stipulated as an appendix at the head
stage, but that there are conditions under which C2 is the head, forcing C1 into an appendix. As
the child develops, s/he must learn to restrict possible appendices.


5.3     Results using other definitions of cluster reduction
So far, I have discussed variation in reduction patterns using a strict definition of cluster
reduction, namely that one of the input consonants is realised. I found that there is little within-
                                                                                                    131


individual variation in reduction strategies. Thus, in by far the most cases, the individual child is
consistent in his/her reduction strategy. In this section, I look at within-child variation using
other definitions of cluster reduction, namely when the realised consonant forms a natural class
(Nat) with one of the input consonants. Recall that Fikkert (1994) discussed reduction patterns in
terms of sonority classes rather than in terms of target consonants. For example, a realisation [l]
for cluster /sn/ is listed by Fikkert as an instance of C2 realisation. An interesting question at this
point is whether the child’s reduction strategy can indeed be classified as realising the obstruent
or the sonorant at a certain point in time, rather than realising target C1 or C2.
         In what follows, I discuss variation results using two different definitions. The first
definition I will refer to as Natvoice. This is when the output consonant forms a natural class
with the input consonant regarding voice specification (repeated in 5.12b). For example, in this
definition both outputs [p]ook and [b]ook for /sp/ook ghost would be counted as C2 realisation
(proces 2).
         The second definition, which I will refer to as Natson, is when the output consonant
forms a natural class with one of the input consonants with respect to sonority value (repeated in
5.12c). For example, outputs [l]ok and [n]ok for /kl/ok clock will both count as C2 realisations
(process 2; see chapter 4), while outputs [k]ok and [t]ok will count as C1 (process 1). I will make
a distinction between fricatives and stops. For example, [h]ook for /sp/ook ghost in which a
fricative is realised, counts as C1, but [t]ook, in which a plosive is realised, counts as C2.
Outputs for cluster /sx/ will not differ from other definitions since there is a fricative in both
positions.


(5.12)   Different definitions of cluster reduction
(a)      Process. 1,2:    Realised consonant must be identical to one of the input consonants
(b)      NatVoice:        Same as process 1,2, disregarding voice specification
(c)      NatSon:          Realised consonant must have same sonority value as input consonant


Only the target database (see section 4.1.1) was adjusted according to the two new definitions
discussed above (i.e., Natson and Natvoice). Thus, the discussion in this section is based on the
database of only the target words, regardless of condition.
         By varying the definition of cluster reduction, outputs originally classified as process 3
(C1C2 is realised as Cother) change class. Recall from section 4.2 that the class of Cother realisations
                                                                                                     132


represents 7.4% of the target word database. Regarding the difference in C1,C2 reduction data
between Process 1,2 and Natvoice, there were 169 instances of reduction that were originally
grouped in process 3 but were regrouped in C1 or C2 in the Natvoice definition, which is an
increase of 2.7%. An example is output [p]oem for /bl/oem flower, which was originally counted
as process 3 (Cother realisation) and reassigned to process 1 (C1 realisation) in the Natvoice
definition. Regarding the difference in C1,C2 reduction data between Process 1,2 and Natson,
there were 1315 instances of reduction that were originally grouped in process 3 but regrouped in
C1 or C2 in the Natson definition, which is an increase of 20.6%. An example is output [l]ein for
/tr/ein train, which was originally counted as process 3 (Cother realisation) reassigned to process 2
(C2 realisation) in the Natson definition. In Table 5.11, an overview is given of the distribution of
data in Process 1, 2, and 3 (in %) for the three definitions of cluster reduction: Process 1,2,
Natvoice and Natson.

As can be seen in Table 5.11, the difference in percentages in data in process 3 between the three

condition almost all data originally classified as process 3 are now classified as process 1 or
process 2. For example, output [t] for input /sp/ is classified as C2, forming a natural class with
the oral stop in the input.
          In section 5.3.1, I discuss variable and stable clusters across data. In section 5.3.2, I
discuss individual reduction strategies, using the 8-criterion (see 4.2). In section 5.3.3, I discuss
the Natson results in more detail. Section 5.3.4 presents the conclusions.

Focusing on the difference within consistent realisations, we see from Table 5.16b that there are

is from child 2, who consistently realises /sp/ as [f]. There are fewer instances of C1 realisation
in the Natson definition for clusters /sn/ and /dr/. Cluster /sw/ has more cases of consistent C1
realisation in the Natson definition. Focusing on within-child variable cases, it is striking that
across clusters, there are many more cases of variation. For example, cluster /dr/ is in 18 cases

the realisations of /dr/, there are many instances of [l] and [d] by the same child, resulting in
