No differences are found for children 4, 7, 17, 18, 19, 21, 23, 24, 25, 26, 27, 28, 29, 34. These
children have an equal number of variable clusters, regardless of the fact that some children have
more cases to analyze. Children who have one more variable cluster in the Natson definition are
children 1, 5, 22, 30, 32, and 33. Children with two more variable clusters are children 6, 9, 13,
15, 16, 20, and 31. Children with three more variable clusters are children 2, 3, 10, and 12.
                                                                                                           139


Children with five more variable clusters are children 11 and 14. The biggest differences in
variable clusters between Process 1,2 and the Natson definition is found in child 8. This child has
six more variable clusters in the Natson definition.
        Looking at the reduction data of child 8 in the Natson definition, there are 14 cases to
analyze. Clusters /sp/, /sx/, /pl/, /br/ are stable, whereas clusters /sk/, /xl/, /tw/, /tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/,
/kn/, /fr/, /xr/, are variable. /sk/, /xl/, and /tw/ were also variable in the Process 1,2 definition. We
see that together with C1, [l] is realised in many obstruent-liquid clusters, such as /tr/, /dr/, /kl/,
/kr/, /xl/, /xr/. Because this child realises singleton /r/ as [l], one could claim that all [l]
realisations in clusters are actually C2 realisations. However, by doing this, it remains unclear
why /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/ are usually realised as C1 (/pr/, /br/), or as correct (/pl/, /bl/). Hardly any
or none of the realisations of [l] are found in labial plosive-liquid clusters /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/. It
seems like this child has a preference for realising the target segment that is a plosive and has the
labial feature. If there is no such segment in the target, a coronal segment that is not a continuant
is inserted (e.g. [l], [t], [d]).
        In summary, we have seen that most reduction strategies become less consistent if data
are collapsed across sonorants and across obstruents (Natson definition). This leads to the
conclusion that reduction strategies should not be analyzed in terms of retaining the sonorant or
obstruent. Instead, a more fine grained notion of sonority is involved in reduction strategies.
        In the next section, I will conclude the findings in using Natvoice and Natson.


5.3.4   Conclusion
In section 5.3, I discussed the results of the study using other definitions of cluster reduction,
namely Natvoice and Natson. Within the former definition the consonant realised must be
identical to one of the input consonants, except that voice specification is not relevant. Within
the latter definition, the consonant realised must share the same sonority value as the input
consonant, to be counted as equivalent to the input (collapsed across all sonorants versus
collapsed across all obstruents). I considered the target word database, using the 8-criterion. I
showed that the results in between-child and within-child variation using the Natvoice definition
are similar to the results using the strict definition of cluster reduction. I concluded that it seems
that voice specification in the target consonant does not trigger a change in the direction of a
realisation strategy.
                                                                                                   140


        Using the Natson definition, we see different results from Process 1,2 vs. the Natvoice
definition. Most importantly, there is more within-child variation in the Natson definition (25%
in the Natson versus 15% in Process 1,2 and in the Natvoice definition). A difference is seen in
20 out of the 34 children. Thus, when data are collapsed across the sonority classes, sonorants
versus obstruents, patterns change. The results argue that collapsing across sonorants is not the
right analysis.
        In the next section, I summarize the findings of this chapter.


5.4     Conclusion
In this chapter, I discussed two issues. First, I discussed the theoretical implications of the results
of cluster reduction, as presented in chapter 4. Second, I compared the results using different
definitions of cluster reduction. The effect of two other definitions of cluster reduction was
discussed.
        Regarding theoretical implications I draw the following conclusions. First is the
conclusion that reduction strategies are systematic since within-child variation is very limited.
Second is the conclusion that sonority by itself cannot explain the cases in which a child
systematically realises the more sonorous segment in a cluster. Third is the conclusion that
discussing reduction patterns of clusters in terms of sonority groups is not always legitimate
since clusters within one group do not always pattern similarly regarding i) being either between-
child stable or between-child variable clusters; ii) the way they are realised in individual
reduction patterns. In addition, variation seems to be related to within sonority divisions. Fourth
is the conclusion that between-child variation and implicational relationships between clusters
are explained by a difference in selecting the head during the head stage. In this stage, the child
has learned more about the relationship between two consonants with respect to sonority distance
and place of articulation. The further apart the two consonants are with respect to sonority and
place, the better onset the consonants form, the easier it is to prosodify the clusters as head-
dependent (head pattern 1). The closer the relationship between the two consonants is, the less
good onset, the more difficult it is to prosodify the cluster as an onset. Clusters with similar
structure are therefore more likely to be prosodified as hetero-syllabic (appendix-head) (head
pattern 2).
                                                                                                141


       Regarding the issue of using different definitions of cluster reduction, variation was
examined using two other definitions of reduction. Under both definitions some reduction data
originally stored as process 3 (Cother) were re-grouped in either process 1 (C1) or process 2 (C2).
The first definition discussed is called Natvoice. Within this definition, the realised consonant
must be identical to one of the input consonants, but voice specification is not relevant. It was
found that results are similar compared to the results using the definition in which voice
specification is relevant. The percentage of within-variation is essentially the same. I claim that
voice quality does not change the direction in realisation strategies. The second definition is
called Natson. Within this definition, the realised consonant must share the same sonority value
as the input consonant. The percentage of within-individual variation is much higher in Natson
compared to that in the strict definition of cluster reduction (i.e., Process 1,2). The results show
that collapsing across sonority class (sonorants versus obstruents only) leads to far greater
variation, and does not illuminate the limited kinds of variability found when a more strict
definition of sonority is used.
       In the next chapter, I present the results of a pilot study on the perception of word-initial
consonants clusters.
                                                                                                            142



Chapter 6 The perception of word-initial consonant clusters


6.1     Introduction
Probably one of the most important issues in child language research is the cause of errors in
production and the status of the child’s phonology (see Dresher 1998 for a detailed overview on
current issues in child phonology and phonological theory). Do children make mistakes in
production because of their child grammar, or because of bad motor control? Could it be that
they initially cannot discriminate between sounds and thus make production errors because of
problems in perception? Are there other reasons for production errors? Velleman (1988, p.221/2)
names three possible causes for production errors in the child’s language, summarized in (6.1).

(6.1)   1      Perception
               failure to recognize that a pair of sounds is contrastive in the adult language due to faulty
               perception; the child does not represent these sounds as contrastive.
        2      Grammar
               failure to represent the pair as distinct representations (URs), due to organizational
               limitations that restrict the number of contrasts that can be represented in the
               phonological system at a particular time.
        3      Motor control
               failure to produce the pair distinctively enough to be perceived by adults as distinctive.


Taking the minimal pair thum and fum for example; under scenario 1, a child simply cannot
perceive the difference between phonemes // and /f/ and has one underlying representation, say
/f/um, for both thum and fum. The same underlying representation may result under scenario 2
because the child has not yet built enough featural structure to make this contrast. Scenario 3
reveals that the child has two underlying representations, but the production of the contrast is not
audible for the adult listener (see also Kornfeld 1971).
        From the summary above it is obvious that in order to gain insight into the causes of
mistakes in production, we need to investigate the child’s perceptual abilities. There may be a
difference between the child’s perceptual ability to distinguish sounds on the one hand and to
distinguish phonemes, or contrasts, on the other. Very young infants are perfectly able to
distinguish speech sounds such as ba versus pa even when these sounds are not present in their
target language (for an overview of the literature on sound discrimination see Goodman et al.
                                                                                                  143


1994). However, somewhat older children (between 18 and 36 months) are reported to have
problems in discrimination tasks, where the child has to distinguish minimal pairs in the
language they are exposed to, such as bear versus pear (e.g. Barton 1976, Brown 1997, Edwards
1974, Eilers and Oller 1976, Templin 1957, Werker and Polka 1993). I will limit the discussion
here to the child’s perceptual abilities in real language (bear versus pear) as opposed to nonsense
syllables (ba versus pa). How accurate are the perceptual abilities of children? It is widely
acknowledged by linguists that the child understands more words than s/he is able to produce
(e.g. Ingram 1989, Matthei 1989, Menn and Matthei 1992, Menn and Stoel-Gammon 1995,
Smith 1973). However, linguists do not agree on whether the child's perception of phonemes is
fully present before s/he begins to speak or whether these abilities develop gradually. Brown
(1997, p.87) distinguishes between two hypotheses, the Building hypothesis, which states that
the ability to distinguish phonemic contrasts improves gradually over the course of acquisition,
and the Pruning hypothesis, which states that the child is able to distinguish a native contrast
from the beginning of language development.
       Some linguists assume that very young children already have full perception of phonemes
before they start speaking (e.g. Barton, 1976, Shvachkin 1973, Smith 1973, Stampe 1969),
supporting the Pruning hypothesis. Perhaps the most convincing evidence for this hypothesis
comes from the numerous reports of children who perceive a difference that they do not, at least
to adult ears, make themselves. Correct perceptual judgements despite incorrect pronunciation
(such as the well known “fis phenomenon” (Berko and Brown 1960)) reveal that the child must
have a clear idea of the underlying representation of words he has in his lexicon. The child
realises the word /IÖ/ as [IÖV] but does not accept the realisation [IÖV] from another speaker as

referring to /IÖ/. The child objects, saying something like: “No, not [IÖV], [IÖV]!” Smith (1973) for
example, notices that his son Amahl could not distinguish the minimal pairs mouth and mouse in
his production (the child produces mou[s]e for both forms). However, the child objected to his
father's imitation of his own speech errors (the father says mou[s]e referring to mouth). Smith
takes this as evidence that the child must be able to perceive speech sounds that he does not
articulate correctly, and thus that the child's representations must be adult-like. Smith further
assumes that perception is mastered before the child begins to speak: The child does not begin to
speak until he has learnt to perceive at least the majority of the contrasts present in the adult
language (Smith 1973, p.134).
                                                                                                    144


         Other evidence for perfect underlying representations comes from speech production.
First, there are systematic correspondences between adult and child forms. Phonological
processes such as substitution and syllable restructuring are widely seen across child languages.
Substitutions by children are quite systematic across children and languages (Bernhardt and
Stemberger 1998, Ingram 1989) and the systematic character of these correspondences suggests
that the child has adult underlying representations, for example, the substitution of stops for
target fricatives. Such a process tends to be across all (or almost all) words. Second, changes in
terms of development are also typically characterized as across-the-board. In other words, a
phonological process could stop applying rather suddenly and across the entire domain in a
child's language. For example, at one stage a child is fronting all dorsal stops (/k/) to coronal
stops ([t]). At a later stage the child realises the dorsal stops correctly (/k/ is realised as [k]) and
distinguishes between these segments and target coronal stops which are realised, as before, as
coronal (/t/ is realised as [t]). The end of a process applying to the entire domain reveals that the
child’s underlying structures are akin to adult structures. Thus, the fact that at a certain point in
time some words change in the way they are realised while other words do not change reveals
that this is because these former words have an underlying pattern that is distinct from the latter
words.
         However, other linguists have argued that the two pieces of evidence given above do not
establish that the child’s production is based on competence. For example Hale and Reiss (1998)
question whether the so-called phonological processes in child language are not in fact phonetic
processes, given in scenario C in (6.2) for example of Sue versus shoe.


(6.2)    Three views of the V merger in child speech (Hale and Reiss 1998)
                                   A                B                 C
                                   Sue     shoe     Sue      shoe     Sue      shoe
Underlying representation          /V     V      V            V      
                                                                                      Phonology
Output of grammar                  [V@     >V@      >V@      >V@      >V@      >@
                                                                                      Performance
Output of body                     [s]     [s]      [s]      [s]      [s]      [s]
                                                                                                                       145


In scenario A, a child does not have the contrast in the underlying representation, thus perception
is imperfect. Neither does the child have the contrast in the output of the grammar and thus not in
the output of the body (i.e. his/her speech). Most linguists assume scenario B, in which
perception is perfect. Thus the contrast between // and /s/ is perceived, but the child's grammar

leads to a neutralization in the output of the grammar; thus the contrast is neutralized in the
child’s speech. In scenario C, the lack of contrast between shoe and Sue is caused by a problem
in performance rather than in phonology, i.e. competence. Hale and Reiss argue that the child’s
errors in production are caused by motor control problems (performance problems).
         Others (e.g. Dinnsen 1996, Ferguson and Farwell 1975, Macken 1980) question whether
changes in the child's language really occur across the board, as suggested by the Pruning
hypothesis, or if there is lexical diffusion. It has been reported that changes may sometimes
affect some words, but not other words within the same phonological domain. For example, a
child may have stopped fronting of velars to coronals in the word cat ([k]at), but still front the
velar in the word coat ([t]oat). Assuming the substitutions are not context-dependant, this would
indicate that the child did not have the same underlying representation for /k/ in both words.
Recall the few cases of lexical effects, reported in chapter 4, in which some children realised C1
in one word, but C2 in another word. For example, child 8 realised cluster /sk/ as [s] in target
word /sk/ieen ski, but realised this cluster at the same point in time as [k] in target word
/sk/ippiebal skippy ball.


In opposition to the Pruning hypothesis, or the view that full perception is present from the onset
of the child's speech production, other linguists argue that phonemic perception develops
gradually, supporting the Building hypothesis (Brown 1997, Edwards 1974, Eilers and Oller
1976, Strange and Broen 1980, Templin 1957, Vihman 1996). In her dissertation, Brown (1997)
discusses the results of one of her experiments in which the perception of phonemic contrasts in
English was tested for (see also Brown and Matthews 1997). In this study she tested the
perception of singleton vs. singleton contrasts (e.g. key versus pee) in 60 observations with 18
subjects from 1;3 to 2;4 years old. 39 The overall performance ranges from 48% (random) to 92%


39
  Brown distinguishes between six items, each targets a featural contrast. There are ten trials per item (which leads
to the total number of 60 observations). Brown uses the criterion 8/10 specified in each item in order to determine
whether a child has acquired the contrast. Thus, out of the ten trials, a child must at least point correctly eight times.
                                                                                                                     146


(near perfect). The results lead Brown to the conclusion that perception is perfect for some
children, i.e. those with near perfect scores, but not for others, i.e. those with scores around 50%.
These results support the Building hypothesis:


In fact the finding that some children are capable of distinguishing all of the contrasts under
examination while others are unable to discriminate any of them is enough to indicate that this
capacity is not present from the initial stages of phonological development
(Brown 1997, p. 109).


If the perceptual abilities of children develop gradually, to what extent are errors in production
caused by poor perception? In other words, how exactly are scores in perception and production
correlated? Some researchers claim that some perceptual errors may lead to production errors
(e.g. Eilers and Oller 1976, Locke 1980, Strange and Broen 1980, Velleman 1988, Waterson
1971). Velleman (1988) tested the perception and production of voiceless fricatives by using a
picture pointing task, a production task and acoustic analysis. She found a correlation between
production and perception scores for //, but not for /s/. More specifically, for // she found that

children have problems in both producing and perceiving the segment //. This is evidence for
the fact that the problems in production are perceptually based. With /s/, on the other hand, she
found that children have problems in producing it but they do not have problems in perceiving
this segment. The fact that /s/ is relatively easy to discriminate from other fricatives in
combination with the fact that it is difficult to produce is evidence that, unlike //, a child must
have /s/ in the underlying representation and the difficulties in producing it are due to motor
control problems. Furthermore, Velleman discusses acoustic comparisons between substitutions
of // and /s/ as evidence for different underlying representations of // and /s/ in child language.
She distinguishes between so-called phonetic substitutions and perceptually based substitutions
(see also Kornfeld 1971, Locke 1980). In the former sort of substitution the error occurs at the
phonetic level; in the latter the error occurs at the phonemic level. Assuming that // might not

be in underlying representations in the language of children who cannot perceive this segment,
Velleman predicts that there should be no acoustic difference between an output x that is a

It turned out that across children (18) and items (6), there are 60 cases (out of 108) only in which the 8/10 criterion
                                                                                                                        147


substitution for // and this output as realisation of /x/. For example, if a child substitutes [f] for

//, then there is no acoustic difference between [f] (/f/) and [f], in which the latter is an output of

//. The results showed that differences in substitutions of [] for /s/ were significantly

acoustically different, while this was not the case in substitutions of [f] or [s] for //. The results
of the perception task and the acoustic analysis lead Velleman to the conclusion that substitutions
for // are grounded in non-adult representations, while substitutions for /s/ could be caused by a

motor control problem for some children.
         Eilers and Oller (1976) tested 14 children between 1;10 and 2;2 years old in order to find
out whether substitution is grounded in perceptual errors. They carried out a study using three
types of target pairs. Each pair consists of a real object and a nonsense object. Type 1 pairs
involved the perception of minimal pair contrasts that are actualized in speech errors. In other
words, type 1 pairs are in a substitution relationship ([IÖ] versus [Ö]: [] is realised as [f]. Type

2 word pairs involve minimal pair contrasts that are not actualized in speech errors ([SÚÖJ] versus

[WÚÖJ]). These types are normally not in a substitution relationship. All pairs in Type 1 and Type 2
differ from each other in one feature only, while Type 3 targets differ in more than one feature
([NÚDZ] versus [pDZ]). Type 3 functions as a control group, to be sure that the task was
understood by children. Each pair was presented eight times, in which the real object was
targeted four times and the nonsense object was targeted four times. The criterion for correct
perception is that the child must have a correct score in at least six out of eight trials.40 Eilers and
Oller predicted that children would make fewer perceptual errors in Type 2 and Type 3 word
pairs than in Type 1 word pairs, because the contrast in Type 2 and Type 3 pairs is easier to
produce. The contrast in Type 1 is difficult to produce and that might be caused by the fact that
the contrast is difficult to perceive. In other words, the difficulty in making contrasts in Type 1
words is expected by the authors to be caused by an error in perceptual contrast. Their hypothesis
was partially confirmed. Although the pairs that turned out to be the easiest to discriminate were
Type 3, Type I word pairs were distributed between easy to discriminate and not discriminated at
all. Thus, within the group of some Type 1 word pairs, some were easy, and others were difficult


is met. The scores per child vary from none of the contrasts acquired to all contrasts acquired.
40
   Before the main test, the children did a pre-test with non-minimal pairs (horse versus dog). In this pre-test they
had to meet the criterion of four in a row correct.
                                                                                                                       148


to discriminate. Some Type 2 pairs were more difficult to discriminate than some Type 1 pairs,
which is contrary to the hypothesis because Type 2 pairs (without a substitution relationship)
were predicted to be perceptually easier to discriminate than Type 1 pairs (with a substitution
relationship). Both Velleman and Eilers and Oller conclude that errors in production are
sometimes, but not always, due to perceptual difficulties: while in some cases differences
between two words are perceived but not pronounced, in other cases, no difference is perceived,
and the words are not distinguished articulatorily either.
         This thesis discusses variation in the reduction patterns of consonant clusters. In the
remainder of this chapter, I therefore focus on the issue of the child’s ability to perceive minimal
pairs with an initial consonant cluster versus singleton distinction, such as in the words bread
and bed. Until recently, the existence of reduced clusters in a child’s language has been assumed
to be a result of an active phonological rule or a phonotactic constraint, without questioning the
assumption that children indeed have adult inputs as their representations with consonant clusters
in words like bread (e.g. Fikkert 1994, Gnanadesikan 1995, Goad and Rose to appear, Ohala
1996; see however Menyuk 1972).41 The assumption that children have adult-like representations
was more or less directly adopted from earlier work in child phonology by scholars such as
Jakobson (1941/1968), Stampe (1969), and Smith (1973) without further experimental research.
Studies that encompass both production and perception of consonant clusters are very rare.
Except for the study of Eilers and Oller (1976), where two targets have a cluster versus singleton
contrast, there is, as far as I am aware, no study that investigates the relationship between
production errors and perception errors, focusing on consonant clusters.42


The observation that most children have at least a few instances of either correct realisation
(process 4'') or epenthesis (process 6) in the production data (see chapter 4) suggests that
underlying representations are indeed accurate. The next section (6.2) presents a pilot study that
attempts to test the child’s ability to discriminate between minimal pairs with clusters and
singletons. This study was done together with the main study of the thesis so that perception

of 8): plok-pok: 5.82 vs. plok-lok: 6.09. This result does not confirm their hypothesis that production errors are
grounded in perceptual errors.
                                                                                                  149


results can be compared directly with production results. The main questions in the perception
study are stated in (6.3):


(6.3)   1)      How well do individual children perform in a cluster discrimination task?
                i) when they have non-adult outputs?
                ii) when they have adult outputs?
        2)      How well are target clusters discriminated from target singletons?


In the next sections, I will present the method (6.2.1) and results (6.2.2) of the pilot study.


6.2     Pilot study on perception
6.2.1   Method
Nineteen children from the main production study also participated in the perception study. The
children who participated in both studies were chosen for practical reasons: they were willing to
spend more time with me, beyond the regular production sessions, and they were children in
daycares that could provide additional time slots in a separate room.
        For each child, the perception test was done three times with three weeks between testing.
It is important to note that the session numbers in the perception task are not the same session
numbers used in the production task. For most children the perception test started only after four
or five production sessions. The ages of the children who performed in the perception task were
between 2;1-3;1. The age range is one year, with a mean age of 2;6 years. There were 10 girls
and 9 boys involved, each sex with roughly the same spread in age. Table 6.1 gives an overview
of the subjects involved in the perception task. Subjects are ordered from the youngest to oldest
at the time they participated in the perception task. Notice that the same child numbers are used
in the production and perception tasks. Age 1 relates to the age of the child during the first
perception session.

The target word pairs were of two sorts: Nine word pairs have a cluster vs. singleton contrast
(6.2a); three word pairs have an obstruent-lateral vs. obstruent-rhotic contrast (6.2b). Except for
one word pair (pair 9) all eight word pairs with a cluster vs. singleton contrast have a singleton
that is the less sonorous of the consonants in the cluster. The target words (and thus the clusters
and singleton counterparts) were chosen for picturability. The target word pairs are given in
Table 6.2.

Table 6.2         Target words in perception test
A          Nr.    CC-C               A clusters                              B singletons
           1      [br] - [b]         [br]oek            pants                [b]oek          book
           2      [bl] - [b]         [bl]okken          blocks               [b]okken        billy goat

           11     [bl] – [br]        [bl]oot            naked                [br]ood         bread
           12     [xl] – [xr]        [xl]as             glass                [xr]as          grass



The task was a picture pointing task. The child sees two pictures on one page (in random order
and in random position) and is asked to point to the picture that is named by the investigator. Just
as with the production study, in the perception study I had the child on my lap or I was sitting
next to the child, so that it is almost impossible that the child would see any possible eye
movement on my part that would lead them towards the right picture. The protocol is as follows:


     1            activation of both pictures: “This is [br]oek and this is [b]oek”
     2            question towards the child: “Where is [br]oek?”
     3            waiting for reaction (reaction is pointing at a picture)
     (4)          repetition of the question
     (5)          repetition plus pointing at pictures (“Is this [br]oek or is this [br]oek?”)



Stage 4 and 5 are optional and only carried out if the child does not respond in the earlier stages.
           The entire perception test was done three times for each child.43 Since one test involves
pointing at 24 pictures, I decided to split up each test into two sessions. Thus, in one perception

session 12 targets of the twelve word-pairs were asked for in random order.44 Usually first one
perception session was done, then we took a break, and then we did one production session. The
other perception and production session was done within the same day or within the same week.


6.2.2    Results

The number of correct scores per child per target is given in Appendix 6.1. The correct scores in

three other ways. In column 5, overall correct scores are counted where correct means that the
child must have pointed correctly at a target word in two out of three sessions. In column 6
correct scores are counted with a stricter criterion: the child must point correctly at a target word
in all three sessions. Column 7 looks at scores per word pair. Percentages indicate correct
pointing at a word-pair at least 5 out of six times. I will refer to a target of two sessions out of
three correct as 2/3, of three sessions out of three correct as 3/3, of five words in a word pair
from three sessions out of six correct as 5/6.

No matter which criterion is used to evaluate performance in production, the most important
conclusion to draw from the mean scores, given in bold, is that scores in perception are not
perfect. The mean score out of the over all mean scores per child indicates the percentage of data
that is pointed at correctly, which is 76% (column 3). However, this number only gives us a basic
idea of overall performance. Notice that a score close to 50% indicates chance pointing. The
three rightmost columns where performance is determined by different criteria indicate the
percentage of targets that are perceived correctly by each child. Considering the two stricter
criteria, three sessions out of three correct and five words out of six correct, we see that results

are far from perfect (respectively 50% and 58%). This means that overall, children only
perceived half of the targets correctly. With a less strict criterion, two out of three sessions, we
see that scores are higher overall (82%), but still almost 20% of the targets are not perceived
correctly.

has high scores, whereas the target word /dr/uif grape has very low scores. An important



46

question at this point is why scores differ between lexical items. I will discuss this in more detail

        All scores are highly correlated. The correlation between mean overall score and two
sessions out of three correct is: (R(24) = .896, p=.000). The correlation between mean overall
and three sessions out of three correct is significant (R(24) = .930, p=.000). The correlation
between two sessions out of three correct and three sessions out of three correct is significant
(R(24) = .725, p=.000).


One crucial assumption in this study is that it is possible to gain insight into the nature of the
child's underlying representations by looking at scores in perception tasks. Brown (1997)
assumes that the type of mistakes made by the child reveal what exactly is measured. Brown
claims that the types of errors found in her database indicated that the child had errors in the
phonological system rather than that the child made perceptual errors. She distinguishes
perception errors from errors in the phonological system as follows. If the number of errors in a
word pair is equally distributed (i.e., a two way mistake), the child does not yet have a contrast in
underlying representation:


This sort of random choice is what we would expect if the phonological representations are
impoverished: the child hears a cue and must choose between two items that are not
distinguished in her lexicon. The child's choice then should vary between the two items and may
be influenced by some non-grammatical factors, such as recency or frequency effects. (Brown
1997, p. 113).


If errors were more frequently made in one target rather than the other target in the word pair
(e.g. a one way mistake), this would indicate that the child's errors are in perception rather than
in the phonological representation:


In contrast if the deficiency were perceptual and two acoustic signals were mapped to the same
category (making them indistinguishable), the errors should occur in only one direction. Both
peas and keys would be perceived as peas or keys, hence the same item should be chosen,
regardless of which cue is given (Brown 1997, p. 113).
                                                                                                  157


Table 6.7 compares the child’s scores across CC targets (cluster targets) with his/her scores
across C targets (singleton targets) in my study.


Table 6.7        Over all mean correct score in CC and C targets per child (in %)
Child       CC       C         Child      CC        C
1           73       96        21         87        67
3           48       59        22         82        89
2           92       81        20         89        96

An interesting fact seen from Table 6.7 is that there is no difference in scores of children over all
between words with clusters and words with singletons. In other words, correct pointing at a
picture does not occur more often with singleton targets or with cluster targets. Children 3, 5, 6,
10, 14, 26, 31 especially have overall equally bad scores. This type of score (i.e., a two-way
mistake, in which the number of errors in a word pair is equally distributed, see above) might
thus suggest that these children do not yet have an adult phonological system, as Brown
suggested.


The following conclusions can be made so far: i) overall, perception is not perfect, ii) perception
scores differ greatly between children, regardless of their age, iii) perception scores differ greatly
between lexical items, iv) overall mean correct scores in perception of singletons versus clusters
do not differ.


6.2.2.2 Results: production versus perception
One of the main goals in this pilot study is to investigate how well clusters are perceived by
children who neutralize these clusters in production compared to children who already produce
clusters. In this subsection, I compare the child’s perception results with his/her production
                                                                                                                    158


results in terms of correct scores in cluster words. In general, the same target words with clusters
that were used in the perception test were also used in the production test.47 I follow the
definition for correct realisation in production, labeled as process 4’’ in chapter 4 (see section
4.1.3). This definition is repeated in (6.4).


(6.4)    Correct realisation (process 4’’)
         Realising both targets or realising a cluster with one or two consonants, with the same sonority value as the
         substitution of the singleton.



Recall that this definition of correct realisation excludes in a better way mistakes made because
of motor control problems (outside the grammar) and distinguishes between phonologically,
rather than phonetically, difficult clusters (see section 4.1.3).
         In Table 6.8, correct scores for over all target clusters are given per child for perception
and production. Notice that I focus now only on the target words with clusters given in Table 6.2
(1-9a and 10-12a and b). Scores of targets with singletons are omitted. Therefore, overall scores
for perception in Table 6.8 are sometimes different from those seen earlier. The number of
targets differs from child to child because some children missed some items in the first
perception test (see footnote 43). Overall production scores are counted considering the same
clusters, regardless of the word. For example, child 1 went through 45 targets with an initial
cluster in the perception test. 73% of these items were correctly pointed at by her. In the
production test, she realised 125 words that begin with the same clusters as the target clusters in
the perception test (/br/, /bl/, /dr/, /kl/, /kn/, /sp/, /st/, /sn/, /fl/, /fr/, /xl/, /xr/). Of these words, 62%
were correctly produced.

For example, child 1 did 45 trials in the perception test and her overall correct score is 73%. For
the production test, there were 125 trials, which is the total of all realisations of the targets
relevant to the targets in the perception test. Her overall score for production is 62%. What we
can see from Table 6.8 is that overall, production of clusters has a lower score than perception:
76% for perception versus 49% for production. This is in fact the case for all children except
child 27 and child 26, who have higher production scores than perception scores. I will discuss
this surprising result later in more detail (see section 6.2.3). Furthermore, we see that higher
scores in perception do not indicate higher scores in production. For example, child 34 pointed
correctly at 95% of the targets with clusters, although she could only produce 9% of the words
with clusters correctly. This result is expected if perception precedes production. For such cases,
a likely explanation is in articulation (outside or inside the grammar). There is no correlation
between scores in perception and scores in production: (R(19) = -.169, p=.490).

As seen from Table 6.9, words that have a high score in perception do not have a high score in
production, and vice versa. For example, the word /st/ok branch has a high score in perception
(95%), though a very low score in production (62%). There is no correlation between target
scores in perception and target scores in production: (R(15) = .275, p=.321).


In (6.5), four scenarios (A-D) are summarized regarding the interaction between perception and
production in the child’s language development at a certain point in time.


(6.5)   Scenario                Perception        Production       Hypothesis
        A                       correct           correct          adult stage
        B                       correct           wrong            initial stage
        C                       wrong             wrong            not expected in Pruning hypothesis;
                                                                   expected in Building hypothesis
        D                       wrong             correct          not expected
                                                                                                                     161




As seen in (6.5), scenarios A and B are expected to occur in the adult and in the initial stage in
the acquisition of clusters, respectively. In scenario A, the child has reached the adult stage of
his/her language: the child can both produce and perceive words with clusters. In scenario B, the
child can correctly perceive words with clusters, although it cannot produce these clusters yet.
Scenarios C and D are not expected to occur following the assumption that the child has adult
underlying representations, i.e. the Pruning hypothesis. In scenario C the child can neither
produce nor perceive cluster words. This is not expected to be the case for a two year old if we
follow the assumption that perception is fully acquired at the moment the child begins to speak.
However, this scenario would be expected if we follow the assumption of the Building
hypothesis, that perception develops gradually. Finally, scenario D is not expected under either
hypothesis. However, some studies report cases where children produce a word without being
able to perceive it correctly (e.g. Barton 1976, Eilers and Oller 1976). This would simply be the
case where the child accidentally produced a correct form even though s/he did not distinguish
between certain clusters.
         In order to investigate the distribution of scenarios, criteria based on individual
performance should be set. I consider the following two criteria for perception: i) the child must
have pointed correctly at a cluster target word in two out of three sessions, ii) the child must have
pointed correctly at a cluster target word in three out of three sessions. Notice that in each
perception test there are 15 target words with clusters (targets 1-9a, and targets 10-12a and b).48
For production, I look at correct realisation of clusters rather than words. In this way there are
more data for each target cluster. In order to look at synchronic performance in perception and
production, only production data from the sessions when perception was also tested are
counted.49 For example, child 1 did the perception test in sessions 4-6. Scores of correct
realisation are counted only for these sessions. The scores in production per cluster per child in
percentages and the number of cluster targets per cluster per child are given in Appendix 6.2.


48
   Recall that some scores for some children might be misleading because of the fact that these children missed items
in the first perception test. There are seven cases where a child has one missing target, one correct answer and one
wrong answer. Although for these children we could add their wrong answers up to two (out of three) in a
hypothetical testing session, they are counted as if they do meet the 2/3 criterion. There are eleven cases where a
child has two correct answers. These data are counted as correct for the 3/3 criterion.
49
   Child 13 and child 26 did the third perception at a later date than when the last production test was carried out. For
these children I count only the results in the other two sessions where production was tested.
                                                                                                     162


        In order to determine performance in production, I define the following criterion: at least
90% should be realised correctly. The distribution of scenarios A-D per child per target is given
in Appendix 6.3 A and 6.3 B, respectively, using the criteria 2/3 and 3/3 for perception.
        Notice that the structure of data for perception and the structure of data for production are
very different. There are many more trials in the production study (preferably 24 observations for
each cluster) and it is unlikely that a certain kind of output might happen by chance. However, in
the perception test each outcome has an a priori 50/50 chance of being right. We thus have to be
cautious here.
        In Table 6.10, the percentages in the different scenarios for the children as a group are
given for both the criteria 2/3 correct and 3/3 correct in perception.


Table 6.10       Percentage of data in scenarios A-D across children and across targets
                 Two out of three                            Three out of three
                 N                  %                        N               %
A                42                 15                       24              8
B                194                68                       115             40
C                40                 14                       119             42
D                9                  3                        27              9



According to the criterion 2/3, most data are cases where perception is correct and production is
incorrect (scenario B). For example, child 34 realises all clusters as singletons, but she has high
perception scores. The most interesting example from this child comes in session three, in target
pair 2 /bl/okken - /b/okken blocks- billy-goats, where she refers four times to /bl/okken and three
times to /b/okken, without making the distinction in production (e.g. realising both targets as
[b]). In (6.6), her phrases are given with the gloss and the target that she is referring to (in bold).


(6.6)   dat zijn [b]okken                 those are blocks                   /bl/
        niet [b]okken                     not billy goats                    /b/
        wel [b]okken                      indeed blocks                      /bl/
        niet [b]okken                     not billy goats                    /b/
        [b]okken                          blocks                             /bl/
        Daar zit wel [b]okken in          In there, there are billy goats    /b/
        [b]okken                          blocks                             /bl/
                                                                                                                          163


To the adult listener, all her realisations sound the same.50 However, the child can perceive the
contrast. Although there are many examples in scenario B, notice from Table 6.10 that more than
10% of the data fell under scenario C, where a cluster is wrongly perceived and wrongly
produced. If we use the three out of three criterion however, the distribution of scenarios differs
greatly. There are fewer cases where a word is perceived correctly but produced incorrectly and
more cases where perception and production are both incorrect. Although the results look
different according to what criterion is chosen, an important conclusion to draw is that at least
17% of the data occurs in the scenarios that are not expected to occur under the Pruning
hypothesis.
         The distribution of scenarios per child per criterion in percentages is given in Table 6.11.

As can be seen in Table 6.11, the distribution of data is quite different depending on the criterion

scenarios that they follow. Some children typically perceive a lot of words that they cannot
produce yet: (children 2, 8, 10, 21, 22, 33), and some are able to produce the words as well (thus
most data in scenario A and B, for children such as 1, 16, 13, 20, 28). Some of the data from
some children seem to be problematic. Child 3 has many instances that are produced and
perceived incorrectly (scenario 3). The same is true for children 5, 6, 14, 31. These results are
unexpected according to the Pruning hypothesis, but fine with respect to the Building hypothesis.
Also some children have data that are correctly produced but wrongly perceived in both criteria
(children 1, 10, 14, 13, 27, 26, 20, 27, 26). These data fall under the hypothesis D.

As is seen from this Table, the distribution of scenarios differs between CC target words. For
example, target word /st/ok branch has no data in scenario D under either of the criteria, whereas
target word /kl/ok clock has data in scenario D.


The following conclusion can be made in this section: i) across children, scores in the production
of clusters are lower than scores in perception, expected under both hypotheses, ii) there is no
correlation between child scores in perception and child scores in production, iii) there is no
correlation between target scores in perception and target scores in production, iv) most data fall
under scenario B, i.e. where perception is correct and production is incorrect, however there are
data in scenarios A, C, and D as well.
                                                                                                 166


Although almost all children have higher perception scores than production scores, the
perception scores of some children are low, indicating perhaps that the child has difficulties in
perception. The question at this point is why children have these difficulties. Is it because they
cannot perceive the contrast that is tested for, or do other (unwanted) factors play a role, such as
distraction, fatigue, etc? Probably one reason why there has not been much work done on the
perception of clusters is because of the difficulty of testing the child’s capacities for perception.
A major concern in phonemic discrimination tasks involves the level of complexity. There are
many factors involved in these tasks. The child can only choose between two pictures, when s/he
i) can hear the contrast, and ii) has the contrast in his/her lexicon. In addition, the child has to
know the words (meaning) and to recognize the words during the testing (memory), assuming
that at the time of testing s/he is interested enough so that performance is according to his
capacity (attention). In other words, there are numerous other factors that might play a role in
testing perception and thus might have an influence on the results. Recall furthermore that we
have to be cautious because of the limited number of trials in the perception test versus the many
trials in the production test.
        In the following discussion, I look at some factors that might play a role.


6.2.3   Discussion
6.2.3.1 Word familiarity
A major concern in perception tests is whether the child knows the word under consideration
well enough to recognize it during testing. Barton (1980) discusses issues that are involved in
testing phonemic perception. One of these issues is word familiarity. How well a word is known
by the child might have an effect on his/her perception scores (Barton 1976, Vihman 1996).
Barton (1980) argues that the child may choose negatively if s/he hears a word that s/he does not
know. In this case the child chooses the unknown picture when s/he hears an unknown word and
thus connects the unknown word with the unknown stimulus. For his doctoral dissertation,
Barton (1976) followed 20 children between 2;3 and 2;11 and tested knowledge and perception
of 20 word pairs. He found a difference in performance between known words and taught words.
Known words had higher scores than taught words.
        Although the children in my study were tested several times in their production of the
majority of the cluster targets used in the perception test, knowledge of words starting with a
                                                                                                        167


singleton was not tested. We thus do not have a clear idea to what extent mistakes are made
because of differences in familiarity of words. An interesting point to look at is the correlation
between scores in perception and frequency of target word use by children, found in longitudinal
studies of natural language. I use The Groningen Corpus from Childes, compiled by Bol,
Krikhaar and Wijnen. In Table 6.13, I give the frequency scores of seven acquirers of Dutch as
their first language with ages between 1;05 and 3;07 (frequency scores of child and child
directed speech per child file are given in respectively Appendix 6.4A and 6.4B). The first
column indicates the frequency of a target word (out of the total of target words) used by seven
children (average percent). The second column indicates the frequency of a target word (out of
the total of target words) used by all speakers that communicated with the seven children during
the recordings (e.g. child directed speech).


Table 6.13           Frequency of target words in child speech and in child directed speech (Childes)

(N=1637)                   (N=4869)                (N=1637)                  (N=4869)
word         %             word        %           word         %            word       %
/dr/uif       0.06          /b/okken    0.03        /sp/in      2.48         /sp/in      2.26
/p/in         0.19          /p/in       0.03        /kl/ok      2.67         /k/ok       2.69

There is a high correlation between frequency of words used by children and the frequency of
words used in child directed speech (R(24) = .919, p=.000).
           The interesting issue to examine at this point is whether there is a correlation between
these frequency numbers and the scores of the target words in the perception test. If high scores
are in words that are of frequent use, then there might be an effect of word familiarity. However,
                                                                                                  168


there is no correlation between scores and frequency in my data set. The correlation between
mean score and frequency in child speech is not significant (R(24) = .234, p=.272). The
correlation between 2/3 and frequency in child speech is not significant (R(24) = .326, p=.120).
The correlation between 3/3 and frequency in child speech is not significant (R(24) = .215,
p=.313). The correlation between mean score and frequency in child directed speech is not
significant (R(24) = .142, p=.508). The correlation between 2/3 and frequency in child directed
speech is not significant: (R(24) = .248, p=.243). The correlation between 3/3 and frequency in
child directed speech is not significant (R(24) = .125, p=.559). Thus, there is no frequency effect
of word use in my corpus.


6.2.3.2 Cluster frequency
Another issue that needs attention is the possible difference in scores between words as whole
units or clusters. Is it the case that a perceived cluster in a word is perceived in another word with
the same cluster or is perception based more on whole units? Dinnsen (1996) claims that a word
that is well perceived does not indicate that another word with the same cluster is also well
perceived. In this pilot study, there was only one case in which the same CC-cluster occurs in
different target words in a word pair with a singleton (in /br/ood bread and /br/oek pants). The
mean scores per child for these targets are given again in Table 6.14.

As can be seen in Table 6.14, although the overall score is the same, there are individual
differences in scores between the target words. However, the number of targets with similar
clusters and the number of sessions are insufficient to see whether scores are more similar in
words with identical clusters.


An interesting aspect related to this is whether there is a correlation between scores and
frequency of initial clusters. In order to count cluster frequency in child directed speech, the
