6.2.3.3 Cues from production
A closer look at the production data might lead us to a better interpretation of the perception
scores of a child. Referring back to scenarios A-D might help us uncover shortcomings of the
perception test. High production scores for example, compared to low perception scores
(scenario D) are suspicious. Recall that it is expected that perception preceedes production. One
might argue that correct production is accidental (random). However, it is not likely that the
higher results in production compared to perception are an accident given the strict criterion on
production, i.e. at least 90% should be realised correctly in production. It is more likely that in
these cases the incorrect perception does not tell us anything about possible absent underlying
contrasts, thus that there is a problem in perception, rather than in the grammar. The child most
likely has an underlying contrast, and thus these scores in perception are misleading, and
probably due to other factors. In a reliable and valid perception test, scenario D should not occur.
In this pilot study, two children had higher production scores than perception scores (see also
Barton 1976, Eilers and Oller 1976 for similar results). Child 27 has a very low mean score in
perception (67%) versus a relatively high score in production 84%. If we look more closely at his
data we see that there are examples in which he makes a contrast in the production of a target
pair, while he points at the wrong target. This suggests that the child must have the contrast in his
grammar but makes an error in the perception task. In session three, he realises [xr]as and [xl]as,
but points incorrectly at /xl/as, when /xr/as is the target. In the same session however, he realises
the contrast between [fl]uit and [fr]uit, but points at the wrong target, saying: “both are [fl]uit!”.
The finding that perception scores are lower than production scores is an awkward result, since
we assume that it is impossible for the child to produce a contrast that it can not perceive. Since
these children accurately produce clusters, the scores on perception of these clusters might not
give us the entire information on the child’s competence.51 It is likely the case that their scores in
perception are influenced by external factors.



51
   Epenthesis is another example, besides correct realization of clusters, in which production data can check whether
scores in perception are really due to a lack of contrast in the underlying representations or to something else. The
insertion of a vowel between two consonants by the child implies that the child has two consonants in the underlying
structure. For example, child 10 realized /dr/ eleven times in the production study during the time that perception
was also tested. She realized this cluster five times correctly ([dr]), five times with a schwa between the consonants
([G?U]) and one time as a singleton ([d]) only. However her perception scores for this cluster are low. She correctly
points at target /dr/uif grape one time. It is highly likely that she has /dr/ underlyingly despite her scores for
perception.
                                                                                                     171


        Cases where production and perception scores are low (scenario C) might lead to the
conclusion that the child has not acquired clusters yet in his/her underlying structure (problem in
grammar). As is seen in Table 6.11 and in Table 6.12, the percentage of data in scenario C differs
because of the use of different criteria (i.e., 3/3, 2/3). This shows that criteria should be chosen
very carefully and at least be based on a sufficient number of sessions (see below). Child 3
reduces all words with clusters to singletons (production score in correct realisation is 4%). He
also has a low perception score, with close to chance pointing (53%). It seems that child 3 does
not have adult outputs as inputs yet. However, we should be careful here. It could be that other
factors play a role.
        Recall that the structure of the study for perception and the structure of the study for
production are different. The production study has many more trials, while the perception study
only has three trials per target. This limited number of trials per target is a major shortcoming in
the pilot study. Although tentative, the results of this pilot study suggest that we cannot be
certain that children always have clusters underlyingly. Keeping in mind the pilot nature of the
study, these results thus support the Building hypothesis.
        The distribution of scenarios for each child across targets only presents a general
impression. It could be that a child does not have a contrast yet in one word (or cluster), while
the contrast occurs in other clusters. For example, child 1 has overall high perception scores
(mean overall 82%). She has problems with pointing correctly at two cluster targets: /sp/in spider
and /dr/uif grape. Despite her low perception scores in /dr/uif, it is not likely that this is because
of a lack in contrast since she is able to make the contrast in production. During the production
sessions, she 100% correctly realises cluster [dr] out of eight times. Thus, presumably other
influences play a role. A possibility of a lack in contrast is higher in /sp/ (though still not
absolute). In the first session she points correctly at /sp/in, but reduces this target cluster to [p] in
production. In session two, she obviously is learning to realise this cluster, she pauses between
the two consonants (indicated with a slash: [s/p]in). She does not point correctly to the word
during the perception test. In session three, she still does not correctly point to the target, but she
produces the cluster correctly. It is difficult to say whether she really has a contrast in underlying
form. It might be the case that she was in the process of acquiring this contrast. More testing
sessions during that period might have led us closer to an answer. Another example where it
seems that a contrast has not been acquired yet is in child 16. She realises both /bl/oot naked and
                                                                                                 172


/br/ood bread as [bl]oot and in perception she explicitly says: “and that is also [bl]oot”, pointing
at the other picture that is not targeted. Another example of using the word “also” is child 14. In
one session he repeatedly says “this is also…”, regardless of whether it involves a singleton or
cluster:


•   investigator:        this is /sp/in
    child imitates:      [sp]in
    investigator:        this is pin
    child:               also a [sp]in!
    investigator:        where is /p/in? (child gives wrong answer)


•   investigator:        these are /b/okken
    investigator:        these are…
    child interrupts:    also [b]okken, these also [bl]okken
    investigator:        where are /b/okken? (child gives wrong answer)


•   investigator:        this is /xr/as
    investigator:        this is /xl/as
    child:               this also [fr]at
    investigator:        where is /xr/as? (child gives right answer)



Presumably, the child knows that the words sound more or less the same. The question is
whether correct answers always indicate that there is an existing contrast, or not. Three sessions
are not sufficient to test this.


In this section, I have discussed two possible factors that might play a role in the perception
results, namely word familiarity and cluster frequency. The results in this study showed no
significant influence of the factor of word familiarity. There is a moderate correlation between
scores in perception and cluster frequency, within the criterion three out of three correct. The fact
that there are data in scenario D is suspicious and reveal that other factors might play a role.
Although the words in scenario D are perceived incorrectly in the perception task, this error
cannot be based as an error in the grammar (i.e., a lack of contrast) since the child is able to
realise the cluster in this word correctly in production. Cases in scenario C support the Building
                                                                                                   173


hypothesis. In the next section, I will summarize the findings and present directions for refining
the design.


6.3    Conclusions and directions for further research
Given the methodological problems that appear to be inherent in studying perception, this pilot
study has shown that while perception has a tendency to lead production, in fact perception does
not appear to be perfect, even with known lexical items. The pilot study shows the following
results: i) over all, perception is not perfect, ii) perception scores differ greatly between children,
regardless of their age, iii) perception scores differ greatly between lexical items, iv) overall
mean correct scores in perception of singletons versus clusters do not differ, v) across children,
scores in production of clusters are lower than scores in perception of clusters, vi) there is no
correlation between child scores in perception and child scores in production, vii) there is no
correlation between target scores in perception and target scores in production, viii) most data
fall under scenario B where perception is correct and production is incorrect, however there are
data in scenarios A: where both perception and production are correct; C where production is
wrong and perception is wrong; D where production is right and perception is wrong.
       The finding that sometimes perception is wrong and production is right (scenario D) is an
awkward result, since we assume that it is impossible for a child to produce a contrast that it
cannot perceive. External factors must play a role. Two possible external factors were
investigated in the study, namely word familiarity and cluster frequency. For neither one was
there a significant effect, except for scores in perception using the 2/3 criterion and cluster
frequency (moderate effect).
       The finding from this pilot study that perception is not always perfect supports the
Building hypothesis which states that the ability to distinguish phonemic contrasts improves
gradually over the course of acquisition. Therefore, the main conclusion that follows from this
study is that it is not right to assume that all children always have clusters in their underlying
representation when they begin to speak. This assumption has been made by linguists without
testing for it. Unless there is evidence from production data that the child has clusters, in cases
where the child does not produce clusters yet, we do not know whether the child really has
underlying clusters.
                                                                                                                 174


In order to investigate more deeply whether low scores in perception are due to poor perception
or to other unwanted influences that might have played a role in testing, further studies are
needed. Future research must have a refined design which limits in every possible way the
influence of external factors. A major shortcoming of this pilot study for example is the number
of sessions. There were only three sessions in this pilot study. At least ten sessions would be
desirable. In order to get random guessing under 5%, the child then has to correctly point in 8/10
sessions (Brown 1997). Furthermore, more targets with different structures need to be added. In
this way, variation between cluster types is targeted. Third, different ways of testing are needed
in order to better control cognitive factors (attention, memory, word knowledge). Another
interesting aspect for future research is to investigate to what extent changes over time are a
result of interaction between perception and production.52 The pilot study followed children for
about six weeks, which is not long enough to see major differences with respect to development
between perception and production. Another aspect to focus on is the input speech during the
perception experiment. In order to avoid differences in the speech of the investigator, one might
consider using a toy with synthetic speech. In this way constant speech across children and
repetitions is controlled for.
         This pilot study represents a first step in research where the nature of underlying
representations in child phonology will have a primary focus, and thus where perception and
production data from the same child are collected within the same project. The next chapter
presents the conclusion of the thesis.




52
  Jusczyk (1997) points out that changes in production may have little to do with perception and vice versa, due to
other influences. Changes in perception could relate to attention and memory, while changes in production could
relate to motor control.
                                                                                                175



Chapter 7 Conclusion


7.1    Introduction
In this chapter, I present a summary of the thesis as well as a discussion of questions that arise
from this thesis and need to be investigated in the future.


7.2    Summary of the thesis
In chapter 1, I discussed the main questions that the thesis is concerned with. The thesis focuses
on variation in reduction strategies in word-initial consonant clusters by Dutch children who are
between two and three years old. The thesis is concerned with empirical as well as theoretical
questions. The empirical questions mainly involve getting insights into patterns of variation in
reduction strategies. First, what are the patterns in between-individual variation and within-
individual variation in reduction strategies in clusters, in which C1C2 is realised as C1 or C2?
Second, assuming that there is variation, are clusters within a sonority group related i) in that
they are all variable (or non-variable) and ii) in that they undergo the same type of reduction
strategy by the same child? Third, what are the patterns in variation when different definitions of
cluster reduction are used? The theoretical questions involve explaining variation. First, to what
extent does sonority by itself explain reduction strategies? Second, if sonority is inadequate,
what is needed to account for between-individual and within-individual variation?
       In chapter 2, I discussed the literature on variation in cluster reduction. Cluster reduction
in child language has been investigated frequently. The data indicate that in most clusters the less
sonorous segment is realised but that variation is seen in /s/-sonorant clusters. However, the trend
in reduction patterns remains unclear. I concluded that studies of cluster reduction lack
quantitative data for both individual and large groups of children, and for many individual
clusters. I analyzed Fikkert’s data (1994) in the light of between-individual and within-individual
variation. Although Fikkert did not sytematically test for clusters in her data collection, the
general trend is that clusters with a plosive are less variable in the way they are reduced than
clusters with a fricative. Also, it seems that children are highly consistent in their reduction
strategy during a certain period of time. However, it is not always clear whether some
realisations are systematic or accidental, since the data per child per cluster are limited. I then
discussed how the issue of variation in reduction strategies is explained in the literature. In
                                                                                                             176


general the process of cluster reduction is explained by making reference to the feature
[sonorant], but there are obvious inadequacies in explaining variation. I compared two accounts
of variation, both within the framework of Optimality Theory. The first is an account by Pater
and Barlow (2002) in which different rankings of the constraint *FRICATIVE play a role. The
second is an account by Goad and Rose (to appear) in which different prosodic representations
play a role. Both accounts make predictions about patterns which occur and do not occur, which
are tested by the data in this thesis.
        In chapter 3, I discussed the methodology of the main study. The main contribution of the
thesis is that variation is studied in various ways. First, variation between clusters: 23 different
clusters each with three tokens are the targets. Second, variation between children: The number
of subjects in the main study is 34, and third, variation within children: Most children went
through eight sessions within a time span of about half a year. In each session every cluster is
tested three times.
        Chapter 4 presented the results of the study focusing on patterns in which either the first
or second consonant is realised. The results are presented in a step-by-step fashion showing that
the various factors involved are not, overall, significant. The eventual analysis is based on the
reduction data of 45 children (including the pilot study). This involves over 9,000 instances of C1
or C2 realisation. A child’s reduction pattern was presented per individual cluster using the 8-
criterion, which states that the reduction strategy of only those children with at least eight C1C2
reductions for a particular cluster was analyzed. This was done in order to analyze consistent
patterns. A consistent reduction pattern was defined as reducing at least 91% of the reduction
data for a particular cluster in one direction (either C1 or C2). There were 407 reduction cases that
met the 8-criterion (each case has at least eight observations of C1 or C2 reduction). The main
conclusions involve three domains. First, regarding variation between and within clusters,
clusters vary in the way they are typically realised in reduction: some are between-child stable,
and thus realised in one direction (/sp/, /sk/, /pl/, /pr/, /bl/, /br/, /tr/, /dr/, /kl/, /kr/, /fr/, /xr/, /tw/,
/sw/) while others are between-child variable, and thus realised in both directions (/st/, /sx/, /sm/,
/kn/, /sn/, /sl/, /fl/, /xl/). Between-child stable clusters are not necessarily within-child stable
clusters and vice versa. Second is a conclusion regarding variation between and within children.
There is variation between children in how clusters are reduced (see above), but the individual
child is in general very consistent in his/her reduction strategy. The third conclusion involves
                                                                                                  177


patterns in reduction strategies. There are implicational relationships between some clusters.
First, realising C2 in /sl/ implies realising C2 in /sn/ implies realising C2 in /sm/, but not vice
versa. Second, realising C2 in /fl/ and /xl/ implies realising C2 in /sl/ (and thus in /sn/ and /sm)
but not vice versa. Third, realising C2 in /kn/ implies realising C2 in /sn/ implies realising C2 in
/sm/, but not vice versa.
       In chapter 5, I discussed the theoretical implications and I discussed the results when
using different definitions of cluster reduction. Regarding the theoretical implications four
conclusions were drawn. First, reduction strategies are systematic since within-child variation is
very limited. Second, classes of sonorant versus non-sonorant alone cannot account for between-
child variation, and discussing reduction patterns of clusters within the same sonority group is
not always legitimate, since clusters of the same sonority group can pattern differently from each
other. I claimed that a much finer tuned notion of sonority is required. Between-child variation
and implicational relationships between clusters are a result of a difference in selecting the head.
This difference is caused by the assumption that the child is starting to make full use of the
structural relationship between the two consonants in the cluster with respect to sonority distance
and place of articulation. This takes place during the head stage. Following Goad and Rose (to
appear) I assume that first there is a sonority stage in which the child selects the head based on
relative sonority. The child notices that most clusters are left-headed (all clusters in which C1 is
the less sonorous) while others are right-headed (all clusters where C2 is the less sonorous, /sp/,
/st/, /sk/). I argue that the child will hypothesize that right-headed clusters are those with similar
structure regarding sonority. This will then lead in the head stage to the extension of the group of
right-headed clusters. Clusters with essentially dissimilar sonorant and place structure between
the two consonants are more likley to be prosodified as head-dependent, since they form a better
onset, while clusters with more similarity between the consonants form a less good onset, and are
thus less likely to be prosodified as head-dependent. As an alternative, some children prosodify
these clusters as appendix-head, akin to the /s/-plosive clusters, and so avoid an onset with
similar structure.
       Regarding the issue of using different definitions of cluster reduction, the results when
using two other definitions of reduction were discussed. The first definition is called Natvoice.
Within this definition, the realised consonant must be identical to one of the input consonants,
but voice specification is not relevant. It was found that results are similar compared to the
                                                                                                   178


results using the definition in which voice specification is relevant. The second definition is
called Natson. Within this definition, the realised consonant must share the same sonority value
as the input consonant. The percentage of within-individual variation is much higher in Natson
compared to that in the strict definition of cluster reduction used in chapter 4. I concluded that in
the analysis of reduction strategies it seems to be right to collapse across voice, but it is not right
to collapse across sonority (sonorants versus obstruents).
       Chapter 6 presented the results of a pilot study that focuses on the interaction between
perception and production of consonant clusters. The pilot study was done with 19 children from
the main study. The main questions of this study are i) how well do individual children perform
in a cluster discrimination task, when they have non-adult outputs or when they have adult
outputs? ii) how well are target clusters discriminated from target singletons? The pilot study
shows that there is no correlation between scores in production and scores in perception and that
perception does not appear to be perfect, even with known lexical items. This finding tentatively
supports the Building hypothesis which states that the ability to distinguish phonemic contrasts
gradually improves over the course of acquisition. The main conclusion that follows from the
pilot study is that it is not right to assume that all children always have clusters in their
underlying representation when they begin to speak. The chapter ends with the conclusion that
perception and production data from the same child need to be collected within the same project
in order to gain insights into the nature of underlying representations in child phonology.
       In the next section I discuss interesting questions that arise from this thesis that need
further investigation.


7.3    Issues for future research
One of the major contributions of this thesis is the finding that between-child variation is very
limited. In chapter 5, I presented an account stating that variation in reduction strategies is a
result of different prosodic representations, due to a difference between clusters with essentially
dissimilar structure versus clusters with similar sonority and place structure. It would be
interesting to see if and how realisation strategies change over time. Within such a study,
children would be followed for a much longer time, starting at a very early age until clusters are
fully acquired. The interval between sessions could be longer than in my study. This would be
                                                                                                  179


legitimate since the results in this thesis show that changes in reduction strategies are limited
within the time period that I studied the children.
         The thesis is concerned with Dutch acquisition. It would be interesting to investigate
patterns in variation at one point in time, and changing patterns over time in languages other than
Dutch.
         Another interesting aspect to investigate in future reasearch is the relationship between
processes 1 and 2 (realising the first or second consonant) and other processes that are involved
in the acquisition of a cluster, such as realising Cother (process 3), CC without being the target
(process 5), correct realisation (process 4), epenthesis (process 6), etc. Recall that the entire
database (including pilot study) involves over 27,000 realisations of word-initial clusters. Only
one third of this database involves processes 1 and 2. It would be interesting to see whether
“head pattern 1 children” and “head pattern 2 children” acquire clusters in the same order (i.e.,
the process of correct realisation). Another example involves the relationship between processes
1 and 2 and process 3. In chapter 5, I discussed the patterns in variation using other definitions of
cluster reduction. I showed that when using a definition based on sonority (i.e., feature sonority
is similar between target and output) more within-variation exists. It would be interesting to
focus more on the status of consonants in group 3 (cases in which neither of the target
consonants is realised), and see whether and how these realisations can be accounted for.
         In chapter 6, I have questioned the assumption made in the literature that toddlers
(between two and three years old) always have clusters in the input. The results of a pilot study
on perception suggest that perception is not always perfect at this age. This result leads to
questions with respect to the nature of the child’s phonological knowledge. Future research must
thus focus more on the mechanism between reshaping the input and reranking constraints.


7.4      Conclusion
One of the major contributions of this thesis involves the methodology. The study examines
reduction strategies in which either the first or the second consonant is realised. The study
reveals patterns in both between- and within-cluster variation, and between- and within-child
variation with an empirical base of over 9,000 C1 and C2 realisations. The thesis has
demonstrated that there is between-child and between-cluster variation in reduction strategies,
but that within-child variation is limited. In addition, the thesis shows that there are implicational
                                                                                                  180


relationships between clusters regarding their pattern in reduction. The results reveal the
shortcomings of the sonority hypothesis and argue that classes of sonority (i.e., sonorant versus
non-sonorant) alone cannot account for reduction strategies in which the more sonorous segment
is realised, and cannot account for different strategies between clusters that belong to the same
sonority group. Instead, variation is accounted for by a finer grained notion of sonority, in which
the relationship between two Cs with respect to sonority distance plays an important role, as well
as the relationship with respect to place structure. Clusters with essentially dissimilar structure
regarding sonority distance, and place are more likely to be prosodified as head (C1) and
dependent (C2) (i.e., as a tauto-syllabic onset), while clusters with a closer similarity with respect
to sonority distance and place are less likely to be prosodified as an onset but rather as hetero-
syllabic, in which C2 is the head and thus C1 is in an appendix position. The fact that both C1
and C2 can be selected as head in clusters with similar structure accounts for the variation in
reduction strategies.

4.3     Overview realisations of target words per child, per session
The columns for singletons indicate: 1) target singleton; 2) realisation; 3) development (change). The number in column 3 refers to
session in which change occurs. “No” means that no development is observed. For example, child 2 substitutes /r/, and the initial
fricatives by [h]. /r/ is always realised as [h] during the recording period. However, from session 4 on, she realisess /s/ correctly; in
session 5, she realises both [x] and [s] for /x/. In session 7, she realises [s] for target /f/ and in session 8: she realises [f] for /x/. The
columns for clusters indicate: 1) target cluster; 2) number of tokens per cluster, column 3 and further: sessions. Ages are given per
session (referring to age in the first recording of a session). Only in cases where the same realisation occurs at least more than once, a
number is added, indicating the number of identical realisations. For example, “x3, k” indicates that child realises [x] three times and
